         The matrix multiplication exponent
                  is nine fourths
                                             OpenAI

                                      November 24, 2026


                                            Abstract
         We prove that the exponent of square matrix multiplication is 9/4 over every field.
      The upper bound was known. For each n ≥ 2, a formal arithmetic circuit multiplying
      two n × n matrices needs at least n9/4 /2 multiplication or division gates. The proof
      shows that a smaller exponent would compress tensor networks enough to violate a lower
      bound for testing whether the entries in each of several independent lists are distinct.


1    The exponent of matrix multiplication
The product of two square matrices of side n has n2 entries. Computing the dot product for
each entry separately takes order n3 arithmetic operations. Strassen’s seven-product identity
showed that sharing work among the entries can save a power of n. How far can those savings
go? The conjecture ω = 2 asks whether, for every ε > 0, the product can be computed in
O(n2+ε ) operations. We show that the arithmetic exponent exceeds the exponent of the
output size by exactly one quarter.

Theorem 1.1. Over every field F , the exponent of square matrix multiplication is ωF = 9/4.

    Here ωF is the infimum of the real numbers s for which two n × n matrices can be
multiplied in OF,s (ns ) scalar operations. We use formal arithmetic circuits. Their inputs are
independent variables, their constants lie in F , and their gates can add, subtract, multiply or
divide by a nonzero rational function. Each output must agree with the corresponding entry
of the product as an identity of rational functions. This convention applies also over finite
fields.
    The 9/4 upper bound was established over C by OpenAI [41] and over every field by
Amanov, van den Berg and Zuiddam [5], following Lü’s extension to arbitrary characteristic
[39]. We prove the matching lower bound. In fact, for every n ≥ 2 a circuit for two n × n
matrices requires at least ⌈n9/4 /2⌉ multiplication or division gates, even if linear operations
are free. The upper bound gives circuits of size OF,ε (n9/4+ε ) for each ε > 0.

The matrix multiplication exponent                                                           2


1.1   Rank at each matrix size
A bilinear algorithm forms products of linear combinations of entries from the first matrix
with linear combinations from the second. It then combines these products linearly to give
all the output entries. The least number of products is the rank of the matrix multiplication
tensor                                   n
                                  Tn =
                                          X
                                                   eij ⊗ ejk ⊗ eki .
                                         i,j,k=1

The third factor pairs the output at position (i, k) with eki . Grouping corresponding factors
gives Tn⊗j ≃ Tnj . Consequently an improvement at one fixed matrix size can be reused at
arbitrarily large sizes.
    Geometric border rank allows the tensor to belong to the Zariski closure, over F , of
tensors computed with a given number of products. Asymptotic rank over F is the limit of
the j-th roots of the ranks over F of the j-th tensor powers.

Corollary 1.2. For every n ≥ 2 and every field F , the geometric border rank of Tn is at
least n9/4 , and the asymptotic rank of Tn over F equals n9/4 . Every formal arithmetic circuit
over F for this product has at least ⌈n9/4 /2⌉ nonscalar multiplication or division gates.

    In particular, an exact bilinear algorithm at side n uses at least ⌈n9/4 ⌉ products. The
factor of two for general circuits comes from extracting the bilinear part of a gate. A
multiplication or division introduces at most two new products of linear forms (Lemma 2.4).
    The square bound also restricts how wide a rectangular product can be while its cost still
has exponent two. Write Ta,b,c for the multiplication tensor of an a × b matrix and a b × c
matrix. Let αF be the supremum of the β ≥ 0 for which multiplying an n × ⌊nβ ⌋ matrix by
a ⌊nβ ⌋ × n matrix has exponent two in n.

Corollary 1.3. For all positive integers a, b, c, the geometric border rank of Ta,b,c and its
asymptotic rank over F are at least

                                  max{ab, bc, ca, (abc)3/4 }.

Over every field, 1/2 ≤ αF ≤ 2/3.

    Three copies of the rectangular tensor, with the roles of the factors cycled, multiply to
the square tensor Tabc . This explains the power 3/4 in Corollary 1.3. The lower endpoint for
                                                             √
αF comes from the known algorithm for inner dimension ⌊ n⌋ [5, 55]. Section 2.4 proves
the two corollaries and explains the consequence for Strassen’s conjecture on tight tensors.

1.2   Why a faster matrix product would test lists too cheaply
Suppose one could save a fixed power of n below 9/4. A single bilinear algorithm witnessing
a strict saving could then be specialized to a finite field. Over that field we compare two
estimates for a different computation. Given a list of 2w words of length 2w bits, return one
if all the words are distinct and zero if any word repeats. Sorting the words and checking
adjacent pairs already gives a small network for this test. The question is how much of its
cost can be shared among several lists whose inputs vary independently.
    We measure cost by counting the vertices and wires in a tensor network with at most
three bits incident at each vertex. The network sums over its internal bits and, for each
The matrix multiplication exponent                                                                     3


setting of its exterior bits, must give the exact zero-one answer. With t lists it must give the
product of their t answers. Its internal wiring may connect different lists. Even with that
freedom, its volume per input bit exceeds wσ for some fixed σ > 0, uniformly for 1 ≤ t ≤ 2w
and all large w (Theorem 2.2). The proof reduces the problem to routing tagged records.
The companion results on tensor geometry and network complexity control this routing or
turn cheap routing into a violation of a counting lower bound [42, 43].
    The hypothetical matrix multiplication algorithm gives an estimate in the opposite
direction. Consider a trivalent network on e exterior bits with size at most e1.05 , where
e is large. We can represent a tensor power of its table in one new network. Per copy,
the new network saves a power of the logarithm on the old size and charges an arbitrarily
small power of the logarithm per exterior bit (Theorem 2.1). The replicated bits remain
individually accessible, so we can apply the construction again. Starting with the sorting test
and compressing a fixed number of times gives volume O(wη ) per input bit for any chosen
0 < η < σ. The batch still has at most 2w lists. Section 2 gives the short contradiction and
the passage back to arbitrary fields. Most of the proof is devoted to deriving this compression
from a strict saving in matrix multiplication.

1.3    Three wires and a shared label
To see how the exponent governs network identities, consider a number assigned to each tensor
which is multiplicative under tensor products, additive under direct sums and monotone
under linear maps on the factors. With the usual normalization this is a spectral point in
Strassen’s sense [52]. Its logarithm is a price. A wire shares an index between two factors.
Its price per bit defines a distance between their sites. The companion paper [42] extends
these distances to spaces of nonpositive curvature. Section 3 recalls the properties used here.
    Figure 1 gives two descriptions of Tn . Each of i, j, k occurs at exactly two factors.
The price of the tensor is therefore the sum of three wire lengths times log2 n. A rank
decomposition with r terms supplies the same tensor from a single shared label, the choice of
term, with r possible values. If the price per bit of this three-way label is D, monotonicity
gives
                      (sum of the three wire lengths) log2 n ≤ D log2 r.
Thus the ratio of a triangle’s perimeter to its shared-label rate is a lower bound on the
exponent. Prices coming from Euclidean flats give ratio two [42]. A different geometry is
needed to reach 9/4.

                               C                                     C
                         i            k
                                                                     ℓ
                     A                    B                A                   B
                                j
                      Three indices of Tn               One label for a rank term

Figure 1. The three tensor factors correspond to the two inputs and the output paired against a
third matrix. On the left, A, B, C see (i, j), (j, k), (k, i). On the right, all three see the same label
ℓ ∈ [r] and apply their own linear maps.
The matrix multiplication exponent                                                              4


    The critical ratio occurs at three tips of a tripod with unit arms. The distances between
tips are all two. If the shared-label rate is 8/3, the ratio is 6/(8/3) = 9/4. Under the strict-
saving hypothesis such a tripod cannot occur. Part I exploits this exclusion. If varying the
parameters of a tensor were to move its preferred site in a certain family of prices, the motion
would produce a homogeneous tripod. A rectangular multiplication decoder first determines
the limiting rate when one arm is long. The rigidity of polynomial multiplication then carries
this equality to the tripod with three equal arms (Proposition 8.1). Ruling out that motion
lets us price an entire family of tensors in a single extension of the sites. This synchronization
is the geometric input to compression. Once the lower bound is proved, we can also identify
the three wire lengths at every price maximizing the value of Tn (Corollary 11.1).

1.4   From prices to a smaller network
The family we use consists of sphere tensors. Their entries are determined by marked points.
When these points collide, a tensor expands into contractions through legs of controlled
dimension. Under the strict-saving hypothesis we can also determine the largest prices
these tensors attain. For prescribed leg widths (logarithms of dimensions), they attain
asymptotically the cost of preparing a tensor at the cheapest common site and transporting
each leg to its destination (Section 14). The marked points that specify the entries and the
sites that determine prices play different roles. Figure 2 shows how this calculation enters
the two parts of the network construction.

                            A strict square saving forces sphere prices
                                              Part I


                      Stable sorting and                  Concentration of
                     batched table lookup                 the incident bits
                            Part II                            Part III


                                  Compression of tensor powers
                                             Part IV

Figure 2. The implication needed for the lower bound. Sorting and concentration give complementary
estimates on the same network. Repeated compression would violate the independent-list lower bound
of Theorem 2.2.

    The compression must pay for each table of a network and for the records that say how
tables are connected. Direct lookup in a table of u incident bits can cost 2u . In a large
batch, sorting queries together with table entries amortizes that cost. Part II uses incidence
inequalities from sphere tensors to route records. For 2u records of width O(u) the resulting
stable sort costs O(up ) per record for some p < 2. Wider records admit a separate estimate
that keeps the cost close to linear in their width.
    The strict inequality p < 2 matters because the quantity controlled by collisions is
proportional to the square of a leg width. Part III realizes the wiring with sphere tensors,
moves insertions across their seams and merges them. The use of fermionic contractions
to reduce wiring is related to the crossing construction of [32, 42] and to compression in
subexponential satisfiability algorithms [17, 40]. Merging concentrates the incident bits at
The matrix multiplication exponent                                                            5


fewer stations while controlling the sum of squared widths. Part IV applies these identities in
rounds. The expansion has many summands with different wirings. A single network must
describe the possible wirings, route the records, look up the entries and sum the contributions.
Counting all four operations gives the logarithmic saving in Theorem 2.1 and completes the
contradiction.

1.5   Earlier bounds for matrix multiplication
Strassen applied his seven-product identity recursively to blocks, giving exponent log2 7 [50].
Subsequent algorithms found savings by working with several matrix products at once. Pan
aggregated trilinear forms [46], Bini showed how interpolation converts approximate bilinear
identities into exact algorithms [10], and Schönhage’s asymptotic sum inequality turned a
direct sum of matrix products into an upper bound on the exponent of a single product [47].
Coppersmith and Winograd studied recursive extraction of disjoint products [23]. Strassen’s
laser method extracts them from tensor powers [51], and Coppersmith and Winograd designed
an auxiliary tensor for which progression-free sets accomplish the extraction [24].
     The successive improvements depended on how many products could be kept indepen-
dent. Analyses of higher tensor powers [25, 37, 49, 56], refined extraction and asymmetric
hashing [2, 3, 27, 57], and numerical optimization [28] sharpened the bounds obtained from
the Coppersmith–Winograd tensor. Staggered extraction gave ωF < 2.371055 over every
field [44]. A separate line of work embedded matrix products in group algebras [18, 20] and
in the algebras of coherent configurations [19].
     The upper bound 9/4 comes from polynomial multiplication. In Strassen’s asymptotic
spectrum [52], asymptotic rank is the maximum of normalized functions that are additive
on direct sums, multiplicative on tensor products and monotone under restriction. Over C,
the upper-bound proof tracks how these functions value polynomial multiplication as the
two input degrees grow. Degenerations give a lower bound on the growth of this profile.
Interpolation gives an upper bound that depends on the value at matrix multiplication.
Comparing the two bounds yields 9/4 [41, Theorem 1.1]. The central degeneration separates
blocks that have independent variables on two legs and share the third. Partial labels and
semidirect sums give related formulations of this step [38, 45]. Lü proved the separation step
in arbitrary characteristic [39]. Amanov, van den Berg and Zuiddam obtained the bound
over every field [5, Theorem 1.1]. See also the tensor-value proof [26] and Shpilka’s explicit
algorithms [48]. The rectangular version of the argument [5, 55] connects these estimates to
the dual exponent studied in [22, 57].
     On the lower-bound side, Bläser proved that the ordinary rank of Tn is at least 52 n2 − 3n
over every field [11]. Over C, Landsberg gave an ordinary-rank bound of 3n2 − o(n2 ) [35],
and Landsberg and Michałek gave a border-rank bound of 2n2 − ⌈log2 n⌉ − 1 [36]. A constant
factor above n2 disappears on taking roots along the sequence Tn⊗j ≃ Tnj . Barriers for the
laser method and bounds from tensor irreversibility address algorithms that pass through
specified intermediate tensors [6, 14]. For polynomial multiplication, Amanov, van den
Berg and Zuiddam similarly proved that 9/4 is optimal for a specified set of degenerations
and combination rules [5, Theorem 5.4]. Theorem 1.1 gives the matching lower bound
for unrestricted arithmetic circuits. Related work on the spectrum constructs quantum
functionals [15] and explicit points over arbitrary fields [4], and describes the sublevel sets
of asymptotic rank by polynomial equations [16]. Wigderson and Zuiddam survey this
framework [59].

2     The obstruction from list distinctness
Fix a finite field k. Consider the test that accepts a list precisely when its records are pairwise
distinct. Sorting gives one way to perform the test. Our lower bound applies to every tensor
network computing it, even when a single network tests many lists at once. The contradiction
will come from estimating the cost per input bit. A matrix multiplication exponent below
9/4 would drive that cost below the lower bound after a fixed number of compressions.

2.1    Two estimates for networks on bits
A bit tensor of arity u is a function {0, 1}u → k. In a tensor network each vertex carries
such a table. A wire pairs two argument positions, and each unpaired position is a named
exterior bit. For a fixed setting of the exterior bits, multiply the vertex entries and sum
over all settings of the internal wires. This gives the tensor represented by the network. A
network is trivalent if each vertex has arity at most three. Its size counts the vertices and
exterior bits. Its volume counts the vertices and all wires, including the exterior wires. These
counts agree up to an absolute factor by trivalency. For networks of unrestricted arity we
count vertices plus their total arity.
    The first estimate starts with an arbitrary trivalent network. Its conclusion gives a
network for a tensor power, with separate exterior bits for each copy. On these bits it must
compute the product of the original answers.

Theorem 2.1 (Compression of tensor powers). Suppose ωk < 9/4. For every η > 0 there
is a γ > 0 such that the following holds for all sufficiently large n. Write h = ⌈log2 n⌉. If a
trivalent k-network of size m ≤ n1.05 with e ≥ n exterior bits represents V , then for some
integer d ≥ 1 its power V ⊗d has a trivalent k-network with de distinct exterior bits and size
at most
                                       d mh−γ + ehη .                                     (2.1)
                                                       

Here d ≤ no(1) uniformly in the input network for fixed k, η.

    Section 27 proves Theorem 2.1. Per copy, the first term saves a power of h on the original
size. The second charges hη for each exterior bit. The choice of η lets us compare this charge
with the cost of list distinctness.
    For w ≥ 1, let Dw be the zero-one tensor for a list of 2w words of 2w bits each. Its value
is one exactly when the words are pairwise distinct. It has nw = 2w 2w exterior bits. The
tensor Dw ⊗t tests distinctness separately within each of t independently specified lists. The

network may connect vertices across lists. Let volk denote minimum trivalent volume over k.

Theorem 2.2 (Volume of list distinctness). There is a constant σ > 0, depending only on k,
such that for all sufficiently large w and every integer 1 ≤ t ≤ 2w ,
                                             ⊗t
                                      volk (Dw  ) > tnw wσ .                                  (2.2)

    Section 4 proves Theorem 2.2. Its uniformity in t is essential because the replication
factor in Theorem 2.1 is chosen by the compression.
The matrix multiplication exponent                                                               8


2.2   Comparison at a fixed input length
Proposition 2.3. Over every finite field k, ωk ≥ 9/4.
Proof. A sorting network on 2w records uses O(2w w2 ) comparisons [8]. Comparing and
conditionally exchanging two 2w-bit words takes O(w) Boolean gates. After sorting, the
conjunction of the inequalities between adjacent words computes Dw . To realize this circuit
as a tensor network, use the zero-one table of each gate, copy bits at fan-outs with equality
tables, and pin the output to one. For each accepted input there is exactly one consistent
assignment of the internal wires. This gives a trivalent network over k of size m0 = O(nw w2 ).
    Suppose ωk < 9/4. Choose 0 < η < σ, take γ from Theorem 2.1, and fix an integer
J > 2/γ. Set n = nw and h = ⌈log2 n⌉ = w + O(log w). These parameters stay fixed through
all J applications of the theorem. At step j write tj for the number of lists, mj for the
network size and xj = mj /(tj n) for the size per exterior bit. Initially t0 = 1 and x0 = O(w2 ).
With replication factor dj , (2.1) gives

                             tj+1 = dj tj ,     xj+1 ≤ h−γ xj + hη .

Inductively, at each of the first J steps the network has at least n exterior bits and tj = no(1) ,
since the bound on dj is uniform and J is fixed. The recurrence gives xj = O(w2 + hη ), so
mj = tj nxj ≤ n1.05 for large w. This verifies the size hypothesis at each step. Summing the
recurrence gives
                                                  J−1
                             xJ ≤ h−Jγ x0 + hη          h−rγ = O(wη ).
                                                  X

                                                  r=0
Since log2 tJ = o(w), Theorem 2.2 applies to these tJ lists. Their network has volume
O(tJ nwη ) by trivalency, which violates (2.2) for sufficiently large w.

2.3   Passing from finite fields to arbitrary fields
The step that permits specialization is to extract a single bilinear identity with a strict rank
saving. Its coefficients generate a finitely generated ring, and its equations survive reduction
to a finite field.
Lemma 2.4 (Bilinearization of a formal circuit). If a formal circuit for multiplying two
s-by-s matrices over F uses q nonscalar multiplication or division gates, then

                                         rankF (Ts ) ≤ 2q.                                   (2.3)

Here q counts a multiplication when both inputs are nonconstant and a division when its
divisor is nonconstant.
Proof. Over the infinite field F , choose matrices A, B at which all intermediate rational
functions are regular and every divisor is nonzero. Evaluate the circuit on A + uX, B + vY
with u2 = v 2 = 0. Each gate has a unique expansion

                                f = f00 + uf10 + vf01 + uvf11 .

Here f00 is a scalar, f10 is linear in X, and f01 is linear in Y . At a product gate f = ab, the
mixed coefficient satisfies

                            f11 = a00 b11 + a11 b00 + a10 b01 + a01 b10 .
The matrix multiplication exponent                                                           9


Only the last two terms require new products of linear forms. At a quotient gate f = a/b,
use bf = a and b00 ̸= 0 to obtain

                           b00 f11 = a11 − b11 f00 − b10 f01 − b01 f10 .

This again requires at most two new products. Linear operations, including multiplication
or division by a field scalar, contribute none. The mixed coefficients of the outputs are the
entries of XY . They all belong to the span of at most 2q products of an X-linear form and a
Y -linear form, giving (2.3).

Lemma 2.5 (Finite specialization). If ωF < 9/4 over a field F , then ωk < 9/4 over some
finite field k.

Proof. The circuit bound has the form O(s9/4−ϵ ) for some fixed ϵ > 0. By Lemma 2.4, at a
sufficiently large side s ≥ 2 we have a rank decomposition of Ts over F with r < s9/4 terms.
Let R ⊆ F be the unital ring generated over Z by all of its coefficients. The decomposition
identities are polynomial equations over R with target coefficients zero and one.
    Choose a maximal ideal of R. Its residue field k is finitely generated as a Z-algebra.
In positive characteristic Zariski’s lemma makes k a finite extension of its prime field. In
characteristic zero the same lemma over Q would make k a number field. Multiplying its
algebra generators by a common positive integer M would then make them algebraic integers.
Consequently the generated ring would lie in the ring of integers of k with M inverted. This
ring omits 1/ℓ for every prime ℓ ∤ M , whereas the field k contains all of Q. Thus k is finite.
Reducing the identities gives rankk (Ts ) ≤ r.
    Finally, take the j-th power of this fixed bilinear algorithm. Each of the three linear
maps can be applied one tensor coordinate at a time. Since s2 ≤ r by flattening rank, every
intermediate array has at most rj entries. The linear work and the rj scalar products together
cost Or,s (jrj ) operations at side sj . Padding to the next power of s gives ωk ≤ logs r <
9/4.

Proof of Theorem 1.1. Proposition 2.3 and Lemma 2.5 give the lower bound over every field.
The upper bound over every field is due to Amanov, van den Berg and Zuiddam [5].

2.4   Rank bounds from the exponent
We use the passage from an algorithm at one fixed size to an algorithm on tensor powers,
together with the closure argument for border rank [10, 13, 52].

Proof of Corollary 1.2. Fix n ≥ 2. A rank-r decomposition of Tnj over any field K gives
ωK ≤ lognj r by the recursion and padding in Lemma 2.5. Theorem 1.1 therefore gives
rankK (Tnj ) ≥ n9j/4 at each j. Taking roots gives the asymptotic-rank lower bound. At
K = F and j = 1, Lemma 2.4 now gives the claimed lower bound of ⌈n9/4 /2⌉ on the number
of nonlinear gates.
    For the geometric border bound, work in the fixed tensor space V over F containing
Tn . Suppose Tn is in the Zariski closure of the rank-at-most-r tensors. For each j, their
j-th powers span a space containing Tn⊗j , since membership of a tensor power in this span
is a closed condition. The number of coefficients of a homogeneous polynomial of degree
j in dim V variables bounds the dimension of the span. We can thus express Tn⊗j using
The matrix multiplication exponent                                                              10


On (j dim V −1 ) such powers, each of rank at most rj . Comparison with the lower bound at nj
and passage to j-th roots give r ≥ n9/4 .
    For the asymptotic-rank upper bound fix ε > 0. At side nj the known circuit upper bound
and Lemma 2.4 give a decomposition of rank rj = OF,ε (nj(9/4+ε) ) over F . Its coefficients
belong to a finite extension Ej /F , of degree dj . For each t, power this decomposition t times.
Expand each of the three vectors in each simple tensor in an F -basis of Ej containing 1,
then project the products of basis coefficients onto the coordinate of 1. The tensor being
represented is defined over F , so this gives

                                      rankF (Tn⊗jt ) ≤ d3j rjt .

The limit defining asymptotic rank exists by submultiplicativity. First take jt-th roots and
let t → ∞ with j fixed. Then let j → ∞ and ε ↓ 0. The resulting upper bound is n9/4 over
the original field.

    At finite sizes, Corollary 1.2 also conflicts with the dyadic border-rank estimate proposed
in the first version of Sun’s preprint [54]. At n = 28 the proposed upper bound is 217 and
our lower bound is 218 .
    The square tensor also decides a prediction about tight three-tensors. A support is tight
when each local basis admits distinct integer weights whose sum is zero on every support
triple. In Tn , take B > n and assign weights i − Bj, Bj − B 2 k and B 2 k − i to eij , ejk , eki ,
respectively. Each assignment is injective on its factor and the three weights sum to zero.
Each one-factor flattening has rank n2 . Strassen’s conjecture for tight tensors predicts
asymptotic rank n2 in this case [53, Conjecture 5.3]. Corollary 1.2 gives n9/4 .

Proof of Corollary 1.3. The three one-factor flattenings have ranks ab, bc, ca. These are
lower bounds for geometric border rank by the closedness of matrix-rank conditions, and for
asymptotic rank by multiplicativity of flattening ranks. Grouping corresponding factors in

                                  Ta,b,c ⊗ Tb,c,a ⊗ Tc,a,b ≃ Tabc

turns each of the three indices into a tuple with abc possibilities. Both ranks are invariant
under cyclic relabeling of the factors and submultiplicative under tensor product. Hence
each of their values on Ta,b,c , cubed, is at least (abc)9/4 by Corollary 1.2. The case abc = 1 is
immediate.
    For a = c = n and b = ⌊nβ ⌋, the geometric border bound is Ω(n3(2+β)/4 ). The bilin-
earization in Lemma 2.4 applies entrywise to rectangular products as well. A quadratic
exponent therefore requires 3(2 + β)/4 ≤ 2. This gives αF ≤ 2/3, and the upper bound for
multiplication at β = 1/2 gives αF ≥ 1/2 [5].


3    Tensor prices and wire distances
A bilinear algorithm of rank r constructs its tensor from r independent scalar products by
local linear maps. A spectral point assigns a value to tensors that can only decrease under
such maps. Tensoring multiplies the values, so their logarithms add. We call these logarithms
prices. Applied to a wire, the price measures the cost of sharing an index between its two
sites. This is how a spectral point gives a geometry on the sites of a tensor network.
The matrix multiplication exponent                                                           11


3.1   From tensors to distances
Fix a field and a finite set S of at least two sites. Each tensor has one finite-dimensional
vector space at each site. A tensor T is a restriction of U if one linear map at each site takes
U to T . For a tensor product, group the factors at each site. For a direct sum, take the
direct sum of the spaces at every site, so the two tensors occupy separate blocks. The unit 1
is the tensor 1 in one-dimensional spaces.
    A spectral point φ is a restriction-monotone map to R≥0 that preserves zero and the unit
and satisfies
                    φ(T ⊕ U ) = φ(T ) + φ(U ),       φ(T ⊗ U ) = φ(T )φ(U ).
Every nonzero tensor restricts to 1, so its value is at least one. The price at scale D > 0 is
H(T ) = D log φ(T ), with H(0) = −∞. In this section and Part I logarithms are natural.
Thus the prices of [42] are multiplied by log 2 at the same scale.
   For a nonempty A ⊆ S, the tensor
                                                 q O
                                      ∆A (q) =
                                                 X
                                                           ej
                                                 j=1 i∈A

shares a q-valued index among the sites of A. Its other legs are one-dimensional. Tensoring
diagonals multiplies their dimensions, and a local projection can discard any set of labels.
Comparing powers of dimensions q and 2 therefore gives

                              H(∆A (q)) = δ(A) log q,           q ≥ 2.

We call δ(A) the common-label rate on A. In particular ∆S (q) is a direct sum of q units, so
δ(S) = D. For a ̸= b, ∆{a,b} (q) is a wire of dimension q. Write d(a, b) = δ({a, b}) and set
d(a, a) = 0. For example, matrix rank across a fixed cut of S is a spectral point. At scale
one its wire distance is one for sites on opposite sides of the cut and zero for sites on the
same side.
     Joining two wires by contracting their common endpoint gives a wire between their
other endpoints. Monotonicity and additivity of price give the triangle inequality for d. A
qi -dimensional wire can also move a leg of dimension qi from xi to yi . Moving each leg of a
nonzero tensor T , then applying the same argument in reverse, gives the transport bound

                          |H(T ; x) − H(T ; y)| ≤       d(xi , yi ) log qi .               (3.1)
                                                    X

                                                    i

Here x = (xi ) and y = (yi ) are the placements of the legs. Group the legs assigned to one
site. When all legs are at a single site, any nonzero tensor there is equivalent to the unit and
has price zero.

3.2   Geometric extension and spectral comparison
A spectral space is a metric space with compatible prices for all finite placements, whose
wire rates are the given distances. Compatibility allows trivial legs to be added and legs at
coincident sites to be grouped. On a finite set of positive diameter the scale is its common-
label rate, which may vary with the set. At a single point the scale is zero. Identifying sites
at zero wire distance turns a spectral price on finitely many sites into a spectral space.
The matrix multiplication exponent                                                              12


   Every spectral space embeds, with all its prices, in a complete CAT(0) spectral space. In
the extension every pair a, b has a unique midpoint m, and every point o satisfies

                          d(o, m)2 ≤ 12 d(o, a)2 + 21 d(o, b)2 − 14 d(a, b)2 .

Prices are jointly convex as their legs move along geodesics. Distances and prices may be
rescaled together. In a pointed metric ultralimit of these spaces, the price of a fixed tensor
on a bounded sequence of placements is the ultralimit of its prices [42, Sections 2 and 2.3,
Theorem 4.3 and Proposition 3.3]. We also use convexity of distance and nonexpansiveness of
nearest-point projection onto a nonempty closed convex set. A finitely supported probability
measure has a unique barycenter b minimizing its mean squared distance. Evaluating the
same mean at z increases it by at least d(z, b)2 [12, 42].
    Over an algebraically closed field, price sublevel sets in a fixed tensor format are Zariski
closed. Under a field extension, the possible joint values of spectral points on tensors defined
over the original field are preserved [42, Section 2]. Finally, if two nonzero tensors on the
same sites satisfy φ(T ) < φ(U ) for every spectral point, there is a fixed integer C such that,
for every j ≥ 1, T ⊗j is a restriction of the direct sum of C copies of U ⊗j . We use this
strict form of spectral comparison to turn price inequalities into exact tensor restrictions [42,
Lemma 2.2].


4     The cost of testing many lists
Two distinctness tests suffice to check that records have reached their specified destinations.
This gives a common starting point for the two cases of Theorem 2.2. In a field without
ports, the possible destinations force many wires to have large total length in a suitable
spectral space. In a finite field with ports, cheap distinctness tests would make it possible to
compress a family of tensors with a known lower bound.

4.1    Checking a tagged permutation
Write h = w − 1, N = 2h , and index records by the h-bit words. The tagged-permutation
tensor Tag(h, h) of [42, Definition 8.1] takes N records (ji , xi ) and an ordered list of N
payloads yj , with h bits per tag or payload. Its value is one when i 7→ ji is a permutation
and xi = yji for every i. Its other entries are zero.
    The first Dw test checks the tags. Encode each ji as (0, ji , 0h+1 ) and fill the remaining
N positions with the distinct words (1, j, 0h+1 ). All 2N words have length 2w, and the test
passes precisely when the tags form a permutation π.
    The second Dw test checks the payloads. Its first N words are (ji , 0, xi , 0). At the position
for j in the second half, sum over two choices, the reserve word (j, 1, 0h , 0) with weight 1
and the comparison word (j, 0, yj , 0) with weight −1. For tags forming π, the only possible
collision at address j is between the comparison word and the word from i = π −1 (j). Thus
the signed sum factors as

                                 1 − 1{xπ−1 (j) ̸=yj } =
                           Y                              Y
                                                               1{xπ−1 (j) =yj } .
                             j                             j

This identity holds in every characteristic. A summed selector bit for each address controls
its word and its weight. Copying the tags, selecting the words, and pinning the constants
The matrix multiplication exponent                                                          13


cost O(N h) trivalent volume. Consequently, if Dw  ⊗t has a realization of volume V , two uses

of that realization give a network for Tag(h, h)⊗t of volume at most 2V + O(tN h), including
when the realization shares work among lists.

4.2   Fields without ports
A class of Hadamard spaces has ports if it admits regular graphs of unbounded order and
common bounded degree with the following property. For every placement of their vertices
into any space of the class, the mean squared distance of two independent uniform vertices is
at most a fixed constant times the mean squared edge length. A field has ports when its
Hadamard spectral spaces have this property [42, Definition 11.1].
Lemma 4.1 (Uniform destinations). Let the field have no ports. Consider a fixed trivalent
topology with r input bits and r output bits. Suppose that for every permutation in the support
of a distribution, some choice of vertex tables realizes the r independent wires joining each
                           √ If each input has a uniform destination on a set of q ≥ 2 outputs,
input to its assigned output.
the topology has size Ω(r log q). The destination sets may depend on the input.
Proof. Give each exterior bit its own equality leaf. Connect the resulting components in a
path by adding edges to the graph used to measure distances. This increases the maximum
degree by at most two and gives m edges, with m at most a constant times the network size.
Let α be the law of a uniform oriented edge, and let µ be the joint law of a uniform input
and its random destination. For any placement in a spectral space with distance d,
                                      r Eµ d ≤ m Eα d.
Indeed, for each permutation the network tensors locally contract the product of its internal
bit wires to the prescribed exterior wires. Monotonicity and additivity of prices bound the
sum of the destination distances by the sum of the internal edge lengths. Average over the
permutations and include the extra edges on the right.
    We need a placement where many destinations are far away at once. The class of
Hadamard spectral spaces is closed under isometry, rescaling, closed convex subspaces, and
pointed ultralimits (Section 3). The product theorem [43, Theorem 6.1] gives a subclass
with the same properties and no ports, closed also under finite ℓ2 products. Let K be the
squared distance tables D = d2 realized in this subclass with Eα D ≤ 1. Products of rescaled
placements give convex combinations of tables. Connectivity bounds every entry, and pointed
ultralimits realize coordinatewise limits. Hence K is convex and compact.
    For a probability law ν ≤ 2µ on ordered pairs, let βν = maxD∈K Eν D. By rescaling,
Eν d2 ≤ βν Eα d2 for every placement in the subclass. Write X, Y for the endpoints under
ν. The graph entropy bound [43, Corollary 5.2] and the bounded degree of our graph give
Hν (Y | X) ≤ C(1 + βν ), with entropy in bits. Every pair has ν-mass at most 2/(rq), so
            Hν (Y | X) = Hν (X, Y ) − Hν (X) ≥ log2 (rq/2) − log2 r = log2 (q/2).
It follows that βν ≥ c log q for all sufficiently large q, uniformly in ν.
    Bilinear minimax on K and the compact convex set of laws ν ≤ 2µ gives a single
D = d2 ∈ K for which Eν D ≥ c log q for every such law. If the pairs with D < c log q
had µ-mass
         √ at least 1/2, conditioning µ on those pairs would violate this bound. Hence
Eµ d ≥ 12 c log q, while Eα d ≤ 1 by Cauchy–Schwarz. The price inequality gives the result.
For bounded q, counting exterior bits suffices.
The matrix multiplication exponent                                                            14


    In a network for Tag(h, h)⊗t , pin each list’s tags to a uniform permutation. The topology
realizes a permutation of r = tN h payload bits. Each input bit has q = N possible
destinations, at its own√ bit position in its own list, all equally likely. By Lemma 4.1,
2V + O(tN h) ≥ c tN h h. Since nw = 4N (h + 1), this proves (2.2) for any fixed 0 < σ < 1/2
and all sufficiently large w, uniformly in t.

4.3   Finite fields with ports
Fix a finite field with ports. We use the signed counting tensor Uℓ∗ on ℓ exterior bits from the

trivalent network of size ℓO(1) for one copy. Sum over its guess strings, requiring the unused
bits on each computation path to be zero, and weight the outcome by its sign. The exact
batch lower bound [43, Theorem 12.1] gives δ, ξ > 0 such that, for all sufficiently large ℓ,

Part I
Sphere tensors and their prices
5     Price extensions and simultaneous minimization
The compression argument introduces new sites at which to place tensor legs. A price on the
old sites already puts a lower bound on the price of any proposed placement. Contract the
legs at the new sites and evaluate the tensor that remains. One can do this on any tensor
power, with each new site contracting all its copies at once. Theorem 5.1 shows that these
lower bounds are sharp and gives a criterion for satisfying several price bounds at the same
placement. In Section 12 the tensors to be placed will depend on a parameter. To control
specialization of that parameter, we prove the criterion over valued fields. For a product of
ordinary wires incident to one new site, the minimum is computed in [42, Theorem 3.1].

5.1   Integral restrictions
Let K be a field with a real additive nonarchimedean valuation ν, normalized by ν(0) = +∞.
Its valuation ring is O = {c ∈ K : ν(c) ≥ 0}. We use the given coordinate bases at all sites.
A restriction is integral if each local matrix has entries in O. A valued price p of scale D > 0
on S (here |S| ≥ 2, as before) is real on nonzero tensors, has p(0) = −∞, p(1) = 0, and is
nonincreasing under integral restriction. Its scalar, product, and direct-sum rules are
                p(cT ) = p(T ) − ν(c)     (c ̸= 0),   p(T ⊗ U ) = p(T ) + p(U ),
                                                                                           (5.1)
                                    M     

                                                      X
                                p                         ep(Tj )/D .
                                     j                j

Here log is the natural logarithm. Thus multiplying by a scalar of positive valuation lowers
the price. The trivial valuation recovers ordinary prices.
    We shall construct prices using a convenient equivalent form of the direct-sum rule. For
tensors in common spaces, the right side of (5.1) bounds p( j Tj ) by local addition of blocks.

5.2   A test for extending a price
Fix p on S, and adjoin a finite set L of sites. Denote by pb an extension to S ⊔ L of the same
scale which retains p on tensors with trivial legs at L. Such extensions exist by placing the
added sites at old ones. Restriction to any intermediate list of sites containing S gives a
valued price of scale D. Indeed the diagonal on that list restricts to the base diagonal and is
a restriction of the full diagonal, so the diagonal test applies.
    A closure applies one integral linear functional at each site of L and leaves all base legs
free. The assisted cost of T is
                                               1
                                βp (T ) = sup    sup p ℓ(T ⊗m ) .                             (5.3)
                                                               
                                           m≥1 m ℓ

                                                                            m
At a site of dimension q the functional in this formula acts on K q , so it may couple the m
copies arbitrarily. Every extension satisfies pb(T ) ≥ βp (T ) by monotonicity under closure and
additivity on powers.
    For example, in the ordinary case take the product of two size-q wires from a to y and
from y to b, where a, b are old sites and y is new. Already on one copy, closing the two legs
at y by the pairing ei ⊗ ej 7→ δij recovers the wire from a to b. This gives the lower bound
d(a, b) log q for the product. Placing y at a attains it. More generally, placing y anywhere on
the geodesic from a to b attains the bound by additivity of distance along that segment.

Theorem 5.1 (Price extension). For every tensor T on S ⊔ L, βp (T ) = minpb pb(T ). For
an arbitrary family of nonzero tensors Ti and real numbers hi , a single extension satisfies
pb(Ti ) ≤ hi for all i if and only if

                              βp (Ti1 ⊗ · · · ⊗ Tij ) ≤ hi1 + · · · + hij                     (5.4)

for every finite list of indices, with repetitions allowed.

Proof. Minimizing one tensor. The zero tensor is immediate. For a prescribed nonzero T we
first assign prices to a finite list of distinct nonzero tensors A1 , . . . , As containing T . The
exponents of their mixed products will serve as variables. For x ∈ Qs≥0 set

                                      1 O ⊗nxi 
                             b(x) =     βp   Ai   ,             nx ∈ Zs .
                                      n    i
The matrix multiplication exponent                                                           17


In (5.3) the supremum can be restricted to multiples of any given integer by repeating each
closure independently. Thus assisted cost is homogeneous under powers and b is well defined.
Independent closures, taken at common multiples, make b superadditive, hence concave by
homogeneity.
    The increment per unit of coordinate i lies in the price interval (5.2) for Ai . For the
lower estimate isolate a minimum-valuation coefficient of every added factor, by coordinate
projections at the base sites as well as the added sites. For the upper estimate expand each
added factor in coordinates. Even for a closure coupling all copies, fixing the added indices of
each term induces an integral closure on the old factors. The coordinate factors remaining at
base sites have price zero, and the ordinary sum bound pays at most D j log qj per added
                                                                            P

factor of format (qj ). This proves the increment bounds after taking powers. In particular b
extends continuously to the real orthant.
    At a strictly positive rational x choose a supporting linear form u for this homogeneous
concave function, so that b(y) ≤ u · y and b(x) = u · x. The two-sided increment estimates
bound ui in the same price interval. We claim that the numbers ui respect the price relations
on the list. For example, if Ai = Aj ⊗ Ak , then b(x + t(ej + ek − ei )) = b(x) for both signs
of sufficiently small rational t. The supporting inequality in these two directions forces
ui = uj + uk . A scalar change shifts b by minus its valuation per copy, giving the scalar
relation by the same argument. If Ai restricts integrally to Aj , replacing copies of Aj by
copies of Ai can only increase b. The supporting inequality in the direction ei − ej therefore
gives ui ≥ uj . A base tensor increases b by its exact price per unit, since its factors pass
unchanged through every closure. For ∆S⊔L (q), closing its added legs by all-ones functionals
yields ∆S (q), and expanding its q diagonal entries gives the reverse bound. Thus its increment
is D log q.
    To check the ordinary sum bound, let Ai = Aj + Ak in the list. Expand nσ copies of Ai
in a product with multiplicities nx, for a rational 0 < σ ≤ xi . A term choosing a copies of Aj
has price after closure at most nu · x − nσui + auj + (nσ − a)uk . The ordinary sum bound
on the base and the binomial formula bound the price after closure of the original tensor by

                          nu · x − nσui + nσD log euj /D + euk /D .
                                                                   


Taking the supremum per n through admissible multiples yields b(x) = u · x on the left. This
proves the required bound on ui . Relations involving zero summands hold automatically.
    Let x tend to the unit vector corresponding to T . The support vectors have a convergent
subsequence and continuity gives uT = βp (T ) in the limit. Every finite collection of price
relations and the equality at T is therefore feasible by applying the construction to the list
of all tensors occurring in those constraints. Compactness of the product of intervals gives
a simultaneous solution for all such relations. The diagonal test following (5.1) makes this
solution a valued price. This proves attainment for one tensor.
Satisfying several bounds. Any extension obeying the proposed bounds satisfies (5.4) by
additivity on products. Conversely, take a finite list of k bounds. Form U as a direct sum of

Restricting a closure to the blocks of that word gives an integral closure, whose price is
bounded by (5.4). Sum over the words on the base and take the supremum per copy to
obtain
                                βp (U ) ≤ D log
                                                X

                                                 i

Choose C > maxi hi and Ni = ⌈en(C−hi )/D ⌉. These multiplicities bring every Ni enhi /D into

                                                                                       D log(2k)
         D log Ni + npb(Ti ) ≤ βp (U ) ≤ nC + D log(2k),           pb(Ti ) ≤ hi +                .

Let n tend to infinity and apply compactness, first to the finite list and then, by the finite

    Two consequences will be used below. Given several extensions of p, there is a single
extension whose evaluations are bounded above by any fixed convex combination of theirs.
Indeed, each extension bounds the assisted cost of every mixed product, so the averaged
bounds pass (5.4). We may also enlarge the base before applying the test. Suppose an
enlarged base carries a price agreeing with p on the old base. A tensor supported on the old
base and the unknown sites has exactly the same closures, with trivial legs on the extra base
sites. Thus its assisted cost is unchanged. This observation also applies when the enlarged
base has larger scale. Theorem 5.1 then gives an extension at that larger scale.

5.3   Geodesic placement
Assume henceforth in this section that K contains a trivially valued subfield F . Given
pairs of sites aj , bj , place new sites yj first at the aj and then at the bj . The interpolation
consequence of Theorem 5.1 gives an extension bounding the price of every tensor by the
affine interpolation of these two evaluations, with weights 1 − θ, θ. In particular

                    d(aj , yj ) ≤ θ d(aj , bj ),   d(yj , bj ) ≤ (1 − θ)d(aj , bj ).

The triangle inequality forces equality in both bounds. Thus yj is the point a fraction θ of
the way along the geodesic from aj to bj in a complete CAT(0) spectral realization over F .
The same extension provides the interpolation bound for all tensors over K.
Lemma 5.2 (Geodesic determination and submean inequality). Ordinary price data on any
subset of a complete CAT(0) spectral space determine all prices on its closed geodesic convex
hull up to a price-preserving isometry fixing the given data. Moreover, suppose the prices at a
central placement are at most the weighted average of prices at finitely many end placements,
for every tensor on the original slots. In each placement adjoin points on corresponding
geodesic segments. If the two distances of each new point to its endpoints at the center are
the weighted averages of their values at the ends, the price inequality holds on the enlarged
placements.
Proof. For each new site, prescribe the prices of its size-two wires to its two endpoints. These
prices are the two geodesic distances multiplied by log 2. We claim that the price of any
T ̸= 0 on the enlarged list is the smallest number h for which the bound h and all the wire
bounds pass the product test. The actual price passes by closure. Conversely, enlarge the
base to include the actual geodesic points, and apply Theorem 5.1 to duplicate sites with
those bounds. In a common metric realization, the triangle inequality forces both endpoint
distances of each duplicate to be the prescribed distances. Uniqueness of geodesics identifies
the duplicate with the actual point. Transport (3.1) therefore gives the assertion. For a
collapsed base all its prescribed segments are constant and the assertion follows directly.
The matrix multiplication exponent                                                             19


    Iterating this characterization determines prices, including distances, on all finite geodesic
constructions. Completing their union gives the closed convex hull by continuity of geodesic
interpolation, and (3.1) extends the price identifications to the completion. An infinite
original subset causes no change since each finite construction starts from finitely many
points.
    For the second assertion, a collapsed central placement is immediate. Otherwise fix a
product in the test, a power and a closure on its new sites. The original submean inequality
bounds the central price of the closed tensor by the average of its closed prices at the ends. At
each end this is at most the price of the unclosed power by restriction, including a collapsed
end, where every nonzero tensor has price zero. Divide by the power and take the supremum
at the center. The averaged actual upper bounds on the enlarged placements thus pass the
product test at the center, and the averaged wire bounds agree with the prescribed ones
there. Apply the characterization just proved.

5.4   Unique evaluations at minimizing sites
A price minimum can admit several placements, as the two-wire example shows. To compare
minimizers as a parameter varies, we add a strictly convex penalty at each unknown site.
Fix o on the base. For a new site y, form the direct sum of 1 and the size-two wire from o to
y. Its direct-sum label is visible at every site in the configuration, so its price is

                     f (r) = D log 1 + exp(r log(2)/D) ,           r = d(o, y).
                                                          


For a nonzero tensor U on the base and a list of new sites, fix an integer M ≥ 1. Its pin
objective V is the product of U ⊗M with one such penalty tensor for each new site. For several
objectives, each is formed on the base and its own list, then padded by trivial legs at the
other lists.

Lemma 5.3 (Pinned evaluations). For a finite family of objectives on pairwise disjoint lists
of new sites, there is a same-scale extension minimizing each objective. All such extensions
have the same evaluations on every tensor on the joint list. These evaluations agree on
overlaps between subfamilies. For any such family, including an infinite one, the resulting
evaluations on tensors over F are realized in a complete CAT(0) spectral space.
    Let W be the product of a finite family, G = V βp (V ), and hT the pinned evaluation of
                                                 P

a nonzero test tensor T . For some cD > 0 depending only on D, and every n ≥ 1,

                                                                  j (log qj )
                                                              P              2
                                          ⊗n
                        0 ≤ hT − βp (W         ⊗ T ) − nG ≤                                  (5.5)
                                                         
                                                                                 ,
                                                                   8cD n
where qj are the dimensions of the test legs grouped at the added sites, including dimension
one for an unused site.

Proof. Successively enlarge the base and minimize the next objective using Theorem 5.1.
Each minimization attains its original minimum by the base-enlargement property.
    To compare two assignments within a common extension, interpolate all corresponding
sites at their midpoints, with valued price on U bounded by the mean of its two prices. For
any two sites a, b and their midpoint m,

                     f (d(o, m)) ≤ 12 f (d(o, a)) + f (d(o, b)) − cD d(a, b)2 .              (5.6)
                                                              
The matrix multiplication exponent                                                           20


Indeed wire distances are at most D, by restriction of the full diagonal. Choose cD > 0 small
enough that f (r) − 4cD r2 is convex and nondecreasing on [0, D], using the positive lower
bounds for f ′ and f ′′ there. Apply midpoint convexity to this function and the squared-
distance midpoint inequality to 4cD r2 . The sites of each objective contain the original base,
so its direct-sum penalty has scale D even in the larger configuration. If two assignments
minimize that objective, the midpoint is bounded above by the same minimum minus cD
times the sum of the squared distances between corresponding new sites. These distances
must all vanish.
    For two minimizing extensions P, P ′ , enlarge P by a second copy of the entire list,
imposing upper bounds on its evaluations from P ′ . These bounds pass the product test on
the old base and therefore on the enlarged one. Each copied objective attains its minimum,
so its sites are at distance zero from their counterparts. Transport shows P (T ) ≤ P ′ (T )
for every test tensor. Interchanging P, P ′ gives equality. Restricting to any subfamily still
minimizes its objectives. The resulting compatible ordinary evaluations on all finite lists give
a spectral space after quotient by zero distances, and hence the claimed complete extension.
    Finally, adjoin an optimizing copy to an arbitrary competing assignment as above, and
let dj be the distances between corresponding sites. The midpoint objective is at least G,
and (5.6) gives pcomp (W ) − G ≥ 2cD j d2j . Transport gives pcomp (T ) ≥ hT − j dj log qj .
                                        P                                          P

Multiply the first inequality by n, add the second, and complete the squares in the dj . Taking
the minimum by Theorem 5.1 proves the upper bound in (5.5). The optimizing assignment
itself gives the lower bound.


6     A valued lift and a submean inequality
In the synchronization argument, one parameter can approach several different values. We
need to compare the rate at which the tensor price falls near each of them. The scalar
model is a polynomial with several roots. The valuation records how closely the parameter
approaches a root, and the multiplicity gives the rate of decrease of the scalar price. For
tensors, Lemma 6.2 bounds the sum of these rates by the growth of the price on an enclosing
disk. The scalar comparison is familiar from the potential theory of the nonarchimedean
projective line [7]. Our tensor inequality follows by interpolation once we construct a price
over a field with a valuation.

6.1   The Gauss lift
Let H be an ordinary price of scale D > 0 over a field E, and put ϕ(A) = exp(H(A)/D),
with ϕ(0) = 0. The ring E[tQ ] consists of finite sums of rational powers of t. On its fraction
field K take the additive valuation ν given by the least exponent on nonzero elements of the
ring. Thus ν(tr ) = r, and a nonzero constant in E has valuation zero. Write [tj ]T for the
coefficient tensor of tj . A monomial tensor tj A should have price H(A) − j. For a general
tensor we form powers using the same t in every factor and take the largest coefficient price
with this valuation adjustment.
Theorem 6.1 (Gauss lift). There is a valued price p of scale D on K extending H. On
tensors with entries in E[tQ ] it is given by
                                        1
                            p(T ) = lim   max H([tj ]T ⊗n ) − j .                         (6.1)
                                                               
                                    n→∞ n j∈Q
The matrix multiplication exponent                                                                 21


Proof. Choose a common denominator m for the exponents and write x = t1/m . Temporarily
assign x an arbitrary weight b > 0. For a nonzero Laurent tensor A(x) consider the weighted
coefficient sum and its regularization
                                                                         D
                Nb (A) =         e−bi/D ϕ([xi ]A),         pb (A) = lim    log Nb (A⊗n ).
                           X
                                                                     n→∞ n
                            i

The sum bound for ϕ makes Nb subadditive. Applying the same bound to coefficient
convolution and using multiplicativity of ϕ shows that Nb is submultiplicative. Hence the
limit exists by subadditivity of the logarithms. The lowest coefficient of A⊗n is the n-th
power of the lowest coefficient of A, which gives a finite lower bound for the normalized
logarithms.
    There are OA (n + 1) exponents in A⊗n . Replacing the sum by its largest term therefore
costs OA (log(n + 1)/n) in normalized logarithmic price. It follows that pb (A) is a limit of
maxima of affine functions of b, whose slopes lie in a fixed bounded interval. In particular,
pb (A) is convex and Lipschitz. Setting b = 1/m gives Equation (6.1), independently of the
common denominator.
Separating the maximal coefficients. Submultiplicativity gives pb (T ⊗ U ) ≤ pb (T ) + pb (U ). For
the reverse inequality, a maximal coefficient from each power may contribute to a convolution
coefficient in which other terms cancel it. We isolate that pair by varying x in the first factor
alone. Multiplying by xa shifts pb by −ba, so it suffices to consider nonzero polynomials T, U .

Scalars and integral maps. For a nonzero scalar Laurent polynomial the lowest coefficient of
each power has the largest weighted size, since all nonzero scalar coefficients have ordinary
price zero. Thus p(f ) = −ν(f ). Multiplicativity now gives a well-defined extension to
fractions by p(A/f ) = p(A) + ν(f ).
    Consider a local map with polynomial entries of nonnegative degree. After taking its n-th
power, the matrix at each site has degree O(n). Each output coefficient of degree j is a sum
over the degrees chosen at the finitely many sites. There are polynomially many terms in n,
and each is an ordinary restriction of an input coefficient of degree at most j. The input
coefficient controls its term by monotonicity of H, and the degree weight only decreases. The
resulting logarithmic loss is O(log(n + 1)), which vanishes after normalization. For a matrix
of integral rational entries, choose a common denominator and remove its lowest power from
both numerator and denominator. The numerator matrix then has only nonnegative powers
and the denominator has valuation zero. Doing this at each site proves monotonicity for
every integral restriction.
    For the ordinary sum bound, the binomial expansion, with local permutations of copies,
gives
                                           n
                                                 !
                                     ⊗n        n
                         Nb ((T + U ) ) ≤         Nb (T ⊗j )Nb (U ⊗(n−j) ).
                                          X

                                          j=0
                                               j

For a fixed nonzero T and all 0 ≤ j ≤ n we have D log Nb (T ⊗j ) = jpb (T ) + o(n) uniformly.
The binomial formula yields exp(p(T + U )/D) ≤ exp(p(T )/D) + exp(p(U )/D). Common
denominators give this inequality on K. The lift agrees with H on constants, including
every diagonal tensor, so the diagonal test of Section 5 gives the direct-sum identity. Set
p(0) = −∞.
