6.2    Prices on disks
We now need a generic value of the price on each disk of parameters. Take an algebraically
closed field F and an ordinary price on a fixed finite set of sites over F . Enlarge the coefficient
field to E = F (U) for an uncountable set U of independent indeterminates. For a rational
tensor depending on finitely many of these variables, define its ordinary price over E as the
supremum over its F -valued specializations on the domain of definition.
    The fixed-format bound in Equation (5.2) makes this supremum finite for every nonzero
rational tensor. Its fixed-format price sublevel sets over F are Zariski closed (Section 3).
Hence the specializations within any positive tolerance of the supremum contain a nonempty
open set. Finitely many such sets meet on the common domain of definition. Applying the
price axioms at these simultaneous specializations and letting the tolerance tend to zero gives
the axioms over E. The extension is consistent on subfields generated by subsets of U and
invariant under permutations of U.
    Apply Theorem 6.1 over E. Write O for the valuation ring of K = Frac(E[tQ ]). A fresh
u ∈ U occurs in none of the data being compared. For r ∈ Q the disk with center a ∈ K and
valuation bound r is a + tr O. Its generic price is the price at a + tr u. Increasing r shrinks
the disk.

Lemma 6.2 (Specialization and the star inequality). For a polynomial tensor T (c) over
K, let P (c) = p(T (c)) on the base sites, or let P (c) = βp (T (c)) when T has additional sites.
With a fresh u and P∗ = P (u), one has

                           P (c) ≤ P∗    (c ∈ O),       P∗ = sup P (a).
                                                              a∈F

The generic price stays the same after an affine change of variable c 7→ a + bc with a ∈ O
and b ∈ O× . Here u is fresh also for a and b.
    Suppose T is nonzero of degree d. Choose finitely many centers cj ∈ O with distinct
residues and positive rational numbers rj , r∞ . Let

                            Pj = P (cj + trj u),       P∞ = P (t−r∞ u).

Then
                                X P∗ − Pj           P∞ − P∗
                                              ≤             ≤ d.                              (6.4)
                                 j
                                        rj            r∞

Equivalently, the first inequality bounds the center price P∗ by the weighted average of the
prices at the ends of the star. The weights are proportional to 1/rj and 1/r∞ . We call the
values on the star harmonic if the center equals this average. The weighted-average inequality
also holds for the zero family.

    For instance, a scalar polynomial T (c) = j (c − cj )mj with distinct cj ∈ F has generic
                                                   Q

price zero on the unit disk. On the inner disk at cj its price is −mj rj , giving a drop of mj
per unit of valuation length. On the outer disk its price is r∞ j mj . Both inequalities in
                                                                  P

Equation (6.4) are equalities.

Proof. The zero family is immediate. First let P (c) = p(T (c)) with T ̸= 0, and write
Q(c) = T (c)⊗n . Its degree is at most nd. Interpolation on nd + 1 fresh indeterminates uses
The matrix multiplication exponent                                                              24


only integral Lagrange weights at any c ∈ O, since the nodes have unit differences. All the
samples have price nP∗ by permutation symmetry, so the ordinary sum bound gives

                                p(Q(c)) ≤ nP∗ + D log(nd + 1).

Dividing by n and letting n → ∞ proves specialization. Conversely, interpolate at nd + 1
distinct nodes of F and evaluate at u. The same argument gives P∗ ≤ supa∈F P (a). Applying
specialization to an integral affine change of variable and its integral inverse proves the
asserted invariance. Specialization on nested disks also gives Pj ≤ P∗ ≤ P∞ .
    The inverse Vandermonde matrix at these nodes has integral entries, so interpolation
controls coefficients as well as evaluations. On the unit disk it gives p([cℓ ]Q(c)) ≤ nP∗ +
D log(nd + 1). Evaluating at t−r∞ u can increase any coefficient price by at most ndr∞ . The
price of their sum incurs another D log(nd + 1), proving P∞ − P∗ ≤ dr∞ in the limit.
    To prove the other inequality, we use interpolation on each inner disk to bound the low
Taylor coefficients at its center and on the outer disk to bound the high coefficients at infinity.
More precisely, interpolating Q(cj + trj v) and Q(t−r∞ v) in v and removing the scalar power
of t gives

                       p([v ℓ ]Q(cj + v)) ≤ nPj + rj ℓ + D log(nd + 1),
                              p([cℓ ]Q(c)) ≤ nP∞ − r∞ ℓ + D log(nd + 1).                     (6.5)

    Suppose the sum of the inner drops per unit length exceeds the outer growth per unit
length. Choose aj strictly between zero and (P∗ − Pj )/rj on the arms with positive drop and
zero on the other arms, so that

                                        aj > b > (P∞ − P∗ )/r∞
                                  X

                                    j

for some b. Put m = ⌈nb⌉ and mj = ⌊naj ⌋. The strict inequalities and Equation (6.5) give
ϵ > 0 such that, for all large n, the coefficients

                  [cℓ ]Q(c)   (m ≤ ℓ ≤ nd),      [v ℓ ]Q(cj + v)   (0 ≤ ℓ < mj )

all have price at most n(P∗ − ϵ). Together they determine Q(u) by an integral linear
combination. Indeed, subtract the high-degree part of Q. The remaining polynomial has
degree < m and its Taylor coefficients through order mj − 1 give its residue modulo (c − cj )mj
at each center. For large n, the total multiplicity j mj is at least m. The moduli are
                                                           P

pairwise comaximal over O because the center differences are units. The Chinese remainder
theorem thus recovers the low-degree polynomial by an O-linear map in monomial coordinates.
Computing the Taylor coefficients of the subtracted part and evaluating at u are integral
operations as well. These identities hold coefficientwise for tensors over K.
    The reconstruction expresses Q(u) using only O(n + 1) coefficients from the displayed list.
The sum bound would give nP∗ = p(Q(u)) ≤ n(P∗ − ϵ) + O(log(n + 1)), a contradiction.
    For the assisted cost, fix a power m in Equation (5.3) and write A(c, z) for the closure
of T (c)⊗m with functional entries z = (z1 , . . . , zs ). These entries occur polynomially. For
any fixed c, the supremum of p(A(c, z)) over Os equals the supremum over F s . Indeed,
interpolate A(c, z)⊗n on a rectangular grid in F s . For fixed m, there are Om (n + 1) nodes
per coordinate and polynomially many nodes in total. Evaluation at integral arguments has
integral weights, so the logarithmic cost per tensor copy tends to zero.
The matrix multiplication exponent                                                                   25


    Consequently βp (T (c)) is the supremum of the functions p(A(c, z))/m over m and constant
closing data. Omit identically zero tests when T ̸= 0. The other tests are nonzero at all
the generic points. Their specialization inequalities pass to the supremum, and taking
suprema over a ∈ F and over tests in either order gives the generic-value identity. Their
weighted-average inequalities pass to the supremum because each weight is positive. Finally,
each test satisfies the outer growth bound dr∞ after division by m, since degc A ≤ md. This
common bound also holds for the supremum.


7     A moving family forces a lower bound of 9/4
A tensor depending on a scalar parameter can have a different price-minimizing placement at
each parameter value. Section 12 will construct these placements on a disk. We show that a
nonconstant family satisfying the disk comparisons forces square matrix multiplication to
have exponent at least 9/4. The first step is geometric. At the depth where a translation
first moves a point, a rescaled neighborhood contains a tree in which two branches meet
at the valuation of the difference of their parameters. The second step uses prices on four
equal branches of that tree. With the fourth tip at a generic parameter, a label shared by
all four tips has price at most 3/2 times the price of a wire spanning the diameter, to first
order. Section 8 uses collisions between the tips to pass from this estimate to a three-site
comparison for matrix multiplication rank.
    Let F be algebraically closed. Take an uncountable set U of indeterminates, and put
            K0 = Frac F (U)[tQ ] ,        O = {z : ν(z) ≥ 0},         J = {z : ν(z) > 0},
                                   

with the lowest-exponent valuation and ν(0) = +∞. An indeterminate is fresh for finite data
in K0 if it does not occur in those data. Consider a family of sites xz , z ∈ J, in a spectral
space over F , subject to the following conditions.
    1. Adding the same w ∈ J to all indices, and permuting U, preserve the prices of all
       tensors over F .
    2. For a nonzero tensor on any finite list of slots, let P (c) be its price at the sites xai +bi c ,
       where ai , bi ∈ J. With u fresh, P (c) ≤ P (u) for c ∈ O. For cj ∈ O of distinct residues
       and positive rational lengths sj , s∞ , the same test satisfies
                           X P (cj + tsj u) − P (u)       P (t−s∞ u) − P (u)
                                                      +                      ≥ 0                  (7.1)
                            j
                                         sj                      s∞

       whenever the indices at the outer end lie in J. Here u is fresh also for the star data.
       Thus the price at the generic point of the disk is at most the weighted mean of its
       end prices, with weights proportional to the reciprocals of the corresponding lengths.
       The two comparisons are the ordinary-price versions of specialization and the submean
       inequality in Lemma 6.2.
    3. Some two sites have positive distance, and d(x0 , xz ) → 0 as ν(z) → +∞.
The inequalities apply in particular to distances, by testing a two-site wire and dividing its
price by the logarithm of its size.
Theorem 7.1 (Rigidity of analytic motion). If such a family exists, square matrix multipli-
cation over F has exact rank at least n9/4 for each side n.
The matrix multiplication exponent                                                           26


7.1   The threshold for displacement
To find a scale at which motion persists after rescaling, fix a point under all translations
of sufficiently high valuation. Its orbit starts to move when the valuation falls below the
threshold for that point. We shall choose the point so that its displacement has a strictly
negative left derivative at the threshold.
    By translation invariance xw ̸= x0 for some w ∈ J. Choose rational 0 < b < ν(w)
and replace xz by xtb z . This still gives a nonconstant family satisfying all three conditions.
Specialization of the wire d(x0 , xtb c ) bounds the radius of the new family by its value at a
fresh u. Thus we can work in the bounded closed convex hull X of the new sites, taken in a
complete CAT(0) spectral extension. By the geodesic determination of prices in Section 5, the
symmetries act on X by price-preserving isometries. Write gw for the action of translation.
    We first extend the affine comparisons in Condition 2 to sites gai +bi c Ai with Ai ∈ X. If
Ai is obtained by a finite sequence of geodesic interpolations between original sites, every
segment in its construction undergoes the same translation as Ai . Its length and the two
distances specifying the interpolation point are constant across the comparison. Lemma 5.2
therefore extends the star inequality through each interpolation. It extends specialization
in the same way, with the specialized placement as center and the generic placement as its
sole end. Now approximate arbitrary Ai by such points and apply the transport inequality
(3.1). The approximating sequences for these finitely many sites involve only countably
many indeterminates, so one choice of u serves throughout. If every Ai is invariant under
permutations, permuting indeterminates while fixing the finite test data makes the comparison
available at any fresh u.
    For r > 0 rational and A ∈ X, take u outside a countable support for A, such as the
variables in a sequence of finite geodesic constructions converging to A. Specialization gives

                         R(A, r) := sup d(A, gw A) = d(A, gtr u A).                       (7.2)
                                     ν(w)≥r

For any given w with ν(w) ≥ r, specialization bounds its displacement by the generic
displacement at a variable fresh for both A and w. Permuting that variable to u while fixing
a countable support for A gives the same bound for every w. Conversely, tr u itself belongs
to the subgroup. Apply (7.1) to the distance d(A, gtr c A) with one inner end at zero. For
positive rational r − s, r, r + b with s, b > 0, this gives

                      R(A, r) − R(A, r − s)   R(A, r + b) − R(A, r)
                                            ≤                       .
                                s                       b
Thus R(A, ·) is convex. It lies between zero and diam X, so it extends continuously to
r > 0 with a Lipschitz bound on each compact subinterval independent of A. Squeezing the
supremum in (7.2) between its values at rational depths identifies it with this extension at
real depths.
    The common fixed set Xr = {A : R(A, r) = 0} of the subgroup ν(w) ≥ r is closed and
convex. The circumcenter of any orbit of this subgroup belongs to Xr . Since the orbit of A
contains A and fits in the ball of radius R(A, r) about A, this gives d(A, Xr ) ≤ R(A, r). In
particular, f (r) = d(x0 , Xr ) is positive whenever the subgroup moves x0 , decreases with r,
and tends to zero by Condition 3. For 0 < h < r, the projection Ar of x0 to Xr satisfies

                       0 ≤ f (r − h) − f (r) ≤ R(Ar , r − h) − R(Ar , r).
The matrix multiplication exponent                                                                  27


The common Lipschitz bound makes f locally Lipschitz. Its strict decrease somewhere gives a
depth r∗ with f ′ (r∗ ) < 0. Fix A = Ar∗ . Every permutation of the indeterminates fixes x0 and
preserves Xr∗ , so it fixes this unique projection. The last display bounds the left derivative of
the convex function R(A, ·) at r∗ by f ′ (r∗ ). To the right of r∗ it vanishes identically. Writing
−k < 0 for the left derivative gives

                                 R(A, r∗ + h) = k(−h)+ + o(|h|).                                 (7.3)

7.2    Rescaling at the threshold
Take rational ε ↓ 0 and choose rational r0 = r∗ − ε + o(ε). In the pointed spectral ultralimit
at A, rescale distances and prices by 2/(kε). For each z ∈ K0 take the point Lz represented
by
                                          gtr0 z(tε ) A,
where the substitution fixes F (U). For fixed z the translation index is in J eventually and
the rescaled displacement is bounded by (7.3). Finite initial terms can be chosen arbitrarily.
Additivity of the index substitution gives translation invariance for all z ∈ K0 . Permutation
invariance follows since every permutation fixes A and commutes with the substitution. To
transfer the star comparison for arbitrary affine expressions in c, observe that their coefficients
become tr0 ai (tε ), tr0 bi (tε ) before passing to the limit. The centers cj (tε ) still have distinct
residues, each star length is multiplied by ε, and all the tested indices lie in J eventually.
Thus the common factors in (7.1) cancel and the inequality passes to the ultralimit. The
same argument applies to specialization.
    At a generic parameter the limiting distance follows directly from (7.2) and (7.3),

                               d(L0 , Ltr u ) = 2(1 − r)+       (r ∈ Q).

The same formula holds at every parameter of that valuation. More precisely,

                                  d(Lz , Lz ′ ) = 2(1 − ν(z − z ′ ))+ .                          (7.4)

For distinct indices write z ′ − z = tr v with ν(v) = 0. Translation and specialization give the
upper bound, which is already equality for r ≥ 1. If r < 1, test d(L0 , Ltr c ) on a star with two
inner ends centered at zero and at v. The slope on the zero arm of any length 0 < a < 1 − r
is −2. On the outer arm it is +2, by the generic distance formula. These slopes cancel in
(7.1), leaving a nonnegative slope on the v-arm. Specialization makes that slope nonpositive,
so its end distance equals the generic value 2(1 − r). Choose the length b ≥ 1 − r on this arm.
The upper bound already proved identifies Ltr (v+tb u) = Ltr v , which gives equality in (7.4).

7.3    Geodesics of the disk tree
The distance formula places the leaves. To locate the branch points, we prove that the
midpoint of two leaves lies on each geodesic joining one of them to a third leaf at least as far
away as the other.
    Let B = Lb , C = Lb+v , d(B, C) = 2h > 0, and let m be their midpoint. Take a star with
inner ends 0, 1, each of length h, and outer length s. On [B, C], prescribe the point m at the
center and the outer end, and B, C at the two inner ends. Its distances to each endpoint
are harmonic for the weights proportional to the inverse end lengths. Lemma 5.2 therefore
The matrix multiplication exponent                                                                28


permits comparing its distance to Lb+cv . At the inner ends this distance is zero by (7.4). At
the outer end it is at most 3h + 2s by going through B. Consequently the generic center
distance J∗ = d(m, Lb+uv ) satisfies
                                        2J∗ 3h + 2s − J∗
                                     0≤−    +               .
                                         h           s
Sending s to infinity yields J∗ ≤ h, and specialization gives the same bound throughout the
unit disk. More generally,
                    d(m, Lb+cv ) ≤ h + 2s           (ν(c) ≥ −s, s ≥ 0 rational).               (7.5)
Indeed the generic value for c = t−s u is convex in s by one-inner-end comparisons. It starts
at most h and is bounded above by 3h + 2s throughout. Its secant slopes from zero are thus
at most 2. Specialization proves the assertion.
    If d(B, Lz ) = 2h + 2s with s ≥ 0, apply (7.5) with c = (z − b)/v. The triangle inequality
forces equality and m ∈ [B, Lz ]. In particular every leaf at distance 2h from B gives the
same midpoint. For each rational ℓ ≤ 1, denote by P (z, ℓ) the point of [Lz , Lz ′ ] at distance
1 − ℓ from Lz for any sufficiently distant Lz ′ . For ℓ < 1 choose first z ′ at distance 2(1 − ℓ) and
use the midpoint just proved. For ℓ = 1 take Lz . Two distinct leaves Lz , Lz ′ at distance 2h
meet at depth 1 − h = ν(z − z ′ ). Choose a third leaf sufficiently far from both. The midpoint
test applied from each original leaf places their common midpoint on both geodesics to the
third. Above the meeting depth the two paths lie in opposite halves of [Lz , Lz ′ ]. Below it
they lie on the same segment from the midpoint to the third leaf, by uniqueness of geodesics.
Coincident leaves have the same path at every depth. This gives
                      d(P (z, ℓ), P (z ′ , ℓ′ )) = ℓ + ℓ′ − 2 min{ℓ, ℓ′ , ν(z − z ′ )}.        (7.6)
The point P (z, ℓ) thus depends only on the disk {z ′ : ν(z ′ − z) ≥ ℓ}. Its ancestors correspond
to the larger disks containing it.
    In a finite star comparison we can now prescribe points P (a + bc, ℓ) with depths harmonic
across that star. Choose a far leaf Lt−N with N sufficiently large. Its distance to every La+bc
in the finite test is the same, and the corresponding segment contains the prescribed site. The
prescribed distances to these two endpoints are 1 − ℓ and its complement in their common
distance. Lemma 5.2 applies. It also gives specialization for constant depths. In particular
the price of a fixed nonzero tensor along a ray c = ξ + tρ u is convex in ρ if each depth is
affine in ρ, since any three rational radii are compared by a star with one inner end at zero.
At ρ = 0, the generic values obtained from u and ξ + u coincide for integral ξ, by the two
specializations in opposite directions.

7.4    The price of a common label at four sites
Place the common label ∆[4] (q) = qj=1 e⊗4  j , q ≥ 2, at the tips of four equal branches of the
                                        P

disk tree. Choose the first three centers z1 = 0, z2 = 1, z3 ∈ F \ {0, 1} and let the fourth

common origin P (0, 0) and pairwise distance 2ℓ. Denote the price of their label divided by
log q by Dℓ . It vanishes at ℓ = 0. Joint geodesic convexity and the upper bound 4ℓ from four
size-q wires to the common origin give a finite one-sided slope
                                                           Dℓ

By (7.6) the two within-pair distances are h = 2ℓ − r, and all four distances between pairs
are 2ℓ. The path joining the pair midpoints grows by precisely the amount the within-pair
distances shrink. Figure 3 depicts the collision with z1 . Relabeling gives the collision with z3
used in Section 8. When r = 2ℓ, both pairs have merged. The grouped vectors ej ⊗ ej are
linearly independent at each end, so the label is equivalent under local maps to one size-q

Propagating the resulting limiting price equality to the balanced tripod gives a common-label
rate of 8/3. The three matrix multiplication wires on that tripod have total length 6, so the
rank comparison has exponent 6/(8/3) = 9/4.


8    A lower bound from the price of a shared label
Consider a square matrix product of side n, with its two inputs and its output at the tips
of a tripod with three unit arms. Each of the three matrix indices is shared by two tips at
distance 2, so the product tensor has price 6 log n. A rank-r decomposition supplies that
tensor from a label with r values shared by all three tips. If the rate of this shared label is
8/3, its price is (8/3) log r, and price monotonicity gives r ≥ n9/4 . We will find such a tripod
in the family constructed in Section 7. We first test the shared-label rate when one arm is
much longer than the other two. The following principle carries the resulting equality to
three equal arms.

Proposition 8.1 (Tripod principle). Over an algebraically closed field, place three sites at
pairwise distances ai + aj for i ̸= j, where a1 , a2 , a3 ≥ 0. Their common-label rate D(a)
satisfies
                                                                     −1 −1
                  D(a) ≥ D∗ (a) := a1 + a2 + a3 − a−1          −1
                                                          1 + a2 + a3       .
The reciprocal term is zero when any ai = 0.
    Now fix three infinite rays in a spectral space that form a metric tripod with origin O,
and suppose that their prices are homogeneous under radial dilation. In D(a), place site i on
ray i at distance ai from O. If D(a) − D∗ (a) vanishes at one positive triple, it vanishes at
every positive triple. The same conclusion holds if the defect tends to zero as one arm tends
to infinity with the other two held at fixed positive lengths.

    The first assertion applies to any placement with the specified distances. The second
compares placements on a single tripod. Sections 9–11 prove both assertions by studying
how the price of polynomial multiplication changes with its degree and with the positions of
its coefficients.
The matrix multiplication exponent                                                           31


8.1   A decoder for rectangular multiplication
Multiply an s × q matrix by a q × s matrix over the algebraically closed field F of Section 7,
taking s = N 1/2 and q = N 1/4 for a fourth power N . The product has N entries. Place its
output at A and its inputs at C, D′ , with respective arm lengths c, a, b. The outer indices
give wires of size s on AC, AD′ , and the inner index gives a wire of size q on CD′ . The price
of the product, divided by log N , therefore satisfies
                                               ab
               c + 34 (a + b) ≤ c + a + b −       ≤ D∗ (c, a, b) ≤ D(c, a, b).
                                              a+b
Here ab/(a + b) = 0 if a = b = 0. This gives the rectangular bound associated with half-width
multiplication [5, 55].
    In particular, for every η > 0 there are arbitrarily large such N for which the exact
tensor rank is at most N 1+η . Indeed, start with a sufficiently large fourth power N0 and a
                         1+η/2
full diagonal of size ⌈N0      ⌉. The displayed inequality at every spectral point gives strict
spectral comparison with this diagonal. Each tensor power of the product is a restriction of
a bounded number of copies of the corresponding power of the diagonal. At sufficiently large
powers the exponent 1 + η absorbs both this multiplicity and the rounding.

Lemma 8.2 (A decoder with short rows). For every ϵ > 0 there is a δ > 0 such that for
unbounded fourth powers N the N output bilinear forms Be of this product admit decomposi-
tions
           Be = Se + Re ,    rank Se ≤ N ϵ ,    dim⟨Re : 1 ≤ e ≤ N ⟩ ≤ N 1−δ .

   The two bounds have different uses. A short sum of simple products computes each Se
once the output index e is shared. The corrections Re may be expensive individually, but
their entire span can be sent across a single wire. To obtain the bounds uniformly in the
base format, we implement a tensor power of a decoder using one work register. For related
nonrigidity estimates for Kronecker products, see [1, 33].

Proof. It suffices to take 0 < ϵ < 1. Fix an integer K > 10/ϵ. For a base format with d
output entries and rank d ≤ r ≤ d1+η , let M be the d × r matrix that expresses its outputs
in terms of r simple bilinear products. We will choose η below. The Km-th tensor power
has N = dKm entries. Pad each decoder block M ⊗m with zero rows and columns to side 2t ,
where t = ⌈m log2 r⌉. The full padded decoder has side T = 2Kt . An index consists of K
registers of t bits each.
    Apply the first block matrix to the first register. For each subsequent block, swap its
register with the first, apply the block matrix there, and swap back. An exchange of registers
performs t simultaneous exchanges of pairs of bits. A bit exchange transposes two of four
letters, 01 and 10. Its matrix factors into a fixed number of elementary shears and diagonal
signs, over any field. Thus a register exchange consists of a fixed number of stages, each
applying the same local shear or diagonal sign at every bit position.
    For a shear, let the high letter be the target of its off-diagonal entry. Immediately before
and after the stage, project onto the coordinates whose register pair contains between t/4 − ρt
and t/4 + ρt high letters, for a fixed small ρ > 0. The discarded fraction of coordinates is at
most e−cρ t for large t, by the binomial tail. Telescoping over the O(K) projections bounds the
rank of the discarded part of the decoder by O(K)T e−cρ t ≤ T 1−γ , with γ = γ(K, ρ) > 0. On
The matrix multiplication exponent                                                           32


a path retained at a shear stage, every change creates a high letter, so at most 2ρt positions
change.
    Only the first register is acted on by a block matrix. Every other register participates in
just two exchanges, so its input lies within Hamming distance Cρt of its output on a retained
path, for an absolute constant C. Each output row of the retained decoder therefore has at
most                                       ! K−1
                                             t 
                            2
                                      X
                                t
                                                        ≤ T 1/K+oρ↓0 (1)
                                     j≤Cρt
                                             j

nonzero entries. Choose ρ so that this exponent is below ϵ/2, then choose η sufficiently small.
Since T ≤ 2K N 1+η , for all large m the two bounds become N ϵ entries per retained row and
rank N 1−δ for the discarded matrix, with δ = γ/2. Remove the zero padding and apply both
matrices to the list of simple bilinear products. The retained rows give the forms Se , and the
image of the discarded row space spans the Re .

Choose the collision in Section 7 where site 4 approaches site 3, and denote sites 1, 2, 3, 4

within AB and CD′ are both h, and all four cross distances are L. Write Dℓ (h) for the
four-site common-label rate. At the collision Dℓ (0) = L, and at the generic four-site placement
Dℓ (L) = Dℓ . Convexity gives

    The first three sites lie on the fixed rays z1 , z2 , z3 out of the depth-zero point. Take the

in a spectral ultralimit. On these fixed rays the rescaled price of every nonzero tensor has
a limit, since its original price is convex in t and vanishes at zero. The limiting prices are
homogeneous under radial dilation.
    Set t = h/2 and hold b = 2L/h − 1 fixed. The three depths become t(1, 1, b). Dividing

Thus 0 ≤ D(1, 1, b) − D∗ (1, 1, b) ≤ 4ϵ for every sufficiently large rational b, and for real
b by continuity. Sending b to infinity and then ϵ to zero gives contact at infinity on this
fixed tripod. Proposition 8.1 now gives D(1, 1, 1) = 3 − 1/3 = 8/3. The three-wire price

9     Polynomial multiplication near contact
The tripod principle compares the price D of a common label at the three terminals with
the lower bound D∗ (a) = i ai − ( i a−1  i )
                                             −1 for their arm lengths a > 0. We call equality
                           P         P
                                                                       i
contact and write ∆ = D − D∗ (a) for the defect. To propagate contact to other triples of arm
lengths, we need to know how a price changes when just one input width varies. Polynomial
multiplication supplies a tensor for which we can measure this change. Its price as one input
grows has a limiting slope and intercept after exponentiation. Proposition 9.2 determines the
intercepts to within O(∆). Section 10 then compares different placements, and Section 11
uses the comparison to propagate equality.
    Throughout these three sections the field is algebraically closed and prices use natural
logarithms with the distances held fixed. A label with q values shared at the terminals costs
D log q. On an embedded tripod, including its origin gives the same price. A duplicate at
either of two terminals is free, and convexity bounds its cost at every point on the geodesic
between them.

9.1   The continuum profile
Place a finite nonempty set of inputs and one output in a spectral space. Write H for price
and D > 0 for the common-label rate of the placement. For integer lengths Li ≥ 1, let M (L)
multiply univariate polynomials of degrees less than Li , retaining all output coefficients. The
following normalization also fixes the constant in the energy formula below.
The matrix multiplication exponent                                                           34


Lemma 9.1. There is a continuous coordinatewise nondecreasing function P on the positive
orthant such that, if Li /N → ti > 0, then

              H(M (L)) = D log N + P (t) + o(1),        P (ct) = P (t) + D log c.

For polynomials in m variables with total input degrees at most ⌊N ti ⌋, the corresponding
price is mD log N + mP (t) − D log(m!) + o(1) for each fixed m.

Proof. Evaluation and interpolation bound H(M (L)) by D log( i Li ). In the other direction,
                                                                 P

M (kL) degenerates to k direct copies of M (L). Indeed, divide [0, 1] into k equal intervals
and choose a continuous convex piecewise affine function with a strict change of slope at
each division. An input monomial of degree di has normalized position (di + 1/2)/(kLi ).
Give it weight kLi times the function there. Give the output the opposite weight at the
weighted mean of those positions, with coefficient k i Li . The output weight depends only
                                                      P

on the sum of degrees. Jensen’s inequality retains precisely the terms whose inputs lie in one
interval. Different intervals use disjoint input and output coordinates, and each surviving
block is a translated copy of M (L). The weight equalities define a rational linear subspace.
Rational approximation within it preserves the finitely many strict inequalities, and scaling
gives integral weights. Rescaling the coordinates and using closedness of price sublevels
therefore gives the asserted degeneration.
    For Li = ⌊N ti ⌋ the normalized cost at N therefore dominates that at any fixed sufficiently
large M , up to D log(M ⌊N/M ⌋/N ). Taking first the lower limit in N and then the upper
limit in M proves convergence. Changing the unit of N gives log homogeneity, first with
floors by squeezing the scales and then for real N . Coordinate monotonicity and squeezing
between (1 ± ϵ)t give continuity and allow o(N ) errors in the lengths.
    For the assertion about total degree, divide the exponent vector at input i by N ti and at
the output by N i ti . All inputs now range over lattice points in the unit simplex. To cut
                  P

this domain by a hyperplane, first discard input points on the plane. Apply the same convex
weighting as above, using the positive part of the defining affine form and coefficients N ti .
Jensen’s inequality retains terms whose inputs are on the same side. Their outputs are on
that side as well, so the two blocks form a direct sum. One may repeat the cut within each
block.
    On a translated axis-parallel cube of side s, multiplication factors over the m coordinates.
The univariate limit shows that its exponentiated price, divided by N m exp(mP (t)/D), tends
to its volume sm . Translation just relabels exponents and rounding a cube side changes
widths by O(1). Packing the simplex with interior grid cubes thus bounds its normalized
price from below by 1/m!, since exp(H/D) adds on direct sums. For the upper bound, start
with an enclosing cube and cut off a slightly enlarged simplex, so all input points of the
desired tensor survive in one cell. Pack the complementary cells with interior grid cubes. The
enclosing tensor dominates the direct sum of the desired tensor and all packed cubes. Refining
the grid and shrinking the enlargement accounts for the whole complementary volume and
gives the matching upper bound 1/m!. Taking logarithms proves the formula.

We also need a lower bound for a sum whose block label is visible at only two of three sites.

writing Ent(q) = − b qb log qb , we have

                                        b

To see the entropy term, take a rational q and a power n for which each nqb is integral. At Y
and Z, retain the M = n!/ b (nqb )! words of type q. Each word has price n b qb H(Tb ). The
                            Q                                                   P

shared-leg separation of [41, Proposition 3.1], in the interpolation form of [39, Proposition 2.1],
degenerates 2M − 1 direct copies of the retained tensor to the full direct sum of the M word
blocks, each tensored with an M -dimensional wire on Y, Z. Interpolation uses 2M − 1 distinct

              nH(T ) + D log(2M − 1) ≥ n              qb H(Tb ) + (D + d(Y, Z)) log M,

where D is the common-label rate of the three sites. Now log((2M − 1)/M ) ≤ log 2 and

9.3    Slopes and intercepts on a triangle
Use i, j, k for the three sites in any order and suppose their arms ai are positive. Put
di = d(j, k) = aj + ak , D = δ({i, j, k}), and write Pk (x, y) for the profile with output k and
input widths x at i and y at j. We identify each argument by its site. For a fixed first input
define
                              Fik (t) = exp Pk (ti = 1, tj = t)/di .
                                                                  

The determinant filtration and the shifted three-block degeneration for polynomial multipli-
cation [41, proofs of Lemmas 4.1 and 4.2] give

                      Fik is concave,        Fik (3t + 1) ≥ 2Fik (t) + Fij (t).

In the first degeneration a two-dimensional wire on j, k supplies two blocks, with the length
at j increased and decreased by one. The blocks are direct on that pair. Optimizing the
pair-label inequality over the block probabilities adds their exponentiated prices at scale di .
This gives midpoint concavity on the lattice of lengths, hence concavity in the limit. In the
second degeneration the longer length is 3Lj + Li − 1, and one of the three blocks exchanges
j and k. The same pair-label inequality and Lemma 9.1 give the second displayed assertion.
Both degenerations work in every characteristic. For the first, injectivity of multiplication by
the determinant and a dimension count give the degree-one Clebsch–Gordan determinant
sequence (see [34]). Lifting monomials to its filtration suffices. The second uses a monomial
grading, and reversal of the order on a leg identifies the middle block.
     Positive increasing concave functions have finite limiting slopes at infinity. Dividing
the three-block inequalities by t shows that the slopes of Fik and Fij are equal. For
S = Fik + Fij we have S(3t + 1) ≥ 3S(t). Iterating along tn = 3n (t + 1/2) − 1/2 gives
S(t)/(t+1/2) ≤ limn→∞ S(tn )/(tn +1/2), twice the common slope. In particular the common
slope is positive. Write it as exp(ci /di ). For either function the excess over the line through
zero of that slope is nonnegative and increasing. The bound on S therefore gives numbers
βik , βij ≥ 0, with βik + βij ≤ 1, such that

                                   t ≤ e−ci /di Fik (t) ≤ t + βik .
The matrix multiplication exponent                                                               36


Here βik is the limiting excess after division by the slope. In particular Pk is di -Lipschitz as
a function of log y with x fixed, since every one-sided derivative of Fik lies between zero and
Fik (t)/t.

remainder by Oη (∆). Each row sum thus lies between −Oη (∆) and zero. Each column sum
is at least −Oη (∆) by the displayed inequality. Since the columns have the same total as the
rows, each column sum is also at most Oη (∆).
    Comparing the two envelopes for Pk (ui , uj ) now gives
                    ci + bi log ui + ui zik = cj + bj log uj + uj zjk + Oη (∆).
Here the logarithmic Taylor remainders are controlled by zik   2 /d and z 2 /d . The row and
                                                                   i       jk j
column estimates show that, up to Oη (∆), the entries (z12 , z13 , z23 , z21 , z31 , z32 ) alternate
between z12 and −z12 . Sum the three comparisons of envelopes for (i, j) = (1, 2), (2, 3), (3, 1).
The terms ci + bi log ui cancel, leaving 2(u1 + u2 + u3 )z12 = Oη (∆). This proves (9.3). The
gap Eik depends only on y/x. The difference between t + βik and e−ci /di Fik (t) decreases with
t, and the latter function increases. Their log ratio hence decreases, which proves (9.4).

    For example, at contact on the unit tripod the predicted intercept is βik     ∗ = 1/2 and

ui = uj = 2/3. Thus e−ci /di Fik (t) = t + 1/2 for t ≥ 1. More generally, (9.3) remains uniform
as a single arm tends to infinity while the other two stay in a compact subset of (0, ∞). If an
arm has length zero, D ≥ D∗ (a) follows from the diameter bound.

10     Energy and radial contraction
Fix an output and a nonempty finite set A of inputs with common-label rate D > 0 on all
the terminals. For costs ℓi > 0 per unit of input width, define the energy

                                 QA (ℓ) = D + sup P (t) − ℓ · t .                             (10.1)
                                                                 
                                                ti >0

It is finite by the evaluation bound, convex in ℓ, and satisfies QA (cℓ) = QA (ℓ) − D log c by
log homogeneity of P . A common surcharge s penalizes a candidate width vector by s i ti .
                                                                                         P

Hence the slope of the optimized energy along a common cost increase measures total width.
We will use the finite difference QA (ℓ) − QA (ℓ + s1). Lemma 10.3 shows that this drop can
only decrease when inputs move towards the output and their respective costs decrease in
proportion. We prove the comparison first for homogeneous degrees, where we can move
an input between two polynomial multiplications, and then recover QA by rescaling those
degrees.
    To compare different placements, first charge a cost fi > 0 per degree of a homogeneous
polynomial. Write Mm    hom (r) for multiplication of homogeneous polynomials in m variables,

with input degrees r = (ri )i∈A . Define
                                                   hom (r)) −
                                                H(Mm
                                                                  P
                                                                     i fi ri
                         BA (f ) =    sup                                      .
                                   m≥1, ri ∈Z≥0         m

This is finite. Evaluation and interpolation at a basis of evaluation functionals on the output
give rank at most its dimension. With R = i ri and q = exp(−        mini fi /D), the exponential
                                              P

of the cost-subtracted price at scale D is bounded by m−1+R          (1−q)−m . The supremum
                                                                R

is unchanged if m is required to be a multiple of a prescribed integer. Indeed, using disjoint
groups of variables recovers any power of the original tensor as a restriction of multiplication
with the combined degrees.

Lemma 10.1 (Adding an input). If ∅ ̸= A′ ⊆ A and s > 0, then

                        BA (f ) − BA (f + s1) ≥ BA′ (f ) − BA′ (f + s1),                      (10.2)

with costs restricted to the indicated input set. If all sites in a restricted placement coincide,
its energy is zero.

Proof. It suffices to add a single input e. Rewrite the claim with BA′ (f ) + BA (f + s1) on the
right, and choose two multiplications approaching those suprema. They can use the same
number m of variables by taking powers in separate groups. Name their variables respectively
u = (u1 , . . . , um ) and v = (v1 , . . . , vm ). At each common input the tensor product of the two
multiplications accepts a bihomogeneous polynomial in (u, v) of prescribed bidegree. At e it
accepts a polynomial h(v) of degree q. Write (U, V ) for the total bidegree, including q.
    We realize this tensor product by a sum of multiplications in which e belongs instead to
the u group. Rename h(v) to h(u) and substitute u − v for u at the common inputs. Multiply,
substitute u + v for u on the output, and project onto bidegree (U, V ). The substitutions
on the common inputs cancel, and the only term of h(u + v) that survives the projection
is h(v). This identity uses no division. Expand the intermediate inputs into bidegrees
before multiplying. If a total of k degrees from the common inputs move from u to v, the
The matrix multiplication exponent                                                                 38


intermediate v-degree is V − q + k. The final substitution can only increase this degree, so
a surviving sector has k ≤ q. The total degree at each input is preserved. Thus the base
cost of every sector is unchanged and its surcharge on v is at most sV . Each sector factors
through a multiplication on u with all inputs and one on v with inputs A′ , with local pre-
and postprocessing.
    Apply this construction to powers in nm variables per group with all degrees multiplied
by n. There are polynomially many bidegree sectors as functions of n, since A is fixed. The
price bound for an ordinary sum costs only O(log n) beyond the largest sector price. After
division by nm and passage to the limit, the right side is bounded by BA (f ) + BA′ (f + s1),
as claimed.

10.1   From degrees to widths
Charging ℓi /N per degree makes degrees of order N correspond to widths of order one. The
factor 1/m! for total-degree polynomials in Lemma 9.1 accounts for the additive D in (10.1).
Lemma 10.2 (Continuum limit of the degree energy). At a placement with common-label
rate D > 0, one has

                    BA (ℓ/N ) = D log N + QA (ℓ) + o(1)      (N −→ ∞).                          (10.3)

Proof. Write Sm (r) for polynomial multiplication with total degree at most ri on each input.
Its price minus degree costs, divided by m, is a lower bound on BA . Indeed, n copies on
disjoint variable groups restrict from homogeneous multiplication with nm + 1 variables and
degrees nri . Use the last variable to homogenize and set it to one on the output. Hence
BA (f ) ≥ n(H(Sm (r)) − f · r)/(nm + 1), and we let n → ∞. For ri = ⌊N mti ⌋, Lemma 9.1
now gives

           lim inf (BA (ℓ/N ) − D log N ) ≥ P (t) − ℓ · t + D log m − m−1 log(m!) .
                                                                                            
           N →∞

The last summand tends to D. Taking the supremum over t gives the required lower bound.
    For the upper bound take integer N . In the supremum defining BA we may use Km
variables grouped into K sets of size m. At each input, record the total degree in each
group by its bin [(bi − 1)N, bi N ), bi ≥ 1. Within a fixed bin pattern the multiplication
restricts from the product of Sm (N b) over the groups, and the cost in each group is at
least i ℓi (bi − 1). The price of the sum over patterns is bounded by the logarithm of the
      P

group enlarges this last sum to a Kth power. Dividing its logarithm by Km and taking the
supremum gives

Lemma 10.3 (Radial contraction). Move each input i towards the output along its geodesic,
to a fraction 0 < λi ≤ 1 of its original distance. Write A′ for the resulting placement and
ℓ′i = λi ℓi . For s > 0 one has

                        QA (ℓ) − QA (ℓ + s1) ≥ QA′ (ℓ′ ) − QA′ (ℓ′ + s1)                  (10.4)

when both placements have positive common-label rate.

Proof. Duplicating an input at its own site and cost leaves B unchanged, by local multipli-
cation. An additional input at the output at zero cost also leaves B unchanged, by local
multiplication on the output. The reverse inequalities in both statements use degree zero at
the added input. For each fixed multidegree the price is convex as the added input moves on
a geodesic, and the subtracted cost is affine. Thus an added input at radial fraction λi and
cost at least λi fi leaves B unchanged, first for one addition and then for all of them. This
applies both at f = ℓ/N and after increasing every cost by s/N , since λi ℓi + s ≥ λi (ℓi + s).
Insert the moved inputs at their specified costs and use Lemma 10.1 to delete the original
inputs. The energy drop for the resulting placement is bounded by the drop for the original
one. Let N → ∞ in (10.3). The leading D log N cancels separately at each placement, which
proves (10.4).


11     Propagation of equality on a tripod
Fix three infinite rays forming a metric tripod with origin O. Prices on these rays are
homogeneous under simultaneous radial dilation. Proposition 9.2 gives D(a) ≥ D∗ (a) at
the three tips of lengths a1 , a2 , a3 . We now prove the rigidity assertion of Proposition 8.1.
Equality at a single triple of lengths forces equality at every triple. The same conclusion
follows from equality approached along one arm as its length tends to infinity with the other
two lengths fixed and positive.
    Use the reciprocal lengths
                                            1
               vi = 1/ai ,    h(a) =                ,    ∆(v) = D(a) − D∗ (a).
                                       v1 + v2 + v3
An infinitely long arm now corresponds to a coordinate face. The proof first compares
integrals of the defect along the three coordinate rays out of v. At a zero of ∆ these integrals
agree. Radial homogeneity then forces their common value to vanish.

12     Synchronization of tensor parameters
Theorem 5.1 tests whether one price extension works for an entire family. Its tests allow one
to choose any tensors in the family, multiply them and close their extra legs jointly. For a
family depending on a marked point s, two factors in such a test can therefore have different
The matrix multiplication exponent                                                                44


marked points. The sphere calculation in Section 14 will give estimates when all factors use
the same marked point. We show that under a strict saving for square multiplication this
diagonal test already controls every choice of the points.
   Fix an algebraically closed field F , an ordinary price H of scale D > 0 on base sites S,
and a finite set L of additional sites, called openings. Recall from Equation (5.3) that βH
measures the largest price per copy obtained by taking a tensor power and applying one joint
functional at each opening. For a polynomial tensor family T (s), s = (s1 , . . . , sd ), write
                                    gen
                                   βH   (T (s)) = sup βH (T (c)).
                                                         c∈F d

This is the assisted cost at independent indeterminates for the generic extension of H in
Section 6. Indeed, fix a power and a closure with rational function coefficients. One can
specialize its entries together with the tensor on a common open set where the resulting price
is arbitrarily close to its generic value. Conversely, a fixed closure over F witnessing a strict
lower bound at one point witnesses the same bound on a nonempty open set, by closedness
of price sublevels. In particular the supremum is unchanged on restricting to any dense open
parameter domain.

Theorem 12.1 (Synchronization). Suppose ωF < 9/4. For any polynomial tensor families
U1 , . . . , Ua on F d with common base and openings,
                                    a                             a
                                                     !                       !
                             gen                            gen

                                    O                             O
                            βH
                                    i=1                           i=1

On the left the a parameter vectors vary independently. On the right they are equal. Both
products group their legs at each opening, so a closure on either side may couple all the
factors.

Proof. Choosing equal parameters gives the inequality from right to left. For the reverse
inequality we first take copies of one univariate family U (s). An identically zero factor makes
the assertion immediate, so we may assume that the families under consideration are nonzero.
              gen
Write B = βH      (U (s)). The diagonal cost of a copies is aB by homogeneity under tensor
powers. We first control different parameters lying in a disk above the same residue s, then
recover independent parameters by letting the valuation depth of that disk tend to zero.
Pinning above one residue. Take an uncountable set U of independent indeterminates, and
let s be a further indeterminate. Put

           E = F (U, s),      K0 = Frac(F (U)[tQ ]),                J = {z ∈ K0 : ν(z) > 0}.

The Gauss lift of the generic extension of H to E is a valued price p on K0 (s) (Theorem 6.1).

                                βp (U (s + z)) = βp (U (s)) = B.                               (12.1)

The m copies of L here are distinct. If two parameters coincide, the corresponding minimizing
sites are at distance zero, by uniqueness of pinning.
    Permutation of the indeterminates in U preserves the prices in Equation (12.2) after
relabeling the zj . Translating every zj by w ∈ J replaces s by s + w in a polynomial family
over K0 , so the disk comparison preserves each finite-n expression as well. For an affine test
zj = aj + bj c in Theorem 7.1, the tensor inside βp is polynomial in c. Lemma 6.2 therefore
gives specialization and the weighted submean inequality at the disk and its inner and outer
ends. Subtracting the same constant nmb at every end preserves these inequalities, and
taking the limit transfers them to the pinned prices.
    It remains to check continuity at zero. At any one placement of the openings, U (s + z) and
U (s) both have an entry of valuation zero, because their common reduction U (s) is nonzero.
Each tensor has nonnegative price there. The entries of their difference have valuation at
least ν(z). Applying the entry bound (5.2) to the difference and the sum inequality in both
directions shows that the prices of the two tensors differ by at most
                            η(z) = D log(1 + exp((C − ν(z))/D))
for a constant C depending only on the format and scale. Evaluate V0 at the sites minimizing
Vz . Its price is at most b + M η(z) since the pin penalties are identical at that placement. In
a common minimizing extension for the two lists, the midpoint estimate (5.6) applied to V0
gives
                                 2cD     d(xz,ℓ , x0,ℓ )2 ≤ M η(z).
                                     X

The right side tends to zero as ν(z) → ∞ at fixed M . Distances are bounded by the scale
D. A nonconstant family of pinned sites would now force exact matrix multiplication rank
at least n9/4 for every n, by Theorem 7.1. Lemma 2.4 would then give ωF ≥ 9/4. Thus for
every ℓ, all xz,ℓ coincide.

Indeed, the coefficients of P (v)⊗n sum to P (1)⊗n . There are at most n deg P + 1 coefficients,

Different families and several coordinates. For arbitrary univariate families Ui , apply the
result just proved to U (s) = i Ui (s). With independent s1 , . . . , sa , regroup j U (sj ) as
                               N                                                     N

the product of a tensors i Ui (si+j ), reading indices cyclically. Each of these has the same
                         N

assisted cost at the generic price on F (s1 , . . . , sa ) because that price is invariant under
permutations of the indeterminates. Superadditivity of assisted cost on products follows
by taking separate closures of the factors on a common multiple of their witnessing powers.
Thus a times the independent cost for the Ui is at most the independent cost for a copies of
                                  gen
U , which we have bounded by aβH      (U (s)).
    For several coordinates choose any c1 , . . . , ca ∈ F d . Coordinatewise interpolation gives
a single polynomial curve γ : A1 → Ad through these points at distinct scalar arguments.
The univariate result applied to Ui ◦ γ bounds the assisted cost of this tuple by the common-
parameter cost along γ, hence by the common-parameter cost on F d . Taking the supremum
over tuples completes the proof.

Corollary 12.2 (Simultaneous pricing). Under the hypotheses of Theorem 12.1, let {Ui (s)}i∈I
be a collection of polynomial families with common openings. For every finite list i1 , . . . , im ,

                             min sup b H(U (c)) = sup βH (U (c)).

Proof. Repeating each finite list gives the assumed bound on closed powers and hence on its
synchronized assisted cost. By Theorem 12.1 this bound also holds at independently chosen
parameters. Theorem 5.1 now yields the single price extension.
    For a finite list of rational families, clear scalar denominators on a dense open set where
they are nonzero. An ordinary price is unchanged by nonzero scalar multiples, so the
polynomial result gives the same product tests there. If such a test failed at a regular tuple, a
fixed power and closure would witness a strict violation. Closedness of price sublevels makes
this violation persist on an open neighborhood of the tuple, which meets the dense open set.
The tests now hold throughout the domain, as required by Theorem 5.1.
The matrix multiplication exponent                                                             47


    For the minimax identity use the supremum of the pointwise assisted costs as the bound
for the single family. If the family is nonzero this is finite by the fixed-format bound. Products
at a shared parameter satisfy the tests by homogeneity of βH on powers, giving an extension
with the stated upper bound. Every extension majorizes βH pointwise, giving the reverse
inequality. The zero family has both sides equal to −∞.

    For the sphere tensor, s specifies the additional marked point and the family index specifies
its energies. Corollary 12.2 places its leg once for all regular positions and all energies. Each
collision with an original marked point can then measure a distance from this same site.


13     Fermionic sphere tensors
We need a tensor whose legs can be combined when their positions coincide. Start with
marked points z1 , . . . , zk in an affine coordinate on the projective line. At each point the
input space has a half-integral energy hi . The energies fix the dimensions of the legs, while
the marked points determine the entries of the tensor. The metric sites at which we will
price these legs can be chosen independently of the zi .
    Two features of this construction enter the proof. When a cluster of marked points
collides, the Laurent coefficient of a given order passes through a single leg, whose energy
the order determines when the other sites stay fixed (Proposition 13.1). Conversely, at a
two-point collision, varying the first input across a bounded range of energies realizes every
linear map from the second input to the fused leg. Lemma 13.3 gives that energy bound.
Together these facts let Section 14 compare the price of a sphere tensor with the price of
moving an independent wire.
    The half-integral grading comes from the free fermion [31, Section 4]. Khoruzhii used its
modes to decompose a crossing of two wires [32]. The crossing argument of [42, Theorem 4.1]
uses the same calculus in arbitrary characteristic.

13.1    The kernel and its coefficients
Let F be a field and take the exterior algebra on generators ψm , m = 0, 1, . . .. Give ψm
energy m + 12 , and denote by Vh the subspace of energy h ∈ 12 Z≥0 . Its basis consists of the
products ψA = m∈A ψm , in increasing order, with m∈A (m + 12 ) = h. In particular V0 = F .
                 V                                       P

Every such product has exterior degree congruent to 2h modulo two.
    For distinct z1 , . . . , zk in F we define a multilinear form C(h; z) on i Vhi . For basis
                                                                             Q

inputs, list all generators in site order. The value is a signed sum over perfect matchings
of this list (Wick’s rule [58]). Its factors are as follows. Two generators at one site have
contraction zero. At different sites, the contraction of ψm at zi with ψn at zj , in that order,
is
                                              1            (−1)n m+n
                                                                      
                                [x y ]
                                  m n
                                                       =            m
                                                                         .                (13.1)
                                       zj + y − zi − x   (zj − zi )m+n+1
Pair the entries of each oriented matching consecutively, multiply their contractions, and
insert the sign of the permutation from the original list to this paired list. Reversing a pair
reverses both the contraction and the permutation sign. Changing the order of pairs preserves
the sign. This sum is the Pfaffian of the alternating matrix of contractions. The empty list
has value one and an odd list has value zero.
The matrix multiplication exponent                                                                        48


    For example, put one generator ψ0 at each of four sites and write Kij = (zj − zi )−1 . The
value on these four inputs is

                                   K12 K34 − K13 K24 + K14 K23 .

At a collision of z1 with z2 the first matching has an internal pair and a pole, while the other
two matchings connect the cluster to the outside. This distinction governs the general fusion
expansion.
    These formulas use integer coefficients and commute with change of field. Two repeated
generators at a site give canceling pairs of matchings, by exchanging their partners. Thus
the forms descend to exterior powers in characteristic two as well. Interchanging sites i, j in
adjacent positions contributes the sign (−1)(2hi )(2hj ) . Each coefficient, viewed as a rational
function, is homogeneous of degree − i hi in the positions, since a contraction has degree
                                         P

minus the sum of its two energies. Its only possible poles are site collisions. As a function of
zi it is bounded at infinity, and vanishes there when hi > 0.

13.2    Fusing a cluster at a marked point
Partition the sites into consecutive clusters J1 , . . . , Js , reordering the sites first if necessary.
In cluster Jj write zi = pj + uai , where the centers pj are distinct and the offsets within each
cluster are distinct. A singleton with offset zero stays fixed. Its fusion map is the identity at
its original energy and zero at every other energy.

Proposition 13.1 (Fusion). For each cluster J there are maps

Proof. Group the perfect matchings by the pairs they use inside each cluster. Fix one cluster

Wedge the resulting vectors in their original order and keep only energy e. Each translation

If a generator is repeated in one input and either copy is contracted, interchanging the copies
pairs terms of opposite signs. If both are unmatched, their translated wedge vanishes. This
The matrix multiplication exponent                                                             49


cancellation works over every field. Removing pairs and raising mode indices preserve energy
modulo Z.
    Decompose each full matching into its internal edges and its edges between clusters. An
internal contraction at the original positions contributes the stated contraction at the offsets
times u raised to minus the energy of the pair. On an edge between clusters the kernel is
                                                                                   ℓ
regular at u = 0. Taylor expansion in the two offsets uses [xm ](uai + x)ℓ = m        (uai )ℓ−m ,
which gives (13.3) and one factor of u per index increase. For a given output energy e, the
total power from the cluster is therefore e − HJ . Moving the internal pairs of all clusters to
the front contributes only the signs already included in IeJ , since an ordered pair can cross
any earlier cluster without changing sign. What remains is the sign of the matching between
the output wedges. This proves (13.2). At fixed total power the sum of the nonnegative ej is
fixed, so the coefficient is finite.

   For example, fuse two generators ψ0 , ψ0 at offsets 0, 1. Their total energy is 1. The internal
contraction produces the vacuum at energy 0 with coefficient u−1 . If the two generators
remain unmatched, their first nonzero translated wedge is ψ0 ∧ ψ1 , of energy 2 and order u1 .
This second term can pair with generators at other centers.

subset of distinct modes, and its energy is

these subsets gives width asymptotic to c h, where c = π/ 3 and the logarithm is natural.

13.4    The width needed to transmit a leg
Consider a two-site cluster at offsets 0, 1 with incoming energies h, g. Write Ie for its fusion
map to energy e. Fixing the first input turns this map into an operator on the second input.
To recover a wire in Section 14, we need every matrix unit from Vg to Ve . The next lemma
                                                    √    √
constructs them with first-input width at most c( e + g) + O(1) per level.
The matrix multiplication exponent                                                              50


Lemma 13.3 (Fusion width). For e, g ∈ 21 Z≥0 put
                                         √   √       √   √ √
                              H(e, g) = ( e + g)2 + ( e + g)/ 2.

The map
                              Vh −→ Hom(Vg , Ve ),     (vh )h 7−→       Ie (vh , −)
                     M                                              X

                   h≤H(e,g)                                         h

is surjective. In the wedge bases, each matrix unit has an integral preimage.

Proof. Fix mode sets A, B of energies e, g, respectively. We construct an input at the first
point that sends ψB to ψA and annihilates every other basis vector of Vg . If e = g = 0,
                                                        √               √
the first input is the vacuum. Otherwise write w = e and v = g. An input at offset
zero will be a wedge of test vectors. Represent a test m tm ψm by its Laurent polynomial
                                                        P

f (X) = m tm X −m−1 . In a matrix coefficient of Ie , the test has two possibilities. It can
         P

contract with input index b at offset one, with coefficient [y b ]f (1 + y) by (13.1). Or it can
occupy output index ℓ, with coefficient tℓ , because translation at offset zero is the identity.
This follows by expanding the output wedge. Each test must occupy one slot of one of these
two kinds, and different tests use different slots.
    We choose tests that can use their designated slots or slots of strictly larger weight. The
target slots will account for the entire available weight, forcing every test into place. Give
output index ℓ the weight τ = (ℓ + 12 )/w and input index b the weight τ = (b + 12 )/v. A side
of energy zero contains no indices and requires no division. The test for each target slot is
given in the table.
                          target         f (X)                r
                          ℓ ∈ A X −ℓ−1 (1 − X −1 )r ⌊vτ − 12 ⌋ + 1
                          b ∈ B X −r−1 (1 − X −1 )b ⌊wτ − 12 ⌋ + 1.
For an output target the coefficient tℓ is one, and the zero of order r at X = 1 forces every
input slot it can occupy to have larger weight. Its other possible output indices exceed ℓ. For
an input target, f (1 + y) = y b (1 + y)−r−b−1 , so its leading input coefficient is one. All other
possible input slots have larger indices, and the choice of r forces all possible output slots to
have larger weight. These conclusions hold over the integers.
    Form the wedge of all the tests in a fixed order. In every matrix coefficient of the indicated
energies the sum of the weights of all available slots is w + v, which is also the sum of the
target weights. The construction gives each test a slot of at least its target weight. Since the
slots have positive weights, every nonzero term must use every slot and attain equality for
each test. The table admits only the designated slot at that weight, even if an opposite-side
slot has equal weight. In particular every generator of the second input has contracted with a
test. The input and output sets are therefore B and A, and the pairing and wedge assignment
are unique. Thus the desired coefficient is 1 up to sign, which can be absorbed into the
chosen input wedge.
    The largest generator energy in each test of weight τ is at most (w + v)τ + 12 , by the
displayed choice of r. A wedge of s generators has energy at least s−1        m=0 (m + 2 ) = s /2,
                                                                                         1     2
                                                                           P
                       √
so there are at most 2(w + v) tests. Adding their individual bounds shows that every
homogeneous component of the input wedge has energy at most H(e, g). Taking these
components gives the claimed preimage.

along nonzero levels with e + g → ∞. The same lower bound holds immediately for larger H.
Lemma 13.3 attains this leading width in every characteristic.


14     The price of a sphere tensor
How expensive can a tensor be when the dimensions of its legs are prescribed? Prepare the
tensor at a site o, then send each leg to its destination xi along a wire. In the logarithmic
price H, a wire costs its length times the logarithm of its dimension. In particular, for every
nonzero sphere tensor of Section 13,
                                                          k
                         H(C(h; z); x1 , . . . , xk ) ≤         d(o, xi ) log dim Vhi .           (14.1)

The marked points zi determine the tensor’s entries. The sites xi determine its price.
For legs of log dimension at most N ai , the cheapest such center gives an upper bound of
N inf o i ai d(o, xi ). Under the standing hypothesis ωF < 9/4, sphere tensors attain this
       P

bound to leading order (Corollary 14.3).

recorded by the order of a collision. Theorem 14.1 identifies the optimizing widths with
distances from a weighted barycenter, whose weights are set by the penalties.

14.1    Pricing one additional leg
The extension must work for all collisions at once. Synchronization reduces that requirement
to a price bound for products of sphere tensors in which all additional legs have the same
marked position s. A principal part at s = zi then allows all the additional legs to be closed
at the single site xi .

15     Incidence expansion
Let S be a finite set of distinct points in an affine plane, and let a secant be a line containing
at least two of the points. Write L for the secants and ti for the number through point i.
For centered real labels vi , meaning i vi = 0, we have

On expanding the squares, each diagonal term occurs ti times and each ordered pair of
distinct points occurs once. Centering gives the identity. We need a version for points placed
in a spectral metric space, where one can measure how far an entire secant pulls a common
center in a given direction. This version will bound the mean squared length of an incidence
edge in the graphs used for permutation routing (Section 17).

15.2    The sharp local link inequality
Suppose F contains Fq . Take the q 2 points of the affine plane F2q and retain the lines in s
parallel classes, 1 ≤ s ≤ q + 1. A point belongs to s retained lines and each line contains
q points. Place the vertices of the resulting bipartite incidence graph in X. Let e be the
mean squared distance across a uniform edge. Give each side half the total mass, uniformly
distributed over its vertices, and let V be the minimum mean squared distance of this
distribution to a common center.

Corollary 15.2 (Affine incidence links). Under the hypotheses of Theorem 15.1,

                                        e ≥ 2(1 − s−1/2 )V.

The coefficient is optimal in every target containing a nontrivial geodesic segment. Omitting
one parallel class gives s = q and a q-regular graph on 2q 2 vertices.

Proof. Take o to be the barycenter of the point sites alone. Write ri , rL for the distances
from o of a point site xi and a line site yL . The mean squared distances on the two sides are

    To attain it, choose one retained parallel class and assign its q lines real labels of mean

     √

                                                  √
mean product of the two endpoint labels is A/ s. Hence V = A and e = 2(1 − s−1/2 )A.
Translate and rescale the labels into any nontrivial segment of X. Nearest-point projection
onto the segment ensures that the ambient minimum has the same value.

    The bound in terms of A + B keeps the point barycenter fixed. Section 17 uses that form
for affine links, and uses the secant inequality directly for projective links.

Part II
Sorting and table lookup
16     Stable sorting under a strict square saving
A tensor network may have to move a table to the place where it will be used, or bring
together all the queries addressed to one table. Moving a table involves long records, while
gathering its queries requires sorting many short ones. We show that a strict improvement
on the exponent 9/4 would make both operations cheap enough for the final assembly in
Sections 26 and 27.
    Fix the finite field k from Section 2 and let n = 2h . The tagged permutation tensor from
Section 4 allows the destination of every record to be part of the input. Write ji for the
h-bit destination of the w-bit source record xi , and yj for the proposed record at position j.
Then Tag(h, w) is the indicator of the event that the ji are distinct and yji = xi for every
i. Similarly, the stable-sort tensor is the indicator that a proposed output sorts the input
records by a designated key and preserves the input order within each key. Both indicators
include the payload bits in the equality tests.

Theorem 16.1 (Conditional stable sorting). Suppose ωk < 9/4, and let n = 2h .

   1. For some 1 < p < 2, Tag(h, h) has an exact trivalent network over k of size O(nhp ).
      The same bound holds for stable sorting of records of at most ch bits, for every fixed c.

   2. For every ζ > 0 there is a > 1 such that, for every w ≥ ha , both Tag(h, w) and stable
      sorting of w-bit records have exact trivalent networks over k of size Oζ (nwhζ ).

16.1    Evaluating a table by sorting its queries
At a station of arity t, the incident bits give the address of an entry in a table of length
2t . When many copies of the station are evaluated together, only the multiplicity of each
address matters to the product of the selected entries. Sorting the queries recovers these
multiplicities and spreads the cost of the table over the batch.

Lemma 16.2 (Batched table evaluation). Assume the ordinary-width stable-sort bound of
Theorem 16.1 over k, with exponent p > 1. Let a network of binary wires and K-valued tables
represent a tensor T , where K/k is any field extension. If tv ≤ u is the arity of station v
                              u
and s = v (1 + tv ), then T ⊗2 has an exact trivalent K-network of size
        P


                                        O 2u s(1 + u)p−1 .
                                                           


The constant is independent of K and of the entries in the tables.

Proof. First consider one table f : {0, 1}t → K, with t ≥ 1, and expose the 2t query addresses
x1 , . . . , x2t as tensor legs. If cz is the number of occurrences of address z, the required product
is z f (z)cz , with an empty product at an unused address. Create one dictionary row for
   Q

each z and guess its count in t + 1 bits. On count bit j, for 0 ≤ j ≤ t, place a unary factor
with entries
                                                           j
                                                 (1, f (z)2 ).
The matrix multiplication exponent                                                            60


The position of this factor already determines z. Thus its two entries are fixed coefficients of
the network.
    Give each query its own row, with zeros in the count field. Sort the 2t+1 rows by address
and then by row type, so that the dictionary row follows all queries to that address. A
counter, initially zero, increments on every query. On a dictionary row it must agree with
the carried count and then resets to zero. This checks the count even at addresses with no
queries. For fixed exposed addresses there is exactly one valid choice of counts, sorted rows
and counter states. The contraction therefore equals i f (xi ) in every characteristic, also
                                                        Q

when a table entry vanishes.
    The counter uses O(2t (1 + t)) gates by ripple-carry addition. Sorting the O(t + 1)-bit
rows costs O(2t (1 + t)p ). The sorting network realizes a zero–one tensor over k and hence
realizes the same tensor over K by scalar extension.
    Group the 2u copies of each station of arity t into 2u−t batches of 2t . A station of arity
zero supplies a scalar directly. Connect the exposed wires according to the original network
                                           u
in each copy. The result contracts to T ⊗2 and its size is bounded by a constant times
