16.2    The two scales of the sorting proof
Assume ωk < 9/4 throughout Part II. We first route wide records. A hop that permutes
records within a smaller address set can be handled recursively. The objective is to shorten
the address by an arbitrarily large constant factor while keeping the total width transported
in each summand within a constant multiple of the input width, independently of that factor.
Repeating such a reduction gives the wide-record estimate in Theorem 16.1. Once that
estimate is available, two levels of radix sorting give the ordinary-width estimate. A short
key selects a digit within a block, and whole packs of records with the same digit then travel
together.
    Section 17 uses the incidence inequalities of Part I to compare the price of a target
permutation with the prices of hops on smaller sets of addresses. The comparison first holds
for an action on a CAT(0) space. Local price data sufficient to realize such an action give the
same bound. If the data fail a local consistency test, spectral comparison instead supplies a
tensor identity that reduces the cost. Section 18 combines these two conclusions to route
payloads and to bound the number of recursive calls in a fixed network.
    Throughout Parts II–IV we use base-two logarithms for ordinary prices. In these units
the distance d is the price of carrying one bit. On a fixed finite list of sites, price data with
bounded scale, including scale zero, form a compact set by (5.2) and Section 3. A bound
on diameter suffices here. Equality along a spanning tree of binary wires realizes the full
diagonal and bounds its price.
    Distances between sites obtained by finitely many geodesic interpolations at fixed fractions
depend continuously on the original prices. The enlarged configurations still have bounded
scale. Along any convergent subnet the interpolation equalities pass to the limit, where
Lemma 5.2 determines the distances from the original data.
The matrix multiplication exponent                                                             61


17     From incidence to permutation actions
A permutation can be factored into hops that change only a short part of the address. Adding
their prices gives a bound proportional to the number of factors. Sorting with wide records
requires a bound independent of that number. We first obtain such a bound on displacement
in a metric space. We then show that finite tests on the addresses suffice to bound the mean
price of the payload wires.

17.1    Two incidence inequalities
Fix a power q ≥ 100 of the characteristic of k. In a placement of a bipartite incidence graph,
a vertex on either side is assigned a site. The incidence energy e is the mean squared distance
between sites at the ends of a uniform incidence.

Lemma 17.1. Suppose ωk < 9/4. In any complete CAT(0) spectral space over k, consider a
placement of either of the following graphs.

  1. The points and lines of the projective space PG(ℓ − 1, q), where ℓ ≥ 3.

  2. The points of F2Q and the affine lines in the q + 1 directions defined over Fq , where
     Q = qR .

Let o be the barycenter of the point sites. Let A and B be the mean squared distances to o on
the point and line sides, respectively, with uniform weights on each side. Then

                                    e ≥ (1 − q −1/2 )(A + B).

Proof. The field k̄ contains every FQ in the statement and satisfies ωk̄ ≤ ωk . To apply
Theorem 15.1 there, recall that scalar extension preserves the possible joint spectral evaluations
on any finite placement of k-tensors (Section 3). In particular, each finite subset of our
metric space has an isometric realization in a complete spectral space over k̄. Take a pointed
ultralimit of these realizations over the finite subsets containing a fixed basepoint, using an
ultrafilter containing every tail under inclusion. Each original site is eventually present with
all its prescribed distances. Completeness and uniqueness of geodesics make the resulting
isometric image closed and convex. For a bounded finite distribution in the ultralimit, the
barycentric variance inequality identifies its barycenter with the limit of the coordinate
barycenters. For a distribution in the image, nearest-point projection places its barycenter in
the image as well. It therefore suffices to prove the inequalities on placements over k̄.
    For the affine plane, apply the unit-mass estimate in the proof of Corollary 15.2 to the
configuration F2Q ⊂ k̄ 2 , retaining q +1 directions. It bounds the cross term in squared-distance
comparison by 2 AB/(q + 1), giving the assertion.
                  p

    For the projective space, map k̄ ℓ linearly into k̄ 3 , preserving the ranks of sets of up to
three projective representatives. Such maps form a nonempty open set. Indeed, preserving
each rank is a nonempty open condition and there are only finitely many conditions. In the
resulting projective plane choose an affine chart containing all the images. Preservation of
pairs and triples makes the images distinct and preserves exactly which triples are collinear.
The secants of the affine configuration thus represent the lines of the original projective space.
The matrix multiplication exponent                                                                    62


    There are j lines through each point and s = q + 1 points on each line. Write ri = d(o, xi )
for point sites and rL = d(o, yL ) for line sites. Set

17.2    Permutations on short address sets
Choose an integer ℓ ≥ 3 and reserve m auxiliary coordinates, where m will depend only on q.
For n = 2h put
          logq n
                                                      l             m
       r=        ,        U = Frq ,   N = qr ,     c = n/N ℓ+m ,              I = U ℓ × [c] × U m .
          ℓ+m
Here [c] = {1, . . . , c}. Identify the original n addresses with the first n labels in a fixed
ordering of I and extend the input permutation π by fixed points. The padded address set
satisfies n ≤ |I| < 2n.
    A permutation function takes a height h and a valid input permutation as parameters
and outputs a permutation of I. We require a single deterministic program using nhO(1)
operations on words of O(h) bits, with the usual word operations of polynomial bit complexity.
These functions form a countable group G under pointwise composition. An explicitly listed
permutation has a linear-time inverse, and π itself is an element of G.
    Identify U ℓ with Fℓq ⊗ U . For a fixed subspace V ≤ Fℓq of dimension at most two, let
KV be the subgroup preserving every fiber of the projection I → (Fℓq /V ) ⊗ U . All auxiliary
labels belong to the fiber. Its size is cN m+dim V , so every hop in KV permutes sets of size at
most 2th+O(1) , where
                                                m+2
                                            t=         .                                  (17.1)
                                                m+ℓ
The additive constant follows from c ≤ q m+ℓ . Increasing ℓ shortens the address within a
fiber.
    The subgroups KV generate G. We give the coordinate factorization of a rearrangeable
network [9] in algorithmic form because the routing protocol will use its factors. Treat
[c] × U m as one coordinate. To eliminate a coordinate of size a, form a bipartite multigraph
on the remaining input and output names, with one edge per requested transfer. Each vertex
has degree a. Give the edges a colors, using each color once at each vertex. In the first stage,
permute the chosen coordinate to its edge color. In the last stage, permute that color to
the destination coordinate. Between these stages the remaining coordinates are permuted
separately for each color. Iterating gives a bounded number of stages for fixed ℓ, m. Each
ordinary coordinate stage is in its coordinate line subgroup, and each auxiliary stage is in K0 .
The matrix multiplication exponent                                                           63


    For completeness, the coloring takes deterministic time linear in the edge count up to
logarithmic factors (see also [21]). An even-regular bipartite multigraph splits into two
regular halves by alternating colors around Euler tours. At an odd degree a we first remove
one perfect matching. To find it, let v be the number of vertices on each side and take the
least H with 2H > av. Multiply each edge multiplicity by ⌊2H /a⌋ and complete the graph to
degree 2H with copies of an arbitrary dummy matching. The total dummy multiplicity is
less than av < 2H . Split into regular halves repeatedly, keeping the half with fewer dummy
copies and breaking ties deterministically. Each split divides even multiplicities equally and
alternates the remaining odd copies around Euler tours, distinguishing dummy from original
edges. After H splits the retained degree-one graph has no dummy copy. Only the original
edges and the dummy matching need be stored, with multiplicities fitting in a word. After
the matching is removed the residual even degree splits in half, so the full coloring recursion
has logarithmically many levels.

17.3    Uniform displacement
A bound obtained by adding displacements along the coordinate factorization would depend
on ℓ. The fixed point in the next lemma bounds the displacement of every product of hops
by a single absolute constant.

Lemma 17.2. Suppose G acts isometrically on a complete CAT(0) space Y in which the
inequalities of Lemma 17.1 hold for all placements. There is an absolute constant C0 such
that, if d(x, gx) ≤ 1 for all g ∈ V KV , then G fixes a point within C0 /2 of x. In particular
                                 S

d(x, πx) ≤ C0 .

Proof. Use a triangle for each flag 0 ⊂ L ⊂ P , where L is a vector line and P a vector plane
in Fℓq . We use the link-energy argument of Garland and its metric fixed-point version [29, 30].
Choose the flag uniformly and, within it, a vertex or edge uniformly. A placement assigns
each V a point yV fixed by KV . For each V we seek another such point zV so that, in a
uniform flag through V ,
                                E d(zV , yW )2 ≤ 34 E d(yW1 , yW2 )2 .                   (17.2)
The vertices W1 , W2 lie opposite V , and W is uniform among them. At V = 0, the opposite
edges are the incidences of projective points L and projective lines P . The barycenter of
the yL is fixed by K0 . Lemma 17.1 bounds its mean squared distance to a neighbor by
1/[2(1 − q −1/2 )] times the opposite-edge energy. At V = L, the barycenter of the yP with
P ⊃ L is fixed by KL . Its variance inequality with test point y0 gives the coefficient 1/2.
The plane vertex. When V = P , the opposite edge joins 0 to a line L ⊂ P . Here y0 and the
yL need not be fixed by KP . We use translates of these sites to build an affine incidence
graph of the same edge energy. The point barycenter of this graph will be nearly fixed by
KP .
    Choose coordinates (x1 , x2 ) ∈ U 2 along P in each fiber modulo P , using a fixed basis
and linear complement. Each coordinate pair names a class of cN m labels. Consider any
other partition of each fiber into classes with these same names and cardinalities, computed
uniformly at all heights and inputs in the time defining G. It is obtained from the coordinate
partition by some g ∈ KP , by sorting and matching equal names within fibers. Explicitly, if
p is the coordinate name, the transported name is p ◦ g −1 . Assign the new partition the site
gy0 . Any other such g differs on the right by an element of K0 , so this site is well defined.
The matrix multiplication exponent                                                            64


Projecting the names modulo L ⊗ U gives a coarser partition to which we assign gyL . The
stabilizer of the reference projected partition is KL . Both assignments commute with the
action of KP . Thus a partition and its projection have site distance exactly d(y0 , yL ).
    Let Q = q R , where R ≤ m will be chosen below. Use R auxiliary U -coordinates as
the values of an Fq -linear map u : FQ → U on a fixed basis of FQ . For a uniform label
in a fiber, u is uniform independently of (x1 , x2 ). For each (a, b) ∈ F2Q form the partition
with name (x1 + u(a), x2 + u(b)). The name is uniform on U 2 . Along an affine line of
direction L defined over Fq , the name modulo L ⊗ U is constant as a function of (a, b),
by linearity of u. We therefore obtain sites for all points and all retained lines. The
incidence energy is eP = EL⊂P d(y0 , yL )2 . At the point barycenter b0 , Lemma 17.1 now gives
(1 − q −1/2 )(A + B) ≤ eP .
A fixed center. For d∗ independent samples from the point-site distribution, form a successive
geodesic mean by moving a fraction 1/i toward sample i at step i. Its final value Md∗ satisfies

                                      E d(Md∗ , b0 )2 ≤ A/d∗ .                            (17.3)

Indeed, condition on the previous mean. Squared-distance convexity and the variance
inequality at b0 bound the new expected squared error by (1 − 1/i)2 times the old error plus
A/i2 .

and set m = R = 4d∗ . For 4d∗ independent uniform samples from FQ , the probability of a

the 2d∗ partitions are independent.

by matching joint names within each fiber. Consequently the distance between the means
of the two halves is the same for all such tuples. Its unconditional expected square is at

17.4    Finite tests on permutation orbits
We now return to the finite set of addresses. In the routing protocol a proposed price is
assigned separately to each payload wire. We will test these prices in balls of bounded radius.
To compare price realizations on overlapping balls we must also test the other tensors on
their common sites.
     Take a finite symmetric set of elements of V KV containing the factors of a fixed
                                                     S

coordinate factorization of π and their inverses. Call these the core generators. For each
input they define an undirected graph on I, with a core link for the hop from a label to its
image under a generator. Its degree is bounded by the size of the set. Each i has a path to
π(i) along the factors, of length bounded by a fixed rational ap independently of h and the
input.
     The four test parameters are the core generators, a ball radius, a maximum number of
sites, and a maximum local dimension. Give each payload wire its own commodity index.
Also index every nonzero k-tensor of permitted format on an ordered list of distinct sites
in a common test ball, using fixed coordinate bases. Assign this index on the whole graph,
so two balls containing the same tensor on the same ordered sites see one price. There are
O(|I|) indices because each ball has bounded size and the field is finite.
     Assign each commodity a nonnegative price. Cap a payload price at ap . For each other
format choose a uniform rational cap sufficient to create the tensor at one site and transport
its legs in binary coordinates along core links in the ball. On a test ball compare the assigned
prices of all its commodities with an ordinary spectral price on its sites. The error is the
largest absolute discrepancy on a commodity or positive excess above unit cost on a core
link with both endpoints in the ball. Duplicate commodities are compared separately with
the spectral price of the same tensor. The minimum error over ordinary prices, allowing the
collapsed price, is the discrepancy of the test. This minimum is attained. Indeed, the core
links connect the ball, so a spanning tree bounds the scale of any price with bounded error,
and prices at bounded scale form a compact set. On a singleton use the collapsed price. A
center passes to tolerance ρ if this minimum is at most ρ.

Lemma 17.3 (Finite detection). For each ℓ there are finite test parameters and ρ > 0 such
that the following holds for all sufficiently large h and every valid permutation input. If all
The matrix multiplication exponent                                                              66


but a fraction ρ of the centers pass to tolerance ρ, the assigned payload prices satisfy

                                    |I|−1         xi,π(i) ≤ C0 + 1.
                                            X

                                            i∈I

Here C0 is the absolute constant of Lemma 17.2.
Proof. If the assertion fails, choose a sequence of counterexamples with h → ∞. Enlarge the
finite symmetric sets of core generators to exhaust the countable union of the KV , and let
the radii and format bounds tend to infinity. Take the tolerance and permitted exceptional
fraction tending to zero, keeping the original factorization of π and the payload cap fixed.
We use these counterexamples to construct an action in which each hop has displacement at
most one and the target permutation has displacement at least C0 + 1.
The marked sites. At each passing center i take a complete CAT(0) spectral realization of a
price attaining the test. At every other center use the one-point space. For g ∈ G mark the
site g −1 i in the chart at i. A fixed word in subgroup elements represents g −1 , so eventually
these hops are tested and the ball contains the whole path from i to g −1 i. Thus the distance
from the center to this mark is uniformly bounded for each fixed g. Use the center itself
when the mark is unavailable.
    At each index take the metric product over all centers with the root mean square distance,
pointed at the vector of centers. Take a pointed ultralimit of the products and let Y be the
closed convex hull of the resulting marks. The product and ultralimit are complete CAT(0)
spaces, as one sees from the midpoint inequality. Both incidence bounds of Lemma 17.1 hold
in Y . A product barycenter is coordinatewise, so the squared inequalities average over the
coordinates. In the ultralimit the variance inequality identifies the limit of barycenters with
the barycenter of a bounded finite placement. Finally, projection onto a closed convex subset
preserves the barycenter of a distribution in that subset.
Translation of the marks. We claim that left multiplication g 7→ ug extends to an isometry of
Y . A point of a dense subset of Y is given by a finite expression of geodesic interpolations
of marks. Fix two such expressions. At center i the translated expressions have the same
underlying address labels as the original expressions at center u−1 i, since (ug)−1 i = g −1 u−1 i.
    For sufficiently large indices, both balls contain these labels. Both centers pass outside a
fraction tending to zero, since i 7→ u−1 i preserves uniform measure. For every fixed tensor
on the distinct labels the two realizations then differ by at most twice the tolerance once the
format bounds include that tensor. Their evaluations use its common commodity index. It
follows that the distances between the two expressions in the two charts differ by a quantity
tending uniformly to zero on such pairs. Indeed, a violating sequence has a subsequence with
a fixed pattern of coincident labels. The finitely many factor words bound the diameter of the
marked list in both charts, so the tree estimate bounds their price scales. By compactness take
limiting prices on the list. They agree on every tensor by the format exhaustion. Continuity
of distances between geodesic expressions, from Lemma 5.2, gives equal limiting distances, a
contradiction.
    All expression distances are bounded even on exceptional pairs. In a passing chart the
fixed word lengths bound all the marks, and a failed chart has collapsed to a point. The
exceptional pairs therefore make a vanishing contribution to the squared mean. Reindexing by
i 7→ u−1 i proves the claimed equality of distances in the limit. The assignment on expressions
thus extends to Y . Its inverse comes from u−1 , and the group law holds first on expressions
and then by continuity on Y .
The matrix multiplication exponent                                                           67


   Let x be the identity mark. For each individual g ∈ KV its hop and inverse eventually
belong to the tested set, so d(x, gx) ≤ 1. The mark π −1 x at center i corresponds to the
address π(i). In a passing chart its distance from the center agrees up to the tolerance
with the assigned price of the payload wire i → π(i). In a failed chart the distance is zero
while the price is at most ap . Thus d(x, π −1 x) is the ultralimit of the root mean square
payload price. This is at least the limiting lower bound C0 + 1 on the arithmetic means of
the counterexamples. Since d(x, π −1 x) = d(x, πx), this contradicts Lemma 17.2.


18     Bounded-width permutation routing
18.1    From a failed price test to a routing identity
Fix the core generators and the test parameters of Lemma 17.3. Write M = |I|, so that
n ≤ M < 2n. Sending a bit along the designated path from i to π(i) pays for every hop
on that path. We seek an exact sum of networks in which the average transport cost per
payload bit has an absolute bound, even as the parameter ℓ increases.

Proposition 18.1. Suppose ωk < 9/4 and fix H ≥ 1. For all sufficiently large h, every valid
set of tags and every h ≤ w ≤ hH , the associated permutation tensor for w-bit payloads is an
exact sum over k of networks with the following properties.

   1. Each label has a bounded number of fixed slots per core generator. A used slot carries
      O(w) bits to a neighbor along the generator or its inverse. Both ends belong to the
      same prescribed group of at most 2th+O(1) labels. The sum of the carried widths in any
      summand is at most Cnw, where C is independent of ℓ and t.

   2. At each label a fixed trivalent network of size O(w(1 + log w)O(1) ) implements the local
      maps. Its configuration consists of a bounded list of menu indices, widths and offsets.

   3. A summand is specified by O(n log(2w)) bits. Given these bits and the tags, a deter-
      ministic controller computes the local configurations and matchings in nhO(1) word
      operations. The exponents and slot capacities are independent of H. The running time
      may have a multiplicative constant depending on H.

Proof. We use the local replacement argument of [42, Section 9]. A request for a commodity
leaves that tensor to be supplied at specified legs. Its cap ai bounds the cost of supplying it
directly, by preparing the tensor at one site and sending its legs along designated core paths.
We encode each leg in binary and pad its unused coordinates by zero. Multilinearity permits
us to substitute a tensor identity for several requests at any stage of a contraction.
The identity on a ball. Let x ∈ i [0, ai ] be a price array with positive discrepancy on a ball.
                                Q

Request one copy of each commodity with xi > 0 on the ball. Their tensor product D has
assigned total price L = i:xi >0 xi > 0, since the zero array passes the test. The available
                            P

supplies Tj are all the commodities on the ball, at their assigned prices, and its binary core
links, at price one. On a singleton we can prepare every demand locally. On a larger ball
there are prices lj strictly above the supply prices and a number 0 < L− < L such that every
normalized spectral log price P satisfies
                                                     P (Tj )
                                    P (D) < L− max           .
                                                 j     lj
The matrix multiplication exponent                                                            68


Indeed a spanning tree of binary core links supplies the full binary diagonal, of normalized
log price one. Thus the maximum ratio is at least the reciprocal of the sum of the lj over the
tree. If the strict inequality with L failed for perturbations decreasing to the original supply
prices, rescale each violating P by dividing by its maximum ratio. The tree bound keeps the
scales bounded. A limit price then satisfies every supply bound and gives D price at least L.
Additivity on products forces equality at each positive-priced commodity, and the remaining
commodities have price zero. This limit would pass the test on the ball. For a fixed successful
perturbation, compactness permits the decrease from L to L− with a uniform strict margin.
    Choose an integer b0 and form the direct sum
                                            M ⊗⌊b0 L− /lj ⌋
                                     U=          Tj           .
                                             j

Rounding costs a bounded amount in log price. For sufficiently large b0 , every normalized
spectral price gives U a higher value than D⊗b0 . Strict spectral comparison in Section 3 now
gives an exact restriction of D⊗b0 r from at most K(b0 ) direct copies of U ⊗r for every r ≥ 1.
Expand the direct sums and apply the restricting maps. The resulting ordinary sum supplies
b = b0 r copies of every demand. Each term uses supplies of total price at most bL− . Its
index specifies a direct copy and an ordered word of r supply choices. With s supply types
this takes at most ⌈log2 (K(b0 )sr )⌉ bits. The ratio of this length to b can be made arbitrarily
small by first increasing b0 and then r.
    Repeat the identity J times. Use only the histogram of the J term indices as the outer
selector. This costs O(log(2J)) bits because the alphabet is fixed. Within the corresponding
network a root sums over ordered words, sends each word along the core tree and enforces
its prescribed histogram. The histogram fixes the number of copies of every supply. The
word assigns these copies to the individual applications of the restricting maps. Each ordered
word contributes exactly once with its original coefficient over k. Choose the rate of the term
indices low enough that the tree transmission costs at most Jb(L − L− )/2. The replacement
thus saves a positive amount per block of demands, after paying for its own control bits.
    These demands remain positively priced, and the replacement retains a strict saving,
throughout a neighborhood of the original array. There are finitely many ball types since the
field, radius, degrees and formats are fixed. Compactness of the price arrays of discrepancy
at least ρ/2 gives a finite menu of replacements. On this set, the saving per unit of demand
and the selected demand prices both have uniform positive lower bounds.
Bounding the total width. Follow one summand in the expansion. Let wzi be its integer stock
of outstanding requests for commodity i, and let S be its installed core width divided by
M w. Give the summand the account S + F (z), where

Fix an integer B > maxi µai . Whenever a stock exceeds wB, supply the excess directly. On

    If the mean maximizing price of the M payloads exceeds C0 + 2, Lemma 17.3 gives more
than ρM balls of discrepancy greater than ρ. A greedy packing selects Ω(M ) disjoint such
balls, allowing a fixed slack in the test. Indeed the discrepancy is 1-Lipschitz in the array of
prices. A sufficiently fine rational grid can assign a valid menu entry to each cell meeting the
set of discrepancy at least ρ, with the entry valid throughout the cell. These finite tables
suffice to select and pack eligible balls.
    On each packed ball apply its replacement with J = ⌊αw/b⌋, for a small fixed rational
α > 0. The bound zi ≥ µxi and the positive lower bound on selected demand prices ensure
sufficient stock. Charge each new core link to S, and enter other supplies as new requests. In
the linear bound on the account, each ball saves a fixed positive amount per retired demand
block, including transmission of the word. For large w the packed balls therefore save at
least cα in the normalized account for some c > 0. The finite menu and the disjoint balls
give maxi |vi | = O(α), so the quadratic error is O(α2 ). Fix α small enough that each parallel
round lowers the account by a fixed positive amount in every resulting summand. Supply
any excess over the stock cap after the round.
    Starting from zero, introduce all w copies of each payload in equal increments on a fixed
fine mesh, rounding to integers with exact total w. Before each increment use parallel rounds
until the mean payload price is at most C0 + 2. The account is nonnegative. The increments
have total linear cost at most C0 + 2, and their summed quadratic cost is at most one for
a sufficiently fine mesh and sufficiently large w. Supply excess stock after each increment.
Every summand therefore undergoes only boundedly many rounds, and its final account is at
most C0 + 3. Supply all the remaining requests directly. Taking x = a in the definition of F
gives
                                       1 X
                                             ai zi ≤ F (z) + 1.
                                      M i
The total width is at most (C0 + 4)M w ≤ 2(C0 + 4)nw. This last coefficient is absolute.
Realizing the summands. A label lies in at most one packed ball per round. Every direct
supply that uses a given edge originates within bounded distance of it. Bounded degree,
boundedly many commodity types per neighborhood, and the stock cap thus give boundedly
many slots of width O(w) per generator. The generator prescribes the group containing both
ends of the slot.
    At the sites of a commodity, keep its requests in common order, retiring them from
the front and appending new requests at the back. For each replacement the histogram
determines the lengths of the lists. Inside its network, prefix counts of the ordered word assign
supply positions to repeated applications. Comparison sorting on addresses of O(1 + log w)
bits realizes these permutations and partial matchings with O(w(1 + log w)O(1) ) trivalent
gates at a site. Complete partial matchings deterministically and set idle coordinates to
zero. Each individual block map has fixed size over k and admits a constant-size trivalent
implementation. Only boundedly many local fields select the maps, lengths and offsets.
    The controller enumerates the bounded neighborhoods, computes prices from the stocks,
uses the finite tables to pack eligible balls, and assigns the slots. There are O(n) histograms
over the boundedly many rounds, with a unique encoding of O(log(2w)) bits each. Enforce
valid encodings and zeros in unused selector positions. Only local counts and indices enter
The matrix multiplication exponent                                                             70


the controller, while the ordered words are summed inside the networks. The enumeration
and stock updates take nhO(1) word operations on tags of h bits and counts of O(log(2w))
bits. This proves the three bounds.

18.2    From local transports to stable sorting
Write S(h, w) for the least trivalent size of Tag(h, w) over k, and put n = 2h . Proposition 18.1
bounds the transport used in each summand. To derive a recurrence for S, we must fit the
calls from all summands into a single network. Different summands can use the same call if
their data can reach it at low cost.

Sharing a fixed pool of calls. Fix the dimension ℓ, the address reduction factor t =
(m + 2)/(m + ℓ) and the protocol of Proposition 18.1. In each term the protocol has at most
Ln links of total width at most Cnw. Of these two constants, C is independent of ℓ. Choose
0 < Y ≤ 1 with L ≤ C/Y and split each link into chunks of w′ = ⌈Y w⌉ bits. Pin the padding
of a final chunk to zero at both ends. The number of nonempty chunks in any term is at most

                           Cnw/w′ + Ln ≤ m∗ n,           m∗ = 2C/Y.

For this fixed protocol there are O(n) candidate slots, grouped according to the short-address
sets within which they can communicate. Duplicating a group for the boundedly many link
ends or chunks it handles changes its size only by a constant factor.
    We use the assignment graphs of [42, Lemma 10.2]. Such a graph connects at most
cK candidates to K slots. Every active subset of at most K/2 candidates can be assigned
injectively to adjacent slots. The degrees are bounded in terms of c, and the assignment
takes Oc (K) deterministic word operations with the graph as fixed advice.
    Choose a dyadic capacity U = 2th+O(1) at least twice the size of the largest candidate
group. Pack whole groups greedily into lists of capacity U , closing a list when the next group
would exceed that capacity. Each closed list is at least half full, so there are O(n/U + 1) lists.
Fix a small dyadic fraction K/U . In a list, divide its active chunks in order into batches of
size K/2, allowing a shorter last batch. With K slots reserved for each nonempty batch, the
total reservation is at most

                              2m∗ n + O((n/U + 1)K) ≤ 3m∗ n.

Here U = o(n) and we choose K/U after fixing the packing constants.
    For each list install enough candidate batches to handle every chunk in it. Use one
assignment graph to place the source ends of a batch in K slots and another for the target
ends. A second assignment step handles whole batches. Its targets are a shared pool of
⌈6m∗ n/K⌉ physical modules, each with K input and output positions. At most half the pool
is active, and the number of candidate batches is a bounded multiple of the pool size. Thus
the two lists of each active batch can be moved together to its assigned module. Including
idle modules, at most 7m∗ n positions are installed for all large h.
    Every module uses Tag(h′ , w′ ) with h′ = log2 K = th + O(1). Fill unmatched positions
in order, pin unused bits to zero and give an idle module the identity tags and zero payloads.
The assignments and padding are deterministic once the term and its tags are given. Because
the assignment graphs have bounded degree, the switches cost O(nw) in total, including
those that move two lists of Kw′ bits at a time.
The matrix multiplication exponent                                                                  71


    The remaining control uses only tags, selectors and slot descriptions. It can be checked
by guessing a time-stamped execution, verifying each instruction, and comparison-sorting
all memory transactions by address, time and access order within each instruction to check
every read against the last write. Fixed initial memory and padding make the accepting
execution unique for every legal selector. A conventional comparison network also checks
the original tags for distinctness and rejects invalid permutations. For each fixed H and
h ≤ w ≤ hH , the size outside the shorter calls is
                   Dcontrol (h, w) = OH nw(log(2h))O(1) + nhJ ,           J > 2.                 (18.1)
                                                                   

The first term pays for the local maps with their polynomial overhead in log(2w). The second
pays for nhO(1) deterministic word operations, their bit implementation and the memory
check. The exponents can be chosen independently of H. We have obtained the recurrence
                                                        7m∗ n
                          S(h, w) ≤ Dcontrol (h, w) +         S(h′ , w′ ).
                                                         2h′

Wide payloads. It suffices to prove the estimate for 0 < ζ < 1. Choose ℓ large enough
that 14Ctζ < 1/4, and then fix the resulting Y and J. Shortening an address from h to h′
reduces the allowed overhead hζ by a factor approaching tζ . To account for calls close to
the width at which conventional sorting takes over, we let the overhead exponent decrease
gradually with the width.
   Choose a smooth nonincreasing u : [1, ∞) → [ζ, 2], equal to 2 on [1, J] and eventually
equal to ζ. Take such a long transition interval that b = −u′ satisfies 0 ≤ b < 1 and Y −b < 3/2
everywhere. Fix H in the interior of the constant tail. For h ≥ 2 and h ≤ w ≤ hH we claim
                             S(h, w) ≤ Dnwhu(a) ,           a = logh w.                          (18.2)
If a ≤ J, Batcher’s bitonic sorting network [8] uses O(nh2 ) comparators of bit cost O(h + w)
to put the tagged sources in destination order. Comparing their tags with the prescribed
positions and their payloads with the proposed outputs gives (18.2).
    For a > J induct on h. For large heights h′ < h and w′ ≥ h′ . Writing a′ = logh′ w′ , we
have uniformly on J ≤ a ≤ H,
                                                        ′

The second formula is Taylor expansion on a compact interval. If w′ > (h′ )H , split the
payload evenly into ⌈w′ /⌊(h′ )H ⌋⌉ slices, using the same tags for each. The number of slices
is bounded, their widths sum to w′ , and their width exponents are H + o(1), where u = ζ.
Copying the tags costs O(h′ ) per slot and slice, within (18.1). The same per-slot estimate
therefore applies.

recursive calls use at most the fraction (1 + o(1))14Ctζ Y −b(a) < 1/2 of the claimed bound.
The extra cost (18.1) is o(nwhu(a) ) uniformly, since a ≥ J and u ≥ ζ. Enlarging D to cover
bounded heights closes the induction. For w > hH , split into slices as evenly as possible with
maximum width ⌊hH ⌋. Each width exponent is H + o(1) for large h, and tag copying costs
at most a constant times the total data volume. At bounded heights the bitonic network
costs Oh (w). This proves, for some A > 1,
                              S(h, w) = Oζ (2h whζ )        (w ≥ hA ).                           (18.3)
The matrix multiplication exponent                                                              72


From tagged permutations to stable sorting. For 2s records of v bits, a stable sort
uses one Tag(s, v) and O(2s (s + v)) additional gates. Give each proposed output a guessed
destination in the original input list. Use the tagged permutation to check that this sends
every output back to an equal input. Along the output list check strictly increasing pairs (key,
guessed input position) in lexicographic order. All records with a common key must then be
exactly the corresponding input subsequence. This forces the order and every guessed index,
even for identical records. The stable-sort relation thus inherits the wide-record bound.

Packing ordinary-width records. We use the wide-record bound twice in each radix
pass. First take ζ = 1/2 in (18.3), with threshold a > 1. Then take ζ = 1/(2a) there, with
threshold B > 1. Start with O(n) records whose keys and payloads have O(h) bits. Choose
a radix digit of s = ⌊h1/a ⌋ bits. Run O(h/s) stable passes starting with the least significant
digit, zero-padding the last digit. Address lengths for the two sorts will be about 2s and h
respectively.
    For one pass, pad the list to blocks of 22s slots. Sort each block by a dummy flag and
the digit, with dummies last in their old order. Choose a power of two P ∈ [hB−1 , 2hB−1 ).
Extend each run of a single real digit to a multiple of P , inserting dummies at its end. Each
pack of P consecutive slots now contains records of at most one real digit. When the packs
are sorted stably by digit, equal-digit records retain their order both within a block and
across blocks. Empty packs sort last. Figure 6 shows one pass with two blocks.

                Input block in order       Packs of digit 0      Packs of digit 1
                a1 b0 c1 d1                       [b, ·]            [a, c] [d, ·]
                e0 f1 g0 h0                   [e, g] [h, ·]            [f, ·]

Figure 6. One radix pass with two records per pack. A subscript is the digit being sorted and a dot
is a dummy slot. Stable sorting of whole packs collects the middle column, then the right column,
each from top to bottom. Reading the real records gives b, e, g, h, a, c, d, f .

    Within a block a fixed allowance of O((2s + 1)P ) extra slots covers the padding for all
real runs, retains the old dummies and rounds the total length to a multiple of P . Scans find
the length of each run, round it up, and assign destinations to all old records, including the
old dummies. A second local tagged permutation checks these destinations. Append new
dummies and require their guessed destinations to be strictly increasing. Only the unused
positions are available to them, so there is exactly one valid filling. Additional temporary
padding to a power of two is removed after the call. Scan each pack to obtain its digit before
the global stable sort. Dummies, including any inside a pack, are ignored when reading the
real order.
    The list remains of size O(n) across all passes. In one pass its multiplicative growth is
1 + O(2−s P ), with an additive cost O(22s + 2s P ) for the last block. Over O(h/s) passes the
multiplicative factors have bounded product because P is polynomial in h and s = ⌊h1/a ⌋.
The accumulated additive cost is o(2h ) since s = o(h).
    Each local call has address length 2s + O(1). Padding its records to a sufficiently large
constant times h meets the first width threshold, so it costs O(hs1/2 ) per slot. The global
sort has O(n/P ) packs of O(hP ) bits and address length h + O(log h). Since hP ≥ hB ,
constant-factor padding meets the second threshold and the global cost is O(nh1+1/(2a) ). The
The matrix multiplication exponent                                                           73


scans, copies and order checks fit the same bound. All passes together cost

                            O (h/s) nh1+1/(2a) = O nh 2−1/(2a) .
                                                                


    To realize Tag(h, h), take the union of the n source records and the n proposed target
records. Give each source its claimed destination and each target its prescribed position,
and radix-sort the union by destination, breaking ties by source before target. Scan the
real records, skipping dummies. For each destination in order the scan requires one source
followed by one target with equal payloads. An O(h)-bit register suffices. This scan rejects
repeated or missing source tags and requires no rearrangement of the final dummy slots.
    Thus Tag(h, h) has exponent p = 2 − 1/(2a), with 1 < p < 2. For width ch, split the
payload across boundedly many tagged calls with the same tags, padding when necessary.
The reduction from stable sorting to tagged permutations now yields the ordinary-width
assertion and completes the proof of Theorem 16.1.


Part III
Concentrating arity on spheres
19     Concentrating the arity of a tensor network
Our aim is to gather the large tables of a tensor network at a few stations while preserving
its contraction. The relevant budget is the sum of the squared arities. For example, W/T
stations of arity T have budget W T . Gathering D at a time into stations of arity DT would
multiply that budget by D. Lemma 19.1 keeps the budget of the large stations√       at O(W T )
while reducing their number to O(W/(DT )). Their total arity is then O(W/ D). The
transformation also introduces small tables. Part IV shortens the wires of this small-table
network between successive concentrations, then uses batched table lookup to evaluate the
result.
    A station is a tensor given by an arbitrary table on an ordered list of bits. Its arity
counts the external bits as well as its ends of internal wires. Group the stations into cells of
a host graph of maximum degree g. Wires run within a cell, between adjacent cells, or to
the exterior. The same host may carry several networks. A cell used in two networks counts
twice. A station designated immobile retains its external bits and its cell. All external bits
must belong to such stations.

Lemma 19.1 (Local concentration). Fix a finite field k with ωk < 9/4, a degree bound g,
and an integer ℓ ≥ 1. For each sufficiently large fixed integer D, the following holds for all
sufficiently large integers T . Use an extension K/k containing a fixed finite extension, with
|K| at least a sufficiently large polynomial in T and [K : k] = OD (log T ). The input tables
may already be over K.
    Suppose there are at most W/(DT ) occupied cells and at most W/(DT ) immobiles in
total. In any one network each cell has at most 100D stations of total arity at most 100DT ,
and over all stations of all networks i t2i ≤ W T . The tensor of each network is a sum over
                                       P

K of networks with the same exposed bits. Every choice of one term per network, taken
together, satisfies the following bounds.
The matrix multiplication exponent                                                            74


   1. There are O(W/(DT )) frozen stations, including the holders of the original external
      bits in their original cells. Their maximum√arity is O(DT ), their squared arities sum
      to O(W T ), and their arities sum to O(W/ D).

   2. All other stations, called the core, have arity at most D−ℓ T and total arity O(W (1 +
      log D)2 ).

   3. A common host replaces each original cell by DO(1) positions, with possible contacts
      only within and between neighboring cells. The induced host on the core positions has
      absolute bounded degree. No two frozen stations touch by a wire. For some c = c(g, ℓ, k)
      all active internal bundles can have width at least ⌈T /Dc ⌉, and the bounds in the first
      two conclusions continue to hold when every station is charged at least that arity.

   4. The outer summation uses OD (log(T + 1)) control bits per occupied cell. For its
      computational bound, represent each input table by a label and its ports by ordered
      bundles, one per station pair, specified by lengths and endpoint slots. Bits agree in order
      along a bundle. Any local reordering is part of the supplied table label. Given a fixed
      coloring with OD (1) colors separating cells within a bounded distance depending on
      D, g, the validity of the indices, output layout, and tracking of exposed sublists can be
      determined in OD (1) rounds of communication on the old host. With input labels of at
      most A bits, the work and communication per occupied cell are OD (A + (1 + log T )O(1) )
      bit operations and bits. Each output table has a label of length OD (A + log(T + 1)) in a
      dictionary fixed by the input dictionary and the parameters.

The implicit constants in the first three conclusions can depend on k, g, ℓ, independently of
D, T, W . A fixed change of the numerical input bound 100 changes only these constants.

    The proof uses two sphere forms in each occupied cell. The first joins neighboring cells
along pairs of legs called seams. It represents a station by an insertion at a marked point. The
energy of this insertion bounds the square of the width needed to encode its state. Moving
insertions across seams and merging them at common marks will concentrate their energy at
the frozen stations. The seams have a separate energy budget. A crossing spends some of
this budget, and at the end each seam is divided among many small tables.
    To begin the motion we must realize the actual wires of the input network. The second
sphere, which stays in its cell, serves this purpose. Corollary 14.4 allows its insertions to
encode the prescribed wiring on the first sphere. Both sphere legs participate in mergers,
but only the first can cross a seam. The two spheres use different numbers of species and
different energy scales. These choices make the private leg left behind at a crossing thin.

    At distinct points zi ∈ K, the sphere form Cr sends a tuple of wedges to a Pfaffian. List

The Pfaffian of the empty matrix is one and an odd list gives zero. Exchanging adjacent
homogeneous sites of energies e, d costs the sign (−1)(2e)(2d) . In particular all such signs are
scalar when the energies are fixed. These definitions include characteristic two by reduction
of the integral alternating pairing formula.

need to transpose a fusion coefficient against this ordinary equality pairing.

20     Planting the wiring at marked points
A station in the input may have wires to many other stations. To move it as one insertion,
we encode its ends of those wires in a single leg. The sphere form must then test whether the
bits at opposite ends agree. We implement that test separately in each occupied cell of each
network.
    There are two sphere forms in a cell. The form A carries the station legs and the seams to
neighboring cells. Extra marks on A supply a joint state that tests the wiring. The private
form B realizes this joint state through a separate insertion table at each extra mark. Thus,
after planting, each insertion has just one leg on each sphere, along with any exposed or
control bits. At original stations and seams the B leg is the vacuum. Figure 7 shows the two
kinds of insertions.
                seam                                                               seam
                       Sewn sphere A                                 R species
                                                                zP
                            original station          auxiliary insertion
                                      vacuum                    z
                       Private sphere B                          R/b species

Figure 7. The station table encodes its incident internal wire bits on A. The auxiliary tables couple
A to B and together impose equality along the wires. Only A is sewn to neighboring cells.
The matrix multiplication exponent                                                              77


    A mark at z on B corresponds to the mark at z P on A, where P is a power of the
characteristic. Moving z by t moves z P by tP . In units of the Laurent order on A, the energy
of the pair is therefore eA + P −1 eB . A given energy budget allows P times as much energy
on B. We keep the corresponding leg narrow by using fewer species on B. This will bound
the width left behind when a station crosses a seam.
    Fix g, ℓ as in Lemma 19.1. Let F be the first characteristic power at least D1000(ℓ+1) and
let a = ⌈F 3 ⌉. For a chunk count M and a sufficiently large characteristic power b > F , take

20.1    The wiring form for one species
Combine the wires between each pair of distinct stations into one ordered bundle, absorbing
endpoint permutations into the station tables. Contract self-loops in those tables first. Split
each internal bundle into packets of R bits, padding the last packet with zeros. Species s
will carry bit s of every packet. A station meets Og (D) bundles, and a cell together with its
incident edges meets Og (D2 ). At a station of arity ti , one species therefore carries at most
ti /R + Og (D) bits.
     Choose distinct wedge basis states at an integer energy hi to encode the √ bit
                                                                                  √ strings at
station i. The logarithm of the single-species dimension is asymptotic to (π/ 3) hi through
integer levels by Lemma 13.2. Since R ≥ T /(2a) and a ≥ D, we may take

                                    hi = Og a2 (1 + ti /T )2 .
                                                            


At seam j the packets across the interface contribute Og (DT /R + D2 ) = Og (Da) bits to one
species. An integer energy qj = Og (D2 a2 ) suffices. The two cells use the same bit order and
the same basis codes there. For fixed D the packet counts per species, energies and codes
range over finite sets.
    Define the multilinear form L on these station and seam levels by its coefficients. Give a
basis tuple coefficient one if all codes are valid and both ends of every bit agree. For a wire
to another cell, compare its station end with its seam end. Every other coefficient is zero.
We will sew a multispecies seam at a fixed total energy. To recover the correct energy in each
individual species, put Q = j qj and extend L by zero to all other tuples of nonnegative
                               P

half-integer seam energies of sum at most Q. The station energies stay fixed.
Lemma 20.1 (Auxiliary marks of bounded weight). Inserting a joint state at polynomially
many extra marks on a single-species sphere realizes the extended form L. At each extra mark
the state uses only the vacuum and ψ0 . In every word in its support the number of occupied
marks is even and at most Og (Da).
Proof. A wedge of length d has energy at least 12 + 32 + · · · + (d − 21 ) = d2 /2. At the stations
the total length is Og (a i (1 + ti /T )) = Og (Da). At the at most g seams the same bound
                         P
The matrix multiplication exponent                                                             78


follows from Cauchy–Schwarz and their total energy bound Q = Og (D2 a2 ). List all pairs
(v, m) of original marks and modes occurring in the resulting input spaces. The list has
polynomial size in D, a.
    An insertion of ψ0 at a new point z pairs with those modes by the covector with coordinates
(z − zv )−m−1 . Distinct principal parts give linearly independent rational functions in every
characteristic. Choose distinct regular points whose covectors span the dual mode space.
Indeed, a nonzero linear combination annihilated by the points already chosen is nonzero at
some unused point of the algebraic closure. At most one point per listed mode is needed, all
in a finite extension. Add vacua if necessary to obtain at least three auxiliary marks.
    For a subset of d new marks, insert ψ0 there and the vacuum at the others. Consider first
the part of the resulting Pfaffian in which every auxiliary generator pairs with an original
generator. This part is itself a linear combination of insertion tests on at most d marks.
Indeed, order all auxiliary generators last and group the matchings by their exact set of
internal auxiliary pairs. Each nonempty set contributes its contracted scalar, with a sign
depending only on those paired positions, times the part on the remaining marks with no
internal auxiliary pairs. Subtracting these contributions inductively isolates the desired part.
    Now take a nonzero vector in the even part of the direct sum of input spaces, and let
d be its greatest nonzero total wedge length. A test on d marks with no internal auxiliary
pairs vanishes on every shorter wedge. On the length-d component it evaluates the exterior
product of the chosen d covectors, up to a common sign. The original basis tuples are distinct
basis wedges in the exterior algebra on the list of pairs (v, m). Some choice of d covectors
therefore detects the component. The resulting even tests span the full dual of the even part.
Each actual insertion used has even weight at most d = Og (Da) and vanishes on odd vectors.
The prescribed integer energies give even lengths, so the zero extension of L also vanishes on
the odd part. A linear combination of insertion tests now gives the required joint state.

    Use one copy of this state in each of the R species. At station i the insertion table enforces
level hi in every species, decodes the packets, checks that padded bits are zero and supplies
the original table entry, with its exposed bits. At seam j we sew by basis equality at total
energy Rqj . To see why this recovers the original wiring, group the Pfaffian by species. Order
the original and auxiliary marks in two consecutive lists and reorder each list by species. The
auxiliary reordering sign can be absorbed in the joint state. Interleaving the lists costs no
further sign because every species has even auxiliary weight. The original reordering sign
depends only on the input wedges. In any nonzero product after the auxiliary state has been
contracted, the seam tuple of each species is either the prescribed tuple or has sum greater
than Q, by the zero extension. The sums over all species add to RQ, so every species has the
prescribed tuple. All the original wedges then have even length and the remaining reordering
sign is one. The product tests exactly the equality of the bits along each wire. We also place
one vacuum collector in each cell for the motion step.

for sufficiently large D, since H = Ra2 and a ≥ F 3 . For storage, use the actual spaces of
the selected block. At mark i either leg needs only O(bui + 1) bits, by Equations (20.3)
and (19.1). Over all blocks, each auxiliary insertion needs O(R) = O(T /a) bits for these two
legs. Summed over the marks, this costs Og,ℓ (DT log D), taking b ≥ m to absorb rounding.
    The selector is summed inside the network, but the outer layout must know the energy
and width at each mark. Split the blocks into M equal consecutive chunks. In the outer sum,
specify for each chunk the histogram of its pairs (type, B-energy tuple). A histogram records
the multiplicities of the pairs, while the private selector records their order. The alphabet is
fixed independently of T , so the histograms cost OD,b,M (log(T + 1)) bits per cell. They fix
the energy and the concatenated code length of each leg in each chunk. The table checks the
histograms against the selector and uses its block order to decode. Each valid selector occurs
in exactly one outer term. Thus M can be chosen for the chunk layout, then ϵ for its selector
routing, and finally b for the comparison and selector width, all before T grows.


21     Moving insertions through a sewn surface
The planting construction expresses the original tables on pairs of spheres in each cell. We
now combine these tables at a small number of marks. Two outcomes of a Laurent expansion
suffice. At an insertion mark, a coefficient fuses the moving table with the table already there.
At a seam, a sufficiently negative coefficient transfers the moving table to the neighboring
cell and lowers the energy of the seam. This decrease bounds both the number of crossings
and the amount of energy that any cell can receive.
    In each cell, designate the marks of the original immobile stations and one additional
vacuum mark as collectors. They stay in their cell. Process the original movable stations in
turn, skipping those already absorbed. The moving table keeps its ordinary bit ports. If its
current legs on the sewn sphere A and private sphere B have energies mA , mB , put

                     m = mA + mB /P,         µ = m + H,        L = ⌈2P µ⌉.

Here P is the exchange rate of Equation (20.1). The reserve H stays with this move from its
start until it ends, while m is updated after every fusion or crossing.

21.1    Reconstructing an evaluation from collisions
Fix the branch of the outer sum and, for the following identity, the private selectors. The
branch determines the energies, so the same identity works for all allowed selector values.
Hold all other positions and insertion tables fixed. Each entry of the contraction is a rational
function of the mover’s B-coordinate z. Its A-coordinate is z P . The sphere kernels have poles
only at other marks on the current sphere and are bounded at infinity. Near a mark with
B-coordinate z0 , write s = z − z0 . Since P is a power of the characteristic, the corresponding
coordinate difference on A is sP . Hence an A-fusion of order i and a B-fusion of order v
The matrix multiplication exponent                                                            81


contribute to order j = P i + v of the contraction. At an insertion of weighted energy m0 the
merged energy is
                                    mnew = m0 + m + j/P.                                (21.1)
Indeed each fusion adds its order to the two incident energies, by Proposition 13.1. Nonnega-
tivity of the two output energies bounds i and v below. Thus the pole orders are bounded,
and only finitely many pairs (i, v) contribute at each j.
    To reconstruct the value at the current position, use the following coefficients at the other
marks of the cell.
   • at every insertion, its entire principal part;
   • at the designated vacuum collector, also the orders 0, . . . , gL, even after this collector
     has absorbed a table;
   • at each seam, only the orders j ≤ −L within its pole bound.
A function on which all these coefficients vanish has poles only at the at most g seams, of
order at most L − 1 each. It has a zero of order at least gL + 1 at the designated collector
and no pole at infinity. The degree of the divisor of a nonzero rational function on the
projective line is zero, so this function vanishes identically. On the space with the prescribed
pole bounds, evaluation at the mover’s position is therefore a linear combination of the listed
coefficients. This combination acts entrywise, independently of the tables.
    A coefficient at an insertion is a sum over the compatible pairs (i, v). For each pair, apply
the two fusion maps and contract them with the moving and stationary tables, including the
sign of their chosen order. A collision with a collector ends the move. At any other insertion
only j < 0 is used, so the combined table continues with at most the sum of the two original
weighted energies. This step can absorb an auxiliary insertion, an original station awaiting
its turn, or a private insertion left by an earlier crossing.

21.2    Crossing a seam
Suppose the selected coefficient has order j ≤ −L at a seam of energy e. Only sphere A is
sewn there. The insertion on B at the seam is the vacuum, so the B-expansion has Taylor
orders v ≥ 0. For each such v with i = (j − v)/P integral and in the fusion range, the new
A-energy at the seam satisfies

                             e′ = e + mA + i ≤ e + mA − 2µ ≤ e − µ.                        (21.2)

Terms with e′ < 0 vanish. With i fixed, interpolate the B Taylor coefficient using evaluations
at fresh regular positions. Each resulting term has one private insertion of energy mB left
behind, with vacuum on A. Its index is carried to the original table by an ordinary equality
wire. We call this private insertion the residue of the crossing.
    To identify the new moving table, fix its old A-state x by linearity. Let a, b range over
wedge basis states of energies e, e′ , respectively, and denote the two remaining sphere forms
by Cnear , Cfar , suppressing their spectator arguments. Lemma 19.2 gives the identity

                             Cnear (b)       [Ie′ (a, x)]b Cfar (a)
                        X                X

                         b               a

                             =α               Cnear (b)       [Ie (b, St x)]a Cfar (a).
                                  X X                     X

                                  t≤mA b                  a
The matrix multiplication exponent                                                                   82


On the right, the inner sum is the Laurent coefficient of order e − e′ − t for an insertion
St x approaching b on the far-side A-sphere. This order is integral, since e′ − e − mA = i
and mA − t are integers for nonzero terms. Interpolate the coefficient by far-side regular
evaluations, with vacuum in the corresponding B-position. The sum over b sews the two
spheres at energy e′ . The new moving table, now on the far side with energies (t, 0), retains
its ordinary ports and the wire to the residue (Figure 8). All reordering signs depend only
on the specified energies, and the identity remains valid when x is coupled to the private
index or to a spectator.

Figure 8. A moving insertion of energies (mA , mB ) crosses from the near cell to the far cell. The
solid wire identifies seam states of energy e′ ≤ e − µ. The dashed wire carries the old private state to
the new moving table, whose sewn energy is t ≤ mA .

    Both interpolations can be made over the same finite field K as the original contraction.
Multiplying by a common denominator embeds the space of rational functions bounded at
infinity with prescribed poles into the polynomials of degree at most the total pole bound.
Evaluation at more than that many regular field points is injective, so these evaluations span
the dual and recover each required Laurent coefficient. Exclude all occupied positions. The
map z 7→ z P permutes K, so regular evaluations can use corresponding positions on both
spheres. The accounting below bounds the degrees by OD (T ) and the number of occupied
positions per cell by a polynomial in D. One can first obtain those bounds over the algebraic
closure and use them to choose K before any interpolation. Fix an ordering of the field
elements and, for each allowed set of regular points in this order, store the entries of one
reconstruction matrix in the table dictionary. The local control need only name the selected
point and check its availability.
    Allow at most U = D5 crossings in a move. When this limit is reached, its current mark
becomes a collector. Nonterminal fusions between successive crossings remove one stationary
insertion each. A completed move never releases a new mover, so this procedure terminates
in every summand.

21.3    Accounting for motion
We first bound the energy stored in the final insertions. An original station of arity ti has
initial weighted energy O((1 + ti /T )2 H). The auxiliaries contribute at most DH per occupied
cell. Also allocate one reserve H to each original movable station. The initial insertion energy
and reserves are O(D2 H) per cell. Over all cells, including all networks in the batch, the
bound is
                                                                            !!
                                                             −2
              O H D #{occupied cells} + #{i} + T                                 = O(W H/T ).
                                                                  X
                                                                      t2i
                                                                  i
The matrix multiplication exponent                                                         83


    A continuing fusion cannot increase the sum of participating energies, by Equation (21.1).
Keep only the reserve of the current move. In a crossing the energy m pays for t ≤ mA on
the far side and mB /P at the near-side residue. At a final fusion the collector may gain
up to m + gL/P ≤ (1 + 3g)µ, since H ≥ 1 and P ≥ 1. Count energy at collectors with
weight 1/(1 + 3g) and energy elsewhere, including reserves, with weight one. The sum cannot
increase at any of the three operations or when a move expires. The total insertion energy in
every resulting summand is consequently Og (W H/T ).
    There is also a bound in each cell. A crossing brings in at most t + H ≤ µ of charge. By
Equation (21.2) the seam loses at least this much energy. A cell has at most g seams, each
initially of energy Og (D2 H). Adding the initial charge within the cell bounds its current
insertion energy by Og (D2 H) at every step. Moreover, each crossing decreases the seam
energy by at least H, so throughout the construction a cell has Og (D2 ) crossing incidences.
Only these incidences can introduce new stationary residues. With H = OD (T ) and P fixed,
these energy and mark bounds give the asserted OD (T ) total pole bound. The number of
expired moves over all cells is at most the total number of crossings divided by U . Including
the initial immobiles and designated collectors, the total number of collectors is O(W/(DT )).
    It remains to route the ordinary wires created  √ in the expansion. By Equation (20.2),
the private state left at a crossing uses Og (DT / F + 1) bits. One endpoint follows the
mover. The other stays at the residue until another insertion absorbs it, then travels with
that insertion. A residue that can still be absorbed carries only the endpoint created at its
own crossing. Once a moving endpoint finishes a move, its collector stays fixed. A move
visits at most U + 1 cells counting repetitions, collecting at most Og (D2 ) residue endpoints
per visit and creating at most U of its own. Hence it carries Og ((U + 1)D2 ) endpoints along
any one hop. Using the bound on incident crossings and including terminations gives the
looser bound Og ((U + 1)D6 ) local wire segments per cell. Successive motions within a single
cell require only one local connection. With F from Section 20, these segments have total
width O(D−2ℓ−1 T ) + OD (1) there.
    The private-selector wires start at marks that read them and at the stations checking
the selectors and evaluating the sphere forms. Their endpoints follow the same paths. Each
cell starts with polynomially many such ports in D and the number of chunks, so the path
and crossing bounds give a polynomial bound in the same quantities on incident selector
segments and copies. This count is independent of b and of the selector rate. After fixing
the number of chunks, choose the rate small enough that the total selector width, including
equality copies, is at most D−2ℓ T per cell. The next section converts the remaining energy
levels into table arities.


22     Accounting for the new arities
After the motion, each collector has two homogeneous sphere legs and may also hold external
bits or thin wires. A seam can still have large energy. We divide the species into groups so
that each group meets a small table, even when one group receives the entire energy of a
seam.
    Choose M , a power of D specified below. Both species counts R, R′ can be taken divisible
by M in Equation (20.1). Divide the species of each sphere into M consecutive equal chunks,
preserving whole blocks in the private construction. Expand each remaining homogeneous leg
as a direct sum over its tuple of chunk energies, using the prescribed tuple at each untouched
The matrix multiplication exponent                                                           84


auxiliary insertion and the same tuple on both sides of a seam. Every basis state occurs once
in this expansion. For fixed chunk energies, the sphere form factors into the chunk forms.
Reordering the generators contributes a single sign determined by the parity of each chunk
at each site.
    Consider a generic leg of energy e on r species, and write ej for the energy in chunk j, so
  j j = e. By Equation (19.1) and Cauchy–Schwarz, the total coded width satisfies

Within a chunk, the total number of bits can be read from the counts of block types and
