Core stations. To bound the total arity at the chunk tables, first sum their port widths
over all chunks. Untouched auxiliary insertions have total coded width O(DT (1 + log D))
The matrix multiplication exponent                                                          85


per cell by Section 20.2. The seams have at most g ports per cell of total width Og (DT )
after summing over √ chunks in Equation (22.1). A cell also has Og (D2 ) residual private legs.
Each costs Og (DT / F ) + O(M ) across all chunks by Equations (20.2) and (22.1), so all
residual legs together cost O(T ) per cell for large T . Legs paired with collectors contribute
at most the global frozen arity. The ordinary wires from motion, the selector wires, and any
scalar tables contribute O(T ) per cell. Thus core arity before controlling the host degree
is O(W (1 + log D)). Untouched
                         √        auxiliaries have individual arity O(T /a), the residues have
individual arity O(DT / F ), and the chunk tables and routing wires have each been made
smaller than D−ℓ T by a fixed margin. These and the selector checks exhaust the core tables.
    To build a common host, reserve slots in each cell for each kind of table and bundle
that can occur simultaneously in a single summand. This requires DO(1) slots per cell. The
block size changes table entries and selector lengths, while the same slots serve every block
type. Combine scalar factors in their cell. Initially allow all connections between slots in
the same cell or in neighboring cells. Split any direct connection between collectors with a
core identity table on the thin wire carried along their paths. For each core station, attach
a fixed balanced binary tree with one leaf per possible bundle. Its root receives the table’s
ordered ports, and each internal vertex separates its list of bits into the sublists destined
for its two children. The corresponding equality tables pass each bit along a unique path
of length O(1 + log D) and no internal arity exceeds twice the original station arity. This
proves the asserted degree and total core bounds. Finally take c larger than the exponents in
these counts by a margin depending on ℓ. Padding a nonempty bundle to ⌈T /Dc ⌉, or making
this minimum charge for any one station, then uses at most O(D−2ℓ T ) extra bits per cell.
Unique zero padding preserves the tensor. It also absorbs any bounded number per cell of
weight-only tables in these counts. This proves the first three conclusions of Lemma 19.1.

Indices of the outer sum. Encode each bundle by its endpoint slots, its length, and
its position in the ordered ports at its ends. The input label already includes any local
permutation of bits. No individual exterior address is needed when a bundle is carried as an
ordered list. The branching data at one occupied cell comprise the initial chunk histograms,
the motion and fusion choices (positions, energies, interpolation points and orders), and
the final chunk splittings. Their number of entries is bounded for fixed D, including the
size of the alphabet of the histograms. Each integer ranges over T OD (1) . A field point uses
OD (log T ) bits. All private selector bits are wires inside the summand and are excluded from
this count. Zero unused entries give a unique padding of a term index.
    Use the supplied coloring of the (4D5 + 20)-th power of the host. Greedy coloring gives
such a coloring with OD (1) colors for fixed g. Start motions successively by color and station
slot. Concurrent motions affect disjoint neighborhoods, since each has at most D5 crossings.
A complete padded schedule therefore has length bounded in terms of D, g only. In each
step the cells may send their local descriptions to their neighbors. A merger concatenates
the descriptions of its two input tables and the operation parameters. A crossing adds the
named interpolation, the fusion, and the new endpoints of its thin wire. The history of any
resulting table draws on a bounded number (depending on D) of these records. Thus a label
of length OD (A + log(T + 1)) specifies each entry as a function of its ports through the fixed
dictionary. The dictionary may use arbitrary interpolation weights, restriction maps and
internal contractions. Generating its labels requires no evaluation of its entries.
The matrix multiplication exponent                                                           86


    It remains to check that the short computations never require inspecting the selector
bits. Counts in a chunk histogram fix the energies and coded widths. Range and total
checks admit every realizable histogram; the selector check gives zero whenever an admitted
histogram is unrealizable. Interpolation may use a fixed ordered collection of all regular field
points and weights indexed by the local pole bounds. The scheduler only checks avoidance
of marks, the ranges of the indices, and the rules of fusion and motion. Zero coefficients
may be retained. The checks and length estimates use arithmetic on OD (log(T + 1))-bit
integers or field coordinates, including division with remainder and rounded square roots.
The scheduler copies old labels as strings in fixed padded slots. At each cell the number of
records and operations on them is bounded independently of T , giving OD (A + (1 + log T )O(1) )
bit operations and bits communicated. This proves the final conclusion of Lemma 19.1.


Part IV
From arity to network volume
23     Reducing the total arity of a tensor power
The concentration estimate of Lemma 19.1 divides a network into a few large stations and
a core of small stations. To lower the cost of evaluating the network we must also reduce
the total arity. Indeed, if uv is the arity at station v, the batched evaluation in Lemma 16.2
costs, per tensor copy, at most a constant times the number of stations plus
                                                         p−1 X
                                          upv ≤ max uv
                                 X
                                                                  uv .
                                                 v
                               v: uv >0                      v

Here 1 < p < 2. We can therefore afford some growth in the largest arity if enough incident
bits disappear. The construction below makes this trade repeatedly, keeping a bound on the
sum of squared arities so that each new network again meets the concentration hypothesis.
    Throughout this part k is a finite field with ωk < 9/4, and logarithms are binary. A table
network over a field K has bit wires and a table {0, 1}s → K at each station of arity s. Its
size is the number of stations plus the sum of their arities, including external legs. Parallel
bits between two stations form an ordered bundle. A label names the station’s table in a
dictionary fixed for the network.

Lemma 23.1 (Compression of a tensor power). Let h = ⌈log2 n⌉, 0 < δ ≤ 1/10, and
0 < β ≤ b ≤ 1/2. For every sufficiently small fixed a > 0 depending only on k, δ, β, and all
sufficiently large n, the following holds. Work over an extension K/k of degree O(log h) large
enough for Lemma 19.1 at the scales used below. Suppose that an input table network has
maximum arity hb or less, (log h)O(1) -bit table labels, size at most m with n ≤ m ≤ n1.1 , and
e ≤ mh−2a external bits. Some d with 1 ≤ d = no(1) admits a table network over K for its
dth tensor power, with all de external bits separately accessible, such that

                    size ≤ md h−(1−δ)a , maximum arity ≤ hb+(1+δ)a .

The output labels again have (log h)O(1) bits. The cutoff for n may depend on the fixed
exponent in the input label-length bound.
The matrix multiplication exponent                                                           87


  Call one application of Lemma 23.1 a macro. Before the macro the lookup bound is
O(mhb(p−1) ) per copy. After it, the bound acquires the factor

                                     h−a{(1−δ)−(1+δ)(p−1)} .

Since p < 2, choosing δ small makes the exponent negative. Section 27 accumulates this
saving before evaluating the tables.
    The macro expands a power of the original tensor as a sum of products. Each product has
many factors, called blocks. We combine blocks in groups of r to pack stations or rearrange
their wires. Each round first concentrates the squared arities, then uses a fixed number of
wire reductions to shorten the core, and finally restores bounded degree so the next round
can start. A wire reduction, called a beat, consists of several contractions followed by a reset
of the core degree. The number of rounds grows with h. Once D is fixed, all repetition counts
within a round are constant. Table 1 collects the budgets and the quantities they measure.
    Different summands can use different wirings. The bounds in Sections 23–25 apply to
each summand. Section 26 represents their sum on one fixed network and completes the
proof of Lemma 23.1.

23.1    The common graph and its initial loads
Each summand is a product of blocks, with disjoint lists of external bits. In a single summand
its Nb blocks use the same simple graph of possible adjacencies, the host. An occurrence
is a pair consisting of a host position and a block. It can be empty. Each actual internal
wire joins stations in the same block, and the local port order tells which bits at its ends
correspond. Thus a host edge can carry different bundles in different blocks. We permit the
host to depend on the summand.
    At a grouping step, relabel the blocks and group them r at a time, with r a power of two.
In each group the old blocks are multiplied and transformed together. New positions may be
indexed by an old position, a dyadic subinterval of the r indices, and a bounded number of
other choices. There are fewer than 2r intervals. If H denotes the number of host positions,
the padding allowed in any one step will have

                                  H ′ ≤ crH,       Nb′ = Nb /r,                           (23.1)


               Quantity   Meaning within one summand
               H, Nb      positions of the common host and blocks using it
               r          number of blocks combined at each grouping step
               S, T0      initial blocks and original copies per block, with d = ST0
               L, T       total arity budget and working arity scale
               D          concentration parameter, fixed for all rounds
               X, Z       width between core stations and total frozen charge
               ∆, ∇       degree bounds for the core host and the full host
               B, u       maximum packing load and unit of internal width

Table 1. Parameters for the compression of a tensor power. The budgets L, X and Z each cover all
blocks of the summand.
The matrix multiplication exponent                                                             88


where c is fixed throughout all repetitions of that step. Constants here may depend on the
round design. Transformations at a single position using a fixed number of slots can be
included in the adjacent grouping step.
    At the beginning of each round, every live occurrence holds one station. The host has
degree bounded by an absolute constant, and external bits are fixed at specified ports. With
T a dyadic width and L a total arity budget, the charged arity of each live station of actual
arity sv is zv = max(sv , θT ), where θ > 0 is a small fixed dyadic constant chosen below. Our
invariants are
                                                          max zv ≤ DT /2.                (23.2)
                     X                 X
                          zv ≤ L,         zv2 ≤ LT,
                                                           v
                       v                v
All three bounds cover all blocks of the summand. The first pays for surviving bits. The
second is the input to the next local concentration, and the third lets us pack several stations
without overflowing one cell. The floor θT also bounds how many table descriptions can
enter a pack of a given charge. Remove every scalar station and retain its entry as a factor
of the summand.
    First replace the input by a graph of bounded degree. On the ports of each station of
positive arity s use a balanced binary tree. The root receives its s bits as a bundle, and
an identity at each split divides one ordered bundle in two, of lengths differing by at most
one. Place an identity at each leaf and attach the original wires and external bits at the
leaves. Connecting two of the resulting leaves also handles an input wire that was a loop.
The arities on either end of a tree edge have bounded ratio. The leaf-to-leaf connections have
matching unit width. There are O(log(2 + h)) levels and O(m log(2 + h)) total arity. All the
new tables have short labels.
    Choose a dyadic T0 ∈ [4hb , 8hb ]. Stack T0 copies of the network just constructed. At a
station of arity s let v be the largest dyadic integer at most T0 /s. Divide the copies into
consecutive intervals of length v. Give each interval its own position carrying the product
of the corresponding tables. Its arity sv belongs to (T0 /2, T0 ]. Comparable arities on the
two ends of each old edge imply that each interval meets only boundedly many intervals
at the other end. Each edge inherits the order of the old bundle and then of the copies,
so its two endpoints use the same bit order. The stacked graph has bounded degree and
H0 = O(m log(2 + h)) positions, since a station of arity s gives at most 2s intervals. The
table at an interval is specified by the old table label, the interval length, and a bounded
number of ordered port lengths.
    Take S blocks of this stack and pad H0 to order m log(2 + h). Include the dth power
of the product of the removed input scalars as a factor of the final tensor. The starting
parameters are

                   d = T0 S,      T = T0 ,     L = L0 = CmT0 S log(2 + h).                  (23.3)

The total charged arity is at most L0 for a sufficiently large absolute C. Each station has
arity in (T0 /2, T0 ], so its square is at most T0 times its arity. This gives all three bounds in
(23.2). The ed exterior bits retain their individual names throughout the construction.


24     Reducing the width between unfrozen stations
After concentration, some stations remain frozen for the rest of this round. The others form
its core. We shall reduce the width between core stations before restoring bounded degree
The matrix multiplication exponent                                                           89


and passing all the stations to the next round. Each core occurrence carries one tensor. Each
frozen occurrence holds a list of tensors and the charge assigned to each when it was frozen.
All exterior legs are on frozen tensors. We discard host adjacencies between frozen stations,
since they carry no wires.
    Let X be the sum, over all blocks, of the widths of the core–core wires, counting each
wire once. Let Z be the total frozen charge. The core–frozen wires, called tethers, have total
width at most Z. For packing, give each core occurrence load equal to its arity and each
frozen occurrence load equal to the sum of its charges times a fixed dyadic factor in (0, 1].
Write B for an upper bound on one load. The sum of all loads satisfies M ≤ 2X + 2Z. Each
internal width is a multiple of an integer u ≤ B.
    The host records possible contacts across all blocks. Write ∆ ≥ 3 for a bound on its core
degree and ∇ ≥ ∆ for a bound on its full degree. A core station can meet different frozen
stations in different blocks, so even a core of degree three can have many potential tethers.
The width estimate below is uniform in their number.

Lemma 24.1 (Reduction of core width). Fix a bound on the incoming core degree and
assume the hypothesis of Theorem 16.1. There is a fixed sequence of grouping and contraction
steps, called a beat, with the following bounds in each summand, for slack σ > 0.

                    Xout ≤ 12 X + C(Z + σ),       ∆out ≤ 3,      Zout = Z.

The identity retains all exterior legs separately. The maximum load, the reciprocal bundle
unit and log(2∇) increase by at most fixed factors. These factors and C are independent of
the incoming full degree.
    At a step with H host positions and Nb blocks, a power of two, choose dyadic r | Nb .
Grouping leaves Nb /r blocks and uses at most crH positions. It suffices to take r ≥ c∗ ,
c∗ HNb B/r ≤ σ at each step and a starting unit divisible by the product of the subdivision
factors. The position and slack constants c, c∗ may depend on the degree bounds at the step
in question.

    A beat consists of several wire-saving subrounds and one reset. Each subround trades
some core width for more possible core contacts. The reset routes the surviving wires through
a core of degree three. We shall choose the number of subrounds so that their combined
saving pays for this routing.

24.1    A wiring identity
Place six parties in the triangular array of Figure 9. A pair is permitted if it lies in one row
or one column. The desired equality wires AB and CD lie in neither. We shall express their
product as a sum of networks on permitted pairs and then contract an entire row or column
partition. This leaves a choice of two layouts for the same summand.
    An s-bit wire is the equality tensor on the two s-bit indices. For a scale-one ordinary
price over k, let m be the greatest price-distance on a permitted pair. A spanning tree of the
permitted graph supplies one bit shared by all six parties, by equating its incident bits at
each vertex. Thus 1 ≤ 5m. Both demanded distances are at most 2m. If d(C, D) = 2m, the
paths C, M, D and C, N ′ , D make M and N ′ the same midpoint in a CAT(0) extension. If
also d(A, B) = 2m, then C and M = N ′ are both midpoints of A, B. This would give C = M
The matrix multiplication exponent                                                              90


              Demanded wires                 Row partition          Column partition

             C      M        A          C         M          A     C       M       A

             N′      D                  N′        D                N′      D

             B                          B                          B

Figure 9. An equality wire between C and M , for example, disappears when the rows are contracted
and survives when the columns are contracted. Every permitted wire disappears in one layout. Across
all copies of the identity in a summand, the cheaper of the two common layouts retains at most half
the replacement width.


although d(C, M ) = m > 0. Compactness of scale-one prices therefore gives a uniform λ∗ < 2
with
                              d(A, B) + d(C, D) ≤ 2λ∗ m.
    Choose λ∗ < λ0 < 2. For fixed η ′ > 0 and arbitrarily large b, we claim that the two
                                                      ′
width-b demands admit an ordinary sum of at most 2η b networks, each with total permitted
width at most 2λ0 b. Indeed, choose h0 large enough that ⌊2λ0 h0 ⌋ > 2λ∗ h0 . As supply, take
the direct sum over permitted pairs of a single wire on that pair of width ⌊2λ0 h0 ⌋. At each
spectral point the price of this supply exceeds that of the two width-h0 demands. Strict
spectral comparison makes the width-b demands, for b = h0 r, a restriction of boundedly
many direct copies of the rth supply power. Expanding the direct sums and applying the
local restriction maps gives the claimed networks. With Eperm the set of permitted pairs,
a summand index takes r log2 |Eperm | + Oh0 (1) bits. Choose h0 and then r large to fit η ′ b.
The identity over k extends to every field K/k. For the subround we need a fixed number of
choices even when each wire carries many copies of a bit.

Lemma 24.2 (A fixed choice of widths). There are constants b ∈ N and λ < 2 such that
for every z ≥ 1 the two width-bz demands are the sum of a bounded number of networks on
the same six parties. Each uses at most 2λb bundles of z bits on permitted edges. Its local
tensors are specified by z, the party and the index of the summand. The index set and choices
of widths (in units of z) can be fixed independently of z.

Proof. Raise the finite identity above to its zth tensor power. A term is initially indexed
by a sequence of z selectors, with at most ⌈η ′ b⌉ bits per selector. For each permitted edge
round up the total number of bits on it to a multiple of z. Allowing empty groups, group the
sequences by the vector of rounded numbers in units of z. This vector belongs to a fixed finite
set, and rounding costs at most one unit per permitted edge. Within each group choose a
spanning tree of the connected permitted graph and send the full sequence on each tree edge,
using at most ⌈η ′ b⌉ extra units per edge. A local table checks identical valid sequences on all
incident tree edges, membership in the group, and zeros in padding positions. It uses the
sequence to split positions on each original edge into the prescribed slices and evaluates the
product of the z local tables. Connectivity makes the encodings of a sequence unique once
the sequence is specified. Summing the tree variables therefore gives precisely the sum for
that group. The total in units of z is at most 2λ0 b + |Eperm | + 5⌈η ′ b⌉. Choose η ′ sufficiently
small and then b sufficiently large to bound this by 2λb with λ < 2.
The matrix multiplication exponent                                                           91


24.2    Subrounds
In a subround we group occurrences at each position into packs. Two outgoing units from
different packs can play the roles AB and CD. We first arrange enough such pairs, with a
bound on how many packs a single contraction can merge.

Balanced partitions. Within each group of r block indices use the dyadic intervals in
their common order. Give every host edge e a propagation distance

                                                          ∆   on core edges,
                                                      (
                     se = 64⌈log2 (2xe )⌉,     xe =
                                                          ∇   on tethers.

At each position mark every interval of load greater than AB, with 2 ≤ A ≤ r dyadic. Marks
require splitting. Propagate each mark to its parent at the same position and to its ancestor
se levels higher at the other end of each incident edge e, whenever the ancestor exists. The
leaves, called colonies, have load at most AB and length at least A. Intersecting colonies at
opposite ends of e differ in length by a factor at most 2se , by propagation from the parent of
the smaller colony.
    A common permutation of the indices inside each group gives a total colony count

                                   Q ≤ C1 (HNb /r + M/(AB))                              (24.1)

with C1 absolute. To see this, identify the indices with Fr in a binary basis and multiply
by a uniformly random nonzero element. The probability that two distinct indices fall in
one depth-j interval is at most 2−j . At a source position in one group let xi ≤ 1/A be the
loads divided by AB, and put x = i xi . An initially marked interval has at least 1/2 in
                                      P

ordered cross products of distinct normalized loads. Thus the expected number of initial
marks at depth j is at most 2x2 2−j . The sum of 2−se /16 over incident edges is at most 1/2
at every vertex. Summing the resulting path weights shows that at most 2 · 2s/16 positions
are reachable with total propagation distance at most s. Fix the total upward displacement
s, allowing steps to parents at the same position. At each reachable position, depth-j source
marks then reach at most 2j−s distinct ancestors. The expected count there, summed over
j ≥ s, is at most
                                 min{2j−s , 2x2 2−j } = O(x2−s/2 ).
                             X

                             j≥s

Summing over distances, sources and groups bounds the number of marks in expectation by
O(M/(AB)). Each binary partition has one more leaf than marked internal nodes. Choose
a multiplier minimizing the actual count.

A wire-saving subround. Orient all core edges. Color each block thick or thin, using
one affine functional over F2 on its within-group index. Every position in a block receives
the same color. To pack tensors at one position, take their tensor product and retain their
wires. Any loops created by later mergers are contracted into the local table. At each core
colony gather all thick tensors in one pack. In order, close a thin pack once it reaches load B.
There are O(A) thin packs, each of load at most 2B. At a frozen colony concatenate the
lists. Let P = 264⌈log2 (2∆)⌉ and K1 = ∆P . Pairwise independence of the colors bounds the
expected absolute imbalance of thick and thin outgoing widths in a colony by (B i ai )1/2 ,
                                                                                      P
The matrix multiplication exponent                                                          92


where ai is the outgoing
                     √ core amount of its ith occurrence. Summing gives a coloring of total
imbalance at most BQX.
    For a source colony, group thick outgoing units by their destination pack. There are at
most K1 bins by the length ratio along core edges. Treat the outgoing units of each thin
pack as one item and match the entire item into any bin with enough remaining capacity. At
the first failure every remaining capacity is below 2B, so the unmatched part of the smaller
total is at most 2BK1 . The one-sided matched width therefore satisfies

                               I ≥ 21 X − 12       BQX − 2BK1 Q.
                                               p


The colony bound (24.1) and the slack condition with c∗ ≥ A give BQ = O((X + Z + σ)/A).
Take A a sufficiently large dyadic multiple, up to rounding, of 1+K1 . Then I ≥ X/4−O(Z +σ)
with absolute implicit constant.
    Within each bin pair its matched thin units with distinct thick units. Use Lemma 24.2
with b∗ = b and z = u/b∗ , an integer by the subdivision hypothesis. In each pair the thick
wire plays AB, with A its source, and the thin wire plays CD, with C its source. The sources
share a colony. The destinations B, D belong to thick and thin packs, respectively, because
a wire stays in one block and the color is common to that block. Introduce fresh auxiliary
parties M, N ′ for each pair. Across all pairs, the two demanded widths have total 2I, and
the replacement wires have total width at most 2λI in each product summand.
    In one such summand, add the widths that survive the row contractions over all pairs.
Do the same for the column contractions. Every replacement wire is internal to one of the
two layouts, so the two totals sum to at most 2λI. Perform the cheaper contraction in every
pair. Further identifications can only decrease the surviving width. The unmatched wires
contribute at most X − 2I, hence

             X ′ ≤ X − (2 − λ)I ≤ ρX + C(Z + σ),            ρ = 1 − (2 − λ)/4 < 1.

     Matching each thin item in its entirety controls the growth in both load and degree. The
merged old packs form stars. A thin pack in its source role joins its own thick pack in the
row layout or its one chosen thick destination in the column layout. In its destination role
it joins only an auxiliary party. Thus one thick center absorbs at most O((1 + K1 )A) thin
packs. Every auxiliary is absorbed into a demand endpoint, to which its new incidences
can be charged at a constant multiple of the matched width. A dyadic load multiplier g,
polynomial in ∆, bounds both the resulting core loads and the scaled loads of the frozen
lists. The new unit is u/b∗ .
     Reserve pack slots at every potential dyadic interval. Possible core contacts run between
packs of one colony or of intersecting neighboring intervals with ratio at most P . There are
O(P + log(2P )) possible intervals per neighbor for each given interval. The stars and the four
matched endpoints have bounded diameter in this graph. Taking a fixed graph power gives a
common core support of degree at most C2 ∆C2 . A tether needs just one old tether adjacency
with its own length ratio, followed by a bounded number of core adjacencies. Hence the full
degree is at most C2 ∇C2 , with absolute C2 enlarged if necessary. There are fewer than 2r
potential intervals per position. This gives the claimed bound on positions and keeps tethers
attached to their respective frozen tensors.

24.3    Resetting the core degree
Use H, Nb , ∆, ∇ for the parameters immediately before the reset, and put P = 264⌈log2 (2∆)⌉ ,
K1 = ∆P . The reset replaces the core by a degree-three host. Its principal cost is a
permutation network at each core base. Fix a trivalent network of size c0 Y w0 that realizes
all permutations of Y rows of width w0 by fixing its tags, where 4K1 ≤ Y and c0 ≥ 1. Call
this network the rotor.
    Figure 10 shows the two routes. First partition the blocks at each position into intervals
called bases, using their mean loads, and reserve capacity between each pair of adjacent core
bases. Mixing the blocks makes most bases fit these reservations. A wire between two such
bases crosses a direct link between dedicated rotor outputs. Every core wire incident to an
exceptional base takes a longer route through binary trees. We must bound the total load on
those bases before charging the extra hops.

capacity at a base is at most 2F B. Base lengths and capacities are fixed from the means

Mixing. Permute all block indices by one invertible binary matrix. Within the bases, mark
dyadic subintervals (including possibly the bases themselves) of load above 2F B, propagating
with the same edge distances. Leaves have load at most 2F B, length at least F , and the
previous length-ratio bounds (base splits above the marked region already satisfy propagation
by comparison of the base depths; propagate as in colony packing). Call a base bad if it
splits or an incident core reservation overflows. For any δ∗ > 0, choosing F sufficiently large
in terms of ∆, Y, δ∗ gives a permutation with

                                              load ≤ δ∗ (M + σ).                          (24.2)

To prove this uniformly in the full degree, count how far one overloaded interval can propagate.
For a given position having nv = Nb /sv bases, normalize old loads by F B, obtaining xi ≤ 1/F .

intervals and half their (normalized) load. The prefix collision probability for distinct indices
under a random invertible binary matrix is at most the reciprocal of the number of intervals.

                                                       EUj ≤ nv 2−j .

A mark at depth j can force a base split only along paths with se ≤ 2j, since neighboring
                                                                   P

base depths change by at most half the edge distance. For this source mark, dyadic nesting
gives at most one affected base at each reachable position. The path count used in (24.1)
therefore bounds the number affected by 2 · 2j/8 . At depth j, use Vj if 1/F ≤ 2−j and Uj
otherwise. Weight this estimator by 4 · 2⌈j/8⌉ . Split the sum at j = log2 F . The expected
charge is at most a constant times nv j≥0 2j/8 min{F −1 , 2−j } = O(nv F −7/8 ). Bases with

load above 2F B are paid for directly by the depth-zero estimate. For core capacity overflows
use the variance estimator for the contact width divided by F B at the depth of the base
intersections. The F B/K1 buffer above the mean ensures that each overflow contributes at
least 1/K12 . Summing the unweighted variances in expectation over core edges costs at most
X/(F 2 B). Each overflow affects at most two bases. Those whose load exceeds 2F B have
already been paid for at depth zero, and every other affected base costs at most 2F B. Let Φ
be the sum of the weighted interval estimators and K12 times the contact variances. We have
bounded total bad load deterministically by O(F BΦ) and have shown

                           F B EΦ = O(F −7/8 (M + σ) + K12 X/F ).

A matrix with estimator at most the mean proves (24.2). Section 26 finds such a permutation
by computing collision scores.

The routes. After mixing, group consecutive runs of r blocks. Give every core leaf Y
input terminals. Assign its old tensors whole to a least-loaded terminal successively, so
each terminal receives at most 2F B/Y + B ≤ 3F B/Y . Keep all tethers at their assigned
terminals, with their other ends in the concatenated frozen list. For every good core base the
The matrix multiplication exponent                                                              95


rotor connects its input terminals to Y output terminals. A reservation of capacity c receives
⌈cY /(8F B)⌉ pairs of dedicated output terminals, joined by direct links. This needs at most
Y /4 + K1 ≤ Y terminals per base. Divide the units of a good–good contact evenly among its
pairs. Their output loads are at most 8F B/Y + u ≤ 9F B/Y .
    At a good base write mij for the number of units routed from input i to output j. The
row and column sums of (⌈mij /w0 ⌉) are at most

                             9F B/(Y uw0 ) + Y ≤ 10F B/(Y uw0 ).

Bring every row and column sum to the largest sum by adding edges. Hall’s Theorem
decomposes the resulting regular bipartite multigraph into perfect matchings. Each matching
configures a layer of the rotor with bit edges powered to width u. Pin unused inputs and
corresponding outputs to zero. Take the tensor product of the layer tables at each rotor
position. This uses only the prescribed equality tensor obtained by pinning the rotor tags.
Balanced binary trees at each rotor row collect its w0 ports, at O(log(2w0 )) hops per unit.
Including direct links this costs O((c0 + log(2w0 ))F B) per good base.
    For a core contact with a bad endpoint use balanced binary contact trees attached to its
input terminals. The possible neighboring leaf intervals have length ratios at most P , with
Y terminals per leaf, giving depth O(log(2∆Y )). Route ordered lists through the trees by
equality tensors. The assigned lists at both ends give a unique internal assignment to the
transmitted bits. Choose δ∗ in (24.2) small enough to pay this routing depth. Thus for an
absolute A0 ,
                           X ′ ≤ A0 (1 + c0 + log(2w0 ))(X + Z + σ).
This counts every core link in the rotors, collecting trees, direct connections and contact
trees. The unit remains u. At every input terminal, output terminal, tree vertex, and rotor
vertex the load is O(F B), as follows from the terminal and layer bounds. For a frozen leaf its
scaled list load is at most 2F B. The core has degree at most three. Rotor vertices have this
degree; input terminals have at most rotor-row (via one edge to a row tree) and contact-tree
connections within the core; output terminals have at most a rotor-row connection and a
direct link. Each binary tree has degree at most three. For tethers the common host allows
every input terminal at every possible intersecting core leaf of the prescribed length ratio.
The resulting full degree is bounded by a fixed power of 2∇, with constants depending on
this reset. Reserving this hardware at all possible interval slots costs at most crH positions.

24.4    Paying for the reset
Write
                                   κ = A0 (1 + c0 + log(2w0 ))
for the width multiplier of the reset. Take t to be the least positive integer such that κρt ≤ 1/2.
Repeated use of the subround estimate gives core width at most ρt X + C(Z + σ)/(1 − ρ)
just before the reset. After it, the coefficient of the incoming X is at most one half. The
remaining cost is a fixed multiple of Z + σ, provided we can choose a rotor with Y ≥ 4K1 for
the degree reached after t subrounds.
    Starting at bounded core degree, the recurrence ∆′ ≤ C2 ∆C2 gives
                                                                        ′
                        log2 (4K1 ) ≤ C t+1 ≤ C ′′ (2 + c0 + log(2w0 ))C .
The matrix multiplication exponent                                                         96


Here C, C ′ , C ′′ depend only on the incoming core degree and the subround constants. For
Y = 2y , Theorem 16.1 gives w0 = ⌈y aζ ⌉ and c0 = Oζ (y ζ ) for each ζ > 0. Fix ζ < 1/(2C ′ ),
then take y large. The displayed polynomial bound is at most y, so this rotor covers the
required core degree.
   The number of subrounds t, the rotor, F and δ∗ depend only on the incoming core-degree
bound and the conditional sorting construction. These parameters bound the growth of
the maximum load. The full degree governs tether propagation and the number of host
positions to reserve. All subrounds together divide u by bt∗ , and their polynomial degree
bounds, followed by the reset, multiply log(2∇) by at most a fixed factor.
   This completes the proof of Lemma 24.1.


25     The arity budgets after one round
                                                                       √
The frozen stations produced by concentration have total arity O(L/ D). The core has
small maximum arity but may still use more than L bits. Repeated beats reduce its internal
width until the total charge fits a smaller budget L/G. To resume concentration we will also
need a bound on the squared charges and an absolute degree bound on the full host.

25.1    From concentration to bounded degree
Apply Lemma 19.1 with arity exponent ℓ = 10. Its absolute degree bound for the core fixes
the parameters of a beat in Lemma 24.1. Choose ϵ ∈ (0, 1/100] so small that in

                                       J = ⌈2ϵ log D⌉

beats the products of the per-beat increases of the maximum load, of the reciprocal bundle
unit, and of the logarithm of the overall degree are each at most D0.03 when D is sufficiently
large. These three per-beat factors are independent of D, which permits this choice. Increase
D below as needed in terms of k, δ, ϵ.
    We require ed ≤ L/D. For each grouping in this round we also require

                                      HNb T /r ≤ cD L                                   (25.1)

with cD > 0 sufficiently small, using the current host counts and the round’s starting L, T .
The choices of S, r meeting these conditions in every round are made below.

Concentrating the squared arities. Group r blocks and apply the dyadic partition
estimate (24.1) to the charged loads at absolute host degree, with maximum load DT /2 and
capacity DT . By (25.1) there are O(L/(DT )) occupied intervals over all positions and groups.
Regard each interval as one vertex of the graph to which we apply Lemma 19.1. Include all
candidate intervals as positions, and allow an edge to an interval at an old neighbor only
when the two intersect and satisfy the absolute length-ratio bound of the dyadic partition.
All actual contacts are present. Dyadic nesting bounds the simple degree of this new host by
an absolute constant.
    Inside each occupied interval retain each station of charged arity greater than T . Pack
the other stations in their order, closing a pack when its total charged arity reaches T . The
last pack can be smaller, and each pack has arity at most 2T . Its table is the product of its
The matrix multiplication exponent                                                             97


members, with any internal indices contracted. An interval contains O(D) resulting stations.
Move its external bits, if any, to one additional identity station, keeping their order and
joining the duplicate ports to the original incidences. Designate these exterior identities as
the immobile stations. Their number is at most the number of occupied intervals. The old
stations and packs had total arity at most DT per interval, and the identity adds at most
2DT . The old large stations contribute at most LT to the square sum. Each new pack has
arity at most 2T , so the packs contribute at most 2T L. If fi is the external width of interval
i, the new identities contribute at most

                                   4       fi2 ≤ 4DT ed ≤ 4LT.
                                       X

                                       i

With W = C1 L and C1 absolute and sufficiently large, these are precisely the occupation,
immobility, and square bounds of Lemma 19.1. In particular there are at most D retained
large stations, D + 1 packs and one exterior identity in an occupied interval, within its bound
of 100D.
    Expand using Lemma 19.1. Write Z for the total charged arity of the frozen stations,
and X for the total width of the internal wires between core stations,
                                                                √       counted once per wire.
In every summand the frozen charges have total Z = O(L/ D), square sum O(LT ) and
maximum O(DT ). The core stations have individual arities at most D−10 T and total arity
O(L(log D)O(1) ). The constants in these estimates are uniform in D. All positive internal
bundles have width at least T /DO(1) .
    To apply the packing operations, take an integer unit u below the minimum guaranteed
internal width, still u ≥ T /DO(1) , divisible by every subdivision prescribed in the J beats.
For fixed D this is possible once T is large, by rounding down to a multiple of their product.
Pad each positive internal bundle to the next multiple of u with bits pinned to zero. The
arity and its square grow by at most factors two and four. Raise each frozen charge to the
larger of its old charge and its padded arity. Its charge grows by at most a factor of two, so
the total Z and the square budget still obey the bounds above. Choose a dyadic load bound
B of order D−10 T covering every core station. For packing only, scale the frozen charges
by a fixed dyadic multiplier of order B/(DT ), small enough that each is at most B. This
changes neither the actual widths nor the frozen square bound. We take u small enough also
to ensure u ≤ B.
                                                                                    √
Removing the core wires. Apply Lemma 24.1 J times, with slack σ = L/ D. The
initial full host degree is polynomial in D. The load bounds at intermediate steps are fixed
multiples, depending only on D, of T , so (25.1) supplies the required slack each time. In the
recurrence of Lemma 24.1 the fixed error C(Z + σ) is multiplied by a geometric series of sum
less than two. Thus

                   Xend ≤ 2−J Xstart + O(Z + σ) = O LD−2ϵ (log D)O(1) .
                                                                            


Frozen stations retain their charges, although one occurrence can now hold a list of them.
Each list has DO(1) members. Indeed, its total scaled load is bounded by the final load bound,
while the positive charge floor gives a lower bound on the scaled load of each member. Each
core station has arity at most D−9 T . The final bundle unit is still at least T /DO(1) , the core
degree is at most three, and the overall degree ∇ satisfies log(2∇) ≤ D0.04 for D sufficiently
large. The latter estimate uses the initial O(log D) bound on the logarithm of the degree.

Figure 11. Two units leave one frozen station. The solid routes are active in this block. The dashed
routes allow either outlet to reach the other endpoint in a different block. A splitting tree on the left
may carry the full frozen arity for O(log D) levels. In the middle each active branch carries a single

Restoring bounded degree. An individual station can meet few actual neighbors and
still have many possible neighbors in the common host. At a frozen position reserve a slot
for each member of its list. The only frozen contacts are tethers to the core. Route them
in three stages (Figure 11). First split its active tether units into separate outlets, in order.
There are DO(1) outlets, by the maximum width and minimum unit. Next, from each outlet
use a binary tree addressing any potential core endpoint. At each core endpoint use a binary
tree for the potential incoming outlets. Link corresponding leaves. An internal branching
table is the identity on the concatenated strings of its active children. Inactive branches need
no station. Each block uses the trees with precisely its own tether assignments, and the trees
and slots themselves are fixed on the common host. The first trees have depth O(log D), and
the others have depth O(log(2∇) + log D) = O(D0.04 ). Their host size increases by a factor
depending only on D. A core endpoint retains at most three core contacts and receives one
tree contact, giving overall degree at most four.
     For the linear budget, the total extra arity is O(ZD0.04 ), since each tether bit travels
along one route of that length. For the square budget, the first splitting tree is bounded in
terms of the frozen arity. On a fixed level of that tree the total width is at most the arity of
its frozen station. The sum of squared arities at that level is at most four times the square of
this arity. Summing first over the frozen stations and then over levels thus gives O(LT log D).
Every other new vertex carries at most the width of one unit or all the tether bits of one core
station, so has arity at most 2D−9 T . Its contribution to the square sum is at most 2D−9 T
times its arity. The old core stations have total arity at most 2Xend + Z and obey the same
estimate.
     Together these bounds give charged arities zv at the end of a round satisfying

For frozen stations we kept the positive floor from Lemma 19.1. Every other remaining
station meets a positive internal bundle of width at least T /DO(1) . Thus all charges in
(25.2) can respect a floor of this order. The constants and the powers of log D in (25.2) are
independent of D. In particular the term ZD0.04 = O(LD−0.46 ) fits the first estimate.
    Set G = 2⌊ϵ log D⌋ and choose a dyadic q with G1+δ/4 ≤ q ≤ G1+δ/3 . This interval contains
a dyadic integer for D sufficiently large. Restart with

                                       L′ = L/G,          T ′ = qT.                               (25.3)

choosing G, q, D requires a lower bound on θ, since the grouping used the total load cap and
repacked the small stations before applying the local lemma. This proves (23.2) for the next
round.

Fix the round just constructed, including D, G, q and its s grouping steps. To save a factor

Packing slack and exterior width. In one grouping step Nb decreases by r while H

The occurrence bound holds throughout, with a constant in OD (a) independent of M, a, b, β.
These scales also justify using a single working field. They range from T0 ≥ 4hβ to a fixed
power of h. Choose the extension degree divisible by the degree of the fixed extension in
Lemma 19.1, and large enough to meet the cardinality requirement at Tj . Its degree is
O(log h) and hence O(log Ti ) for every i.

Descriptions of tables. An occurrence carries a label for its table, the widths of its
bundles, and their positions in its port order. The cost of naming its incident host edges is
bounded in terms of D because each edge can be named by its place in the local neighbor
list. Constants indexed by D may depend on the round design. These local data have total
length per occurrence at most

                                C3j+1 (log h)O(1) = hOD (a)+o(1) .                        (25.6)

    At initialization the stack at a position has a fixed table label, specified by its original
table, its stack length and the locations of its ordered sublists. Its description has length
(log h)O(1) . Within one round the following facts bound the number of old labels that can
enter any new label.

   • A colony of capacity DTi contains at most D/θ live entering stations, since each is
     charged at least θTi . The local expansion in Lemma 19.1 uses a bounded number of
     old labels, discrete choices and field elements in each output description. Its additional
     description length is OD (log h).

   • In a subround or reset, both the bound on load per leaf and the bound on the number
     of leaves contributing to one resulting pack are fixed multiples, depending on D, of
     Ti and 1, respectively. Each live core contributes at least one internal unit of width
     u ≥ Ti / poly(D). The charged load of an individual frozen station has the same
     kind of lower bound even after the fixed rescaling. Scalar stations are withdrawn as
     soon as they form. It follows that a contributing pack has a bounded number of old
     labels and a bounded number of units. Lists of unit pairings, rotor layers and ordered
     sublists at a port therefore have bounded numbers of entries. Each entry needs at most
     OD (log h + log r) bits. This also describes the routing on the final trees at the frozen
     stations.

Thus if A bounds the current label length, one step gives a bound C4 A + C4 (log h)C5 ,
including the descriptors of locally withdrawn scalar factors. The multiplicative constant
counts old labels, independently of the field degree and the length of the input dictionary
labels. Iteration through the sj steps proves Equation (25.6). Global host addresses enter
the control of a whole position only. They never have to be appended to an individual table
description.

The tensor represented by the sum. Each term now consists of R surviving blocks
and the scalar factors withdrawn along its history. Regrouping permutes the exterior lists
along with their stations and preserves each product entry by entry. A local tensor identity
replaces a product by a sum with the same entries on those ed named ports. Colorings,
packings, and routings use fixed tie-breaking rules. Only choices from local tensor identities
The matrix multiplication exponent                                                         101


index the expansion. The final internal wire assignments are summed when the R blocks are
contracted.
    For this evaluation one dictionary suffices. A key records the type, stage, description and
arity of a table, followed by its incident bits. Length fields or fixed padding give a unique
encoding. The input dictionary and the rules of the construction assign a field value to each
valid key. The values at unused keys may be fixed arbitrarily. In particular, a key for a
withdrawn scalar has no incident bits. A longest key has length

                                    OD (Tj ) + hOD (a)+o(1)

by the maximum-arity invariant and Equation (25.6). A derived table description can exceed
(log h)O(1) bits. Section 26 places that description in the argument of a dictionary call. The
label of the installed table need only specify the key format and length. This accounts
separately for the arity of a lookup and the short label required as input to the next macro.


26     Realizing the expansion on a fixed network
Different summands in Section 25 can finish with different wires and different tables. To
represent their sum on one network, we encode a choice of summand, together with an
assignment to its final wires, as a transcript. A local check determines which transcripts
contribute, and a dictionary supplies their tensor entries. The size bound depends on how
the host is shared. All blocks at one host position use the same address. The bits carried on
a wire vary independently across blocks.

26.1    From a transcript to a tensor
Let K be the working extension of k, and put h = ⌈log2 n⌉. Fix P ≤ nO(1) numbered
processors. Each has binary advice of at most hc bits and a fixed ordered list of exterior
ports. Its local record includes guesses and at most hc incoming and outgoing messages. A
message consists of an O(h)-bit key identifying source, destination and port, together with a
bit string. Communication is valid if every used key occurs exactly once at each end with
the same string.
    One fixed finite bit-RAM program checks the local record against the advice and exterior
bits. It may emit strings of length at most U as arguments to a common dictionary W with
values in K. Its decisions depend only on the strings. For fixed exterior bits, the transcript
tensor is the sum over accepted transcripts of the product of all values returned by W . The
record format, including length fields and padding, is fixed. All size-dependent program data
enter through the advice.
    The charge at a processor includes its deterministic running time, advice, exterior bits,
guesses, messages with headers, and emitted strings. Count at least one unit per record or
item. This charge is determined by the record. A local address or time uses (log h)O(1) bits.
Bit arithmetic on words of that length adds at most a factor (log h)O(1) to the bounds below.

Lemma 26.1 (Realizing a transcript). Suppose the charge of each local record is at most
               O(1)
Vmax = 2(log h) . Let V ≤ nO(1) bound the total charge of every accepted transcript and the
volume of the fixed advice and exterior interface, including one unit per processor. If U ≤ h
The matrix multiplication exponent                                                          102


and the wide-record bound of Theorem 16.1 holds, then for every fixed τ > 0 the transcript
tensor has a fixed network of size at most

                                         hτ V + P hC0 .

The gates are trivalent or are dictionary tables of arity O(1 + U + log h). Their labels use
(log h)O(1) bits. The constant C0 depends only on τ, c, the polynomial bounds in n and
the wide-record estimate. The exponent in Vmax occurs only in factors (log h)O(1) before
absorption.

Proof. Allocate space by record length. For each dyadic capacity t, from 1 to the least power
of two at least min(V, Vmax ), reserve Nt = min(P, ⌊2V /t⌋) slots. A processor with charge
s uses the least t ≥ s. Every record of this class has charge greater than t/2, so Nt slots
suffice. Guess their processor identifiers and records. Check the identifier range and the
charge, require occupied slots to form an increasing prefix in identifier order, and pin all
unused entries and padding. The two relevant capacity bounds are

                   tNt = O V (1 + log Vmax ) ,          Nt = O P (1 + log Vmax ) .       (26.1)
              X                                   X                            

               t                                    t

    Check the computation and its dictionary arguments. In a slot of capacity t, guess the
deterministic execution for at most t steps. Time-ordered checks verify its instructions,
registers, counter, addresses and halt flag, with fixed padding after halt. To check the
memory trace, sort the reads, writes and initial contents by address and execution order.
Scan each address to verify that a read gives the most recent value, starting at zero for unused
cells. This is the trace construction of OpenAI [42, Section 16.2]. A comparison network on
O(t) accesses uses O(t log2 (2t)) comparisons of (log h)O(1) -bit words. Long inputs, advice,
messages and emitted strings occupy addressed arrays and are processed in pieces. The local
cost is t(log h)O(1) plus hO(1) per slot for long identifiers and framing.
    Within the slot, group the emitted strings by least dyadic padded length u, counting an
empty string as length one. Reserve ⌊2t/u⌋ calls in that class and take them in issuance
order. The checker writes each emitted bit at the address given by its length class, ordinal
and bit offset. The same memory-trace test checks these arrays against the inputs of the
installed calls, including their active flags, actual lengths and zero padding. Each address
has O(1 + log t + log h) bits. Accounting for all the padded seats adds t(log h)O(1) to the
checking cost. A seat returns the value of W on its indicated active string and returns 1
when inactive. Its table is named by the padded length. The arity is O(1 + U + log h) and
the total table size per slot is O(t log(2 + U ) log(2 + h)).
    Match the messages. Fix w = ⌈hC1 ⌉, where C1 > 1 will be chosen for the wide-record sort.
Split each message into w-bit chunks, padding the last one and reserving one for an empty
message. Its chunk record contains an active flag, key, direction, total length, chunk number
and payload. Add a fixed sender for each processor. Its key belongs to a reserved namespace
and supplies that processor’s advice and exterior bits with their component lengths. An
occupied slot must receive the whole supply for its claimed identifier even when the supply is
empty.
    Check each outgoing source and incoming destination against the slot identifier, and
check the splitting, piece order, lengths and padding. Sort the records by active flag, key,
chunk number and direction. In the active part, require every key and chunk to occur as one
The matrix multiplication exponent                                                          103


send and one receive of equal length and payload. A duplicate message on one side collides
at chunk zero. In particular each private supply is matched to exactly one slot, so every
processor occurs once. For a given input the stable-sort relation has just one output, which
can be summed out after these checks. Its zero-one tensor over k extends to the same relation
over K.
    A slot reserves O(t/w + hc + 1) chunk records. The O(h)-bit header is copied once per
chunk, so constructing them costs (t + hO(1) )(log h)O(1) . Charge a fixed sender for its actual
supply and one partial chunk. By Equation (26.1) the reserved bit volume, including padding
at message boundaries, is
                                  V (log h)O(1) + P hO(1) .
Pad the list to a power of two. Its length is polynomial in n, so its logarithm is O(h).
Theorem 16.1 with tolerance τ /3 permits a fixed C1 large enough that sorting costs at most
O(hτ /3 ) times this volume. The subsequent scan has linear size. Equation (26.1) gives the
same volume bound for the local computations and the dictionary tables. Absorb the powers
of log h into hτ and the per-processor costs into P hC0 .
    The checking and sorting gates are trivalent over k, and each dictionary gate is named by
its length class. These names have the stated length. For each accepted transcript the slot
capacities, identifiers, order, traces, calls and chunks are uniquely specified. Conversely the
local and communication checks recover an accepted transcript. Contracting the installed
relations therefore gives its weighted sum exactly over K.

26.2    The transcript of the rounds
We now apply Lemma 26.1 to the expansion in Section 25. Initially there are S blocks of T0
copies, so d = T0 S. At every stage of the j rounds there are Nb ≥ R blocks on a host with H
allocated positions. Give each position one processor, including at the terminal stage, and
give each step O(H) more processors for balanced aggregation trees. An occurrence consists
of one position in one block.

One address for all blocks. A position holds the ordered list of its host neighbors and an
array of occurrence records in block order. Its advice specifies the parent and child naming
conventions, integer parameters, field presentation, and the initial convention for ordering
exterior ports. At an initial position it also gives the input table label. Exterior bits enter
through fixed ports in stack order.
    In one step every host degree is bounded in terms of the fixed round design. A ball
of radius OD (1 + log r) thus contains rOD (1) positions. Exchange neighbor lists to that
radius, remove repetitions by identifier and order the resulting names. This locates each
possible child and contact by an index in a local list, at a cost of hO(1) common operations
per position. A message to a contact can now carry an entire array of occurrence records
under one O(h)-bit header. Within that array only local indices are needed. There are
hO(1) phases and declarations per position. By Equation (25.6), handling the arrays costs
Nb rOD (1) (log h)O(1) hOD (a) = Nb hOD (a)+o(1) per position. The coefficients in OD (a) are
independent of a, b, β.
    For decisions shared by the host, each position computes its local contributions to the
candidate scores. Sum the integer arrays along a balanced tree and broadcast the first
minimizing candidate. At height i the width of a sum of initially z-bit entries is at most
The matrix multiplication exponent                                                           104


z + i, while the number of positions falls by a factor of order 2i . Since i≥0 2−i (z + i)O(1) =
                                                                           P

O((1 + z)O(1) ), aggregation preserves the preceding bit-cost bound. At the root allow an
                    O(1)
additional 2(log h)      operations to choose the minimizer. A greedy coloring of a given host
can be checked locally as well. In fixed identifier order, require each color to be the least
one absent from its lower neighbors. Induction on identifiers makes the checked assignment
unique.

Checking the changes of host. Each of the operations in a round supplies its contacts
from these neighbor lists and its common parameters from additive scores.

  1. For colony packing, try the multipliers of the r slots, propagate the overloaded marks to
     ancestors and neighbors, and sum the candidate colony counts. A propagation moves
     upward by at least one dyadic level, so the radius is OD (1 + log r). Relative interval
     endpoints, loads and dyadically scaled charges all have short encodings. Summing the
     local scores also chooses the affine coloring for whole-item pairing.

  2. At a local concentration step, the colony interval and its old contacts give the possible
     neighbors of each sphere. In the checked color order, the movement and interpolation
     choices of Lemma 19.1 specify the new tables, withdrawn scalars and ordered bundles.
     Its description bounds pay for the choices and labels. The port orders identify the
     exposed sublists.

  3. In a wire-saving subround the source colony and bin determine the seats of its matched
     units. Record one summand choice for each actual gadget. Sum the surviving replace-
     ment widths under each of the two contractions and take the same minimizer for all
     blocks. The widths before identifying coincident endpoints suffice for this decision.
     Bounded neighborhoods of the colonies then determine the new contacts, port orders
     and table labels.

  4. For a reset, compute each position’s mean load over the blocks. The smoothed mean has
     floor F B/r and a propagated contribution decays by at least a factor of two per edge.
     The starting load is at most B, so a neighborhood of radius O(1 + log r) determines
     the bases and their capacities.
     For the common mixer, let xi be the normalized load at a position or the normalized
                                      log N
     width at an edge in block i ∈ F2 2 b . At prefix length s the part of the collision
     estimator of Section 24.3 that depends on a candidate matrix A is a multiple of

      local integers. Aggregate the coefficients by difference and prefix length. At the root
      one can enumerate all invertible matrices in dimension log2 Nb = O((log h)2 ) within
              O(1)

      ing. Positions permute their block arrays accordingly. The bounded length ratios of
      intersecting leaves and the bounded number of units per leaf then allow the terminal
      assignments, rotor settings and new labels to be determined from neighboring records.

   5. The final splitting trees for tethers have bounded size, depending on D, per possible
      station and contact. Adjacent positions determine the active sublists. An edge between
      new children is supported by a bounded path between their parents. In the next phase
      the children use their own host adjacency, so every neighborhood collected is local to
      its current step.

    Declare messages in a fixed order of phases, ports and blocks. Enforce each successive
array and common host from the preceding data and the local summand choices. Each
term of a local identity, including a term of weight zero, has one prescribed encoding. The
intermediate arrays therefore introduce no further multiplicities.

Independent bits and tensor entries. Transfer the exterior bits in the order specified
by the splits and concatenations. The source and destination share a processor header for all
pieces sent along one contact. Their array positions and bit offsets have length (log h)O(1) .
When a sphere collects bits at its exterior station, the station is frozen during the ensuing
steps. When the blocks are reordered, permute its lists with them. Neighborhood collection
transports descriptions. Each of the ed exterior bits travels only along its specified route, at
most once per transfer. The O(log h) steps therefore use ed(log h)O(1) payload work.
    At the terminal stage, guess one bit per internal wire at its designated endpoint and
send it to the other endpoint in contact and bundle order. A table call consists of the
station description and its ordered port bits. The terminal payload costs Lj (log h)O(1) by
Equation (25.5). A scalar withdrawn earlier issues its call at withdrawal, with just the short
description. The input scalars are fixed. If their product is c, one nullary table of value cd
supplies their contribution to the entire expansion. Naming this element of K costs O(log h)
bits. For fixed summand choices, the free payload guesses now range exactly over the terminal
wire assignments. The product of call values is their contraction weight. Summing over these
assignments and then over summands gives the input tensor power on its ordered ed exterior
bits.

Size of the fixed network. At each stage Equation (25.5) gives HNb ≤ mShOD (a)+o(1) .
There are O(log h) steps, and Nb ≥ R. The bounds for processors and for their total charge
are therefore

                  P ≤ mShOD (a)+o(1) /R,
                                                                          O(1)
                 V ≤ P hC2 + mShOD (a)+o(1) + (Lj + ed)ho(1) + 2(log h)          .       (26.2)

The first two terms pay for the common work per position and the short records per occurrence.
The third term pays for independent wire bits, and the last for the root computations. Every
                                        O(1)
local record has charge at most 2(log h) , since Nb ≤ S, log S = O((log h)2 ) and an occurrence
has only polynomially many bits in h. The exponent C2 and the exponent bounding the
The matrix multiplication exponent                                                            106


declaration count can be fixed independently of M in R = hM +o(1) . Common work uses
O(h)-bit addresses and rOD (1) candidate contacts. The dependence on M is confined to
factors polynomial in log S and the additive root cost.
    The longest dictionary call uses OD (Tj ) + hOD (a)+o(1) bits. Choose a small enough that
Ca + 2a < β for each coefficient C in the short-work and label bounds, and b + (1 + δ)a < 1
for all allowed b. Equation (25.5) gives Tj = O(hb+(1+δ/3)a ), so the calls have length below

             mShCa+o(1)                       ed                 Lj

Here d = T0 S and T0 = Θ(hb ). The additive root cost is no(1) while m ≥ n. Consequently the
fixed network has size at most md h−a+τ +o(1) ≤ md h−(1−δ)a for large n. Its maximum table
arity, including the activity and length fields, is at most hb+(1+δ)a . A dictionary table retains
its short length-class label, ready for another application of Lemma 23.1. This completes the
proof of that lemma.


27     Trivalent compression and the exponent
Each application of Lemma 23.1 reduces the size per copy and raises the maximum arity.
We stop while the saving in size still exceeds the cost of evaluating the larger tables. Two
operations then recover a trivalent network over the original field. Batched evaluation uses
Lemma 16.2, and a single coordinate projection removes the field extension used in the local
identities.

Lemma 27.1 (Descent of a trivalent network). Let K/k have finite degree D. If a trivalent
K-network of size s represents a tensor with entries in k, the same tensor has a trivalent
k-network of size O(sD2 ) with the same exterior positions.

Proof. Choose a k-basis of K whose first element is 1. For λ ∈ K, let Mλ represent
multiplication by λ in that basis. Order the stations along a chain and give each of them two
extra ports carrying the incoming and outgoing basis indices. If an assignment to its original
ports has weight λ, the enlarged table returns the indicated entry of Mλ . Pin both ends of
the chain to the first index. For any fixed assignment to the original wires, contracting along
the chain now gives the first coordinate of the product of the weights. This coordinate map
is k-linear. Summing over the original internal bits therefore takes the first coordinate of the
old tensor entry, which belongs to k.
    Encode each basis index in ⌈log2 D⌉ bits, giving weight zero to unused codes. The enlarged
table has at most r = 3 + 2⌈log2 D⌉ bits. Every table on r bits has a trivalent realization
of size O(2r ). In the binary prefix tree compute, at each node, the indicator that the input
has that prefix. At depth j, copying the next input bit to the 2j nodes and testing the two
children of each node costs O(2j ) gates. Give each leaf a unary weight equal to its table
entry when its indicator is one and to 1 otherwise. Exactly one leaf supplies the selected
entry. Summing the level costs gives O(2r ) = O(D2 ) per original station.

Proof of Theorem 2.1. Fix the finite field k with ωk < 9/4 and the exterior-bit allowance
η > 0. Write h = ⌈log2 n⌉. The ordinary-width sort in Theorem 16.1 supplies an exponent
The matrix multiplication exponent                                                            107


1 < p < 2. Put ρ = p − 1. A total arity increase from hβ to hb∗ will cost O(hρb∗ ) per surviving
incidence in the final lookup. Choose 0 < δ ≤ 1/10 so that 1 − δ > ρ(1 + δ). Take A > 0
sufficiently small, and then β > 0 sufficiently small, that b∗ = β + (1 + δ)A satisfies
                     b∗ < 21 ,     ρb∗ < 12 η,     ∆ = (1 − δ)A − ρb∗ > 0.                 (27.1)
The positive gap ∆ is the net saving on the original network size. Choose a fixed integer J
large enough that a = A/J satisfies Lemma 23.1 uniformly for β ≤ b ≤ 1/2 and 2a < η/2.
    One extension K/k suffices for these J applications. Each width scale T lies between
a constant times hβ and a fixed power of h. The local identities require a fixed constant
extension and a field of cardinality at least a fixed power of T . Choose [K : k] large enough
for the largest such power and divisible by the degrees of the required constant extensions.
Since k is finite, we can do this with [K : k] = O(log h) = O(log T ) at every scale.
    Let sj count stations and their total arity after j applications, let dj be the cumulative
number of copies, and put ej = dj e. Initially d0 = 1, s0 = O(m), the arity is at most 3 ≤ hβ ,
and the trivalent k-tables have a fixed finite dictionary. At step j < J use the size allowance
                                     Mj = max{sj , ⌈ej h2a ⌉}.
This allows the construction to continue even when the exterior bits already account for
most of the current size. We have Mj ≥ n, ej ≤ Mj h−2a and maximum arity at most
hβ+(1+δ)aj . If this application makes rj copies, Lemma 23.1 preserves the short table labels,
gives dj+1 = rj dj and yields
                                 sj+1 ≤ rj Mj q,    q = h−(1−δ)a .
Its construction in Section 25 has log2 rj = O((log h)2 ) uniformly in the input network.
Inductively, the original bound s0 , e0 = O(n1.05 ), multiplied by a fixed number of these copy
factors and fixed powers of h, gives Mj ≤ n1.05+o(1) ≤ n1.1 at every invocation.
    In terms of size per exterior bit, xj = sj /ej , the replication factor cancels. For large h,
                    xj+1 ≤ q max{xj , 2h2a },       xJ ≤ max{q J x0 , 2qh2a }.
Indeed q < 1, so every contribution arising from an exterior-bit allowance is bounded by the
most recent such allowance.
    Let u be the final maximum arity. Applying Lemma 16.2 makes 2u copies and multiplies
the size per copy by O((1 + u)ρ ). The resulting tensor is the dth power of the original k-tensor,
with
                      d = 2u dJ ,    log2 d ≤ hb∗ + O J(log h)2 = o(h).
                                                                  

Thus d = no(1) uniformly at fixed k, η. Each of its de exterior positions is retained through
lookup and through Lemma 27.1. Descent costs O([K : k]2 ) = ho(1) . Combining these
bounds, the trivalent size over k divided by de is at most
                                 O(m/e) h−∆+o(1) + h2a+ρb∗ +o(1) .
Choose γ = ∆/2. The strict inequalities ∆ > γ and 2a + ρb∗ < η absorb the constants and
lower-order terms for large n. Multiplication by de gives Equation (2.1).
    For the distinctness tensor of Theorem 2.2, the independent exterior positions are the
entries of separate lists. Choosing η < σ, Proposition 2.3 iterates the compression until the
volume per exterior bit is O(wη ), below the required wσ . This rules out ωk < 9/4 over every
finite field. Lemma 2.5 transfers the lower bound to any field, where the matching upper
bound gives Theorem 1.1.
